Let Wi⊂ℝ2​nW_{i}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}, i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k, be compact domains with piecewise-smooth boundary whose interiors contain the origin.
Assume that H2​(Wi,ℝ)=0H^{2}(W_{i};{\mathbb{R}})=0 for all ii. Set 𝕎:=⨆i=1kWi{\mathbb{W}}:=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}W_{i}.
Then, for any ε>0\varepsilon>0,
νT,ε​(M,ω1,𝕎)=νT,ε​(M,ω2,𝕎).\nu_{T,\varepsilon}(M,\omega_{1},{\mathbb{W}})=\nu_{T,\varepsilon}(M,\omega_{2},{\mathbb{W}}).
For the proof of 3.4 see Section 11 (11).
Remark 3.28
3.4 generalizes a similar result in [EV1] about symplectic (not necessarily
tame) embeddings and its proof is similar to the proof in [EV1].
Let us present applications of 3.4.
Corollary 3.29
Assume:
•
MM is either 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n} or a smooth manifold underlying a K3 surface.
•
𝕎:=⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri){\mathbb{W}}:=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i}) is a disjoint union of kk (possibly different) balls.
•
ε>0\varepsilon>0.
Then the following claims hold:
A.
For any irrational Kähler-type symplectic form ω\omega on MM we have
νK​(M,ω,𝕎)=1,\nu_{K}(M,\omega,{\mathbb{W}})=1,
meaning that Kähler-type embeddings of λ​𝕎\lambda{\mathbb{W}} into (M,ω)(M,\omega) are
unobstructed.
B. There exists a Diff+​(M)\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(M)-invariant open dense set of Kähler-type symplectic forms on MM, depending on 𝕎{\mathbb{W}} and ε\varepsilon and containing, in particular, all irrational Kähler-type symplectic forms on MM,
so that for each ω\omega in this set
νT,ε​(M,ω,𝕎)=1\nu_{T,\varepsilon}(M,\omega,{\mathbb{W}})=1 – meaning that ε\varepsilon-tame embeddings of λ​𝕎\lambda{\mathbb{W}} into (M,ω)(M,\omega) are
unobstructed.
Corollary 3.30
Assume:
•
M=𝕋2​nM={\mathbb{T}}^{2n}.
•
𝕎:=⨆i=1kWi{\mathbb{W}}:=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}W_{i} is either a disjoint union of kk identical copies of a 2​n2n-dimensional polydisk
B2​n1​(R1)×…×B2​nl​(Rl),n1+…+nl=n,R1,…,Rl>0,l>1,B^{2n_{1}}(R_{1})\times\ldots\times B^{2n_{l}}(R_{l}),\ n_{1}+\ldots+n_{l}=n,\ R_{1},\ldots,R_{l}>0,l>1,
or a disjoint union of kk identical copies of a parallelepiped
P(e1,…,e2​n):={∑j=12​nsjej,0≤sj≤1,j=1,…,2n},P(e_{1},\ldots,e_{2n}):=\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{2n}s_{j}e_{j},0\leq s_{j}\leq 1,j=1,\ldots,2n\right\},
where e1,…,e2​ne_{1},\ldots,e_{2n} is a basis of the vector space ℝ2​n{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}.
•
ε>0\varepsilon>0.
Then the following claims hold:
A.
For any positive volume there exists a dense Diff+​(𝕋2​n)\mathrm{Diff}^{+}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n})-orbit (of an irrational Kähler-type symplectic form depending on 𝕎{\mathbb{W}}) in the space of Kähler-type symplectic forms of that volume on 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n}
such that for any ω′\omega^{\prime} in this orbit we have
νK​(𝕋2​n,ω′,𝕎)=1\nu_{K}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},\omega^{\prime},{\mathbb{W}})=1 –
or, in other words, Kähler-type embeddings λ​𝕎→(𝕋2​n,ω′)\lambda{\mathbb{W}}\to({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},\omega^{\prime}) are unobstructed.
B. There exists a Diff+​(𝕋2​n)\mathrm{Diff}^{+}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n})-invariant open dense set of Kähler-type symplectic forms on 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n}, depending on 𝕎{\mathbb{W}} and ε\varepsilon and containing, in particular, all irrational Kähler-type symplectic forms on 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n},
so that for each ω′\omega^{\prime} in this set
νT,ε​(𝕋2​n,ω′,𝕎)=1\nu_{T,\varepsilon}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},\omega^{\prime},{\mathbb{W}})=1 –
or, in other words, ε\varepsilon-tame embeddings λ​𝕎→(𝕋2​n,ω′)\lambda{\mathbb{W}}\to({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},\omega^{\prime}) are unobstructed.
Corollary 3.31
Assume:
•
MM is a smooth manifold underlying a K3 surface.
•
𝕎:=⨆i=1kWi{\mathbb{W}}:=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}W_{i} is a disjoint union of kk identical copies of a parallelepiped
P(e1,…,e2​n):={∑j=12​nsjej,0≤sj≤1,j=1,…,2n},P(e_{1},\ldots,e_{2n}):=\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{2n}s_{j}e_{j},0\leq s_{j}\leq 1,j=1,\ldots,2n\right\},
where e1,…,e2​ne_{1},\ldots,e_{2n} is a basis of the vector space ℝ2​n{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}.
•
ε>0\varepsilon>0.
Then there exists a Diff+​(M)\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(M)-invariant open dense set of Kähler-type symplectic forms on MM, depending on 𝕎{\mathbb{W}} and ε\varepsilon and containing, in particular, all irrational Kähler-type symplectic forms on MM,
so that for each ω′\omega^{\prime} in this set
νT,ε​(M,ω′,𝕎)=1\nu_{T,\varepsilon}(M,\omega^{\prime},{\mathbb{W}})=1, meaning that ε\varepsilon-tame embeddings of λ​𝕎\lambda{\mathbb{W}} into (M,ω′)(M,\omega^{\prime}) are unobstructed.
For the proof of 3.4, 3.4 and 3.4 see Section 11. These corollaries generalize similar results for symplectic – but not necessarily tame – embeddings that were proved in [EV1].
Remark 3.32
We know only one way to show the possible existence of embeddings (of a disjoint union of domains) into (M,ω)(M,\omega) that are symplectic and non-tame, or tame and not Kähler-type – via the volume restrictions: e.g., if Kähler-type embeddings cannot fill more than a certain portion of the volume of (M,ω)(M,\omega) while tame embeddings can. This is how we got in
3.4 that there exist symplectic embeddings (of balls) that are not of Kähler-type.
It would be interesting to find out whether there are other ways of detecting symplectic and non-tame, or tame and not Kähler-type, embeddings.
Together with 3.4, this leads to the following questions.
Question 3.33
Does there exist a closed manifold equipped with a Kähler-type symplectic form that admits a
symplectic embedding which is not tame, or a tame embedding which is not of Kähler-type? In particular, does there exist a rational Kähler-type symplectic form ω\omega on M=𝕋2​nM={\mathbb{T}}^{2n}, n>1n>1, or on a smooth manifold MM underlying a complex K3 surface, and a disjoint union of balls 𝕎{\mathbb{W}}, Vol⁡(𝕎,ω0)<Vol⁡(M,ω){\rm{Vol}\,}({\mathbb{W}},\omega_{0})<{\rm{Vol}\,}(M,\omega), so that 𝕎{\mathbb{W}} does not admit a tame embedding into (M,ω)(M,\omega)?
3.5 The IHS-hyperkähler case
K3 surfaces are particular examples of so
called IHS-hyperkähler manifolds,
that is, compact hyperkähler manifolds
of maximal holonomy. Most of the results
of Section 3.4
generalize to such manifolds. In this section we present
relevant definitions and statements.
Recall that a hyperkähler manifold is a manifold
equipped with three complex structures I1,I2,I3I_{1},I_{2},I_{3} satisfying the
quaternionic relations and three symplectic forms
ω1,ω2,ω3\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3} compatible, respectively, with
I1,I2,I3I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}, so that the three Riemannian metrics ωi(⋅,Ii⋅)\omega_{i}(\cdot,I_{i}\cdot), i=1,2,3i=1,2,3, coincide. Such a collection ℌ={I1,I2,I3,ω1,ω2,ω3}{\mathfrak{H}}=\{I_{1},I_{2},I_{3},\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3}\} of
complex structures and symplectic forms on a manifold is called a
hyperkähler structure. All the complex and
symplectic structures appearing in a hyperkähler
structure induce the same orientation on the manifold.
Assume that MM is a closed connected and simply connected manifold.
The Levi-Civita connection of a Kähler manifold
preserves its complex structure and the Kähler form.
Therefore, the Levi-Civita connection of a hyperkähler
manifold preserves the three complex structures I1,I2,I3I_{1},I_{2},I_{3} and the three
symplectic forms ω1,ω2,ω3\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3}.
The subgroup of G​L​(ℝ4​n)GL({\mathbb{R}}^{4n}) preserving both the quaternionic
Hermitian metric and the quaternionic action is
called the compact symplectic group and is denoted
by S​p​(n)Sp(n). This is the group of all quaternionic
unitary matrices; it can also be obtained as
a maximal compact form of a complex symplectic
group Sp⁡(ℂ2​n)\operatorname{Sp}({\mathbb{C}}^{2n}). Since the Levi-Civita
connection of (M,g,I1,I2,I3)(M,g,I_{1},I_{2},I_{3})
preserves the metric and the quaternionic operators,
its holonomy belongs to S​p​(n)Sp(n). However, a priori the
holonomy group can be smaller; for example,
for a hyperkähler flat torus, the holonomy is trivial.
The de Rham theorem and Berger’s classification of
irreducible holonomies imply that any
compact hyperkähler manifold has a finite
covering which is decomposed into a product
of a torus and manifolds with maximal holonomy;
this result is called the Bogomolov
decomposition theorem, [Bo1].
This theorem is the reason why the
maximal holonomy hyperkähler manifolds are also
called irreducible
holomorphically symplectic (IHS).
A maximal holonomy compact hyperkähler manifold
can be characterized cohomologically: MM
has maximal holonomy if and only h2,0​(M)=1h^{2,0}(M)=1
and b1​(M)=0b_{1}(M)=0. Here h2,0​(M)h^{2,0}(M) denotes the
dimension of the group of closed, holomorphic 2-forms on MM,
for any of its complex structures.
K3 surfaces, as well as the Hilbert schemes of
points for K3 surfaces, are known to admit IHS-hyperkähler
structures.
We say that a symplectic/complex structure on MM is
of IHS-hyperkähler type, if it appears
in some maximal holonomy hyperkähler structure. In
particular, all IHS-hyperkähler-type symplectic and
complex structures are of Kähler type.
Remark 3.34
1. Note that even if a symplectic and a complex structure are of IHS-hyperkähler type and compatible, it still does not mean that they can be included in the same IHS-hyperkähler structure.
2. All complex structures and all Kähler-type symplectic structures on a K3 surface are in fact of IHS-hyperkähler type – see e.g. [EV3, Prop. 3.1].
As we have already mentioned, it is an open question whether any symplectic form on a K3 surface is of Kähler type.
3. Manifolds admitting IHS-hyperkähler structures are also
called hyperkähler manifolds of maximal holonomy, because
the holonomy group of a hyperkähler manifold is Sp⁡(n)\operatorname{Sp}(n) (the
group of invertible quaternionic n×nn\times n-matrices) – and not
its proper subgroup – if and only if the hyperkähler structure is IHS
[Bes].
4.
Note that the property of being IHS is a topological
invariant: a holomorphically symplectic Kähler manifold
MM, dimℂM=2​n\dim_{\mathbb{C}}M=2n, is IHS if and only if π1​(M)=0\pi_{1}(M)=0 and
there exists a primitive integral quadratic form qq on H2​(M,ℝ)H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}})
(called the Bogomolov-Beauville-Fujiki form [Bea1], [Fu])
that satisfies, for some rational C>0C>0,
the
Fujiki relation ∫Mη2​n=C​q​(η,η)n\int_{M}\eta^{2n}=Cq(\eta,\eta)^{n}.
Therefore, the set of
IHS-hyperkähler type symplectic/complex structures on
a given IHS-hyperkähler manifold coincides with the set of
hyperkähler-type symplectic/complex structures – i.e., the symplectic/complex structures appearing in some
hyperkähler structure.
Now we can go over Section 2 and define IHS-hyperkähler-type
embeddings by replacing the words “Kähler-type” in the relevant definitions by the words “IHS-hyperkähler-type”. In particular, in this way we can define IHS-hyperkähler-type symplectic embeddings of domains in ℝ4​n{\mathbb{R}}^{4n} into MM equipped with an IHS-hyperkähler-type symplectic form.
Note that, in view of part 2 of 3.5, for symplectic embeddings into a K3 surface being of IHS-hyperkähler type is the same as being of Kähler type.
The results of Section 3.4 on Kähler-type
embeddings generalize to the IHS-hyperkähler case as follows:
- Part A of 3.4
generalizes directly to the IHS-hyperkähler case as long as one assumes that the Campana-simple complex structure II is of IHS-hyperkähler-type.
- The claim in part C of
3.4
concerning the action of Symp⁡(M,ω)∩Diff0​(M)\mathrm{Symp}(M,\omega)\cap\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M)
generalizes to the IHS-hyperkähler case, if 𝐶𝐾⁡(M)\mathit{CK}(M) is replaced by the space of the IHS-hyperkähler-type complex structures on MM.
For a general IHS-hyperkähler manifold (M,ω)(M,\omega) we cannot say anything about the transitivity of the
SympH​(M,ω)\mathrm{Symp}_{H}(M,\omega)-action.
Let us discuss the results on
ε\varepsilon-tame
embeddings into (M,ω)(M,\omega) for an IHS-hyperkähler-type form ω\omega.
One can define
tame and ε\varepsilon-tame
embeddings into (M,ω)(M,\omega) exactly as in 2.3
using Kähler-type complex structures. Another possibility is to modify the definition of
tame and ε\varepsilon-tame
embeddings into (M,ω)(M,\omega)
by using IHS-hyperkähler-type complex structures in 2.3 instead of Kähler-type ones. The claim νT,ε​(M,ω1,𝕎)=νT,ε​(M,ω2,𝕎)\nu_{T,\varepsilon}(M,\omega_{1},{\mathbb{W}})=\nu_{T,\varepsilon}(M,\omega_{2},{\mathbb{W}}) of 3.4 holds for the original νT,ε\nu_{T,\varepsilon} defined in 2.3, as well as for a version of νT,ε\nu_{T,\varepsilon} defined using the
ε\varepsilon-tame
embeddings in the second sense above,
if ω1\omega_{1} and ω2\omega_{2} lie in the same connected component of the space of IHS-hyperkähler-type symplectic forms on MM.
In Section 12 we discuss how to modify
the proofs of the results of Section 3.4 in order to obtain the above-mentioned results in the IHS-hyperkähler case.
4 Plan of the paper and an outline of the proofs
In this section we describe the plan of the paper and outline the proofs.
In order to simplify the exposition, let us fix a connected component 𝒞0{\mathcal{C}}_{0} of 𝐶𝐾⁡(M)\mathit{CK}(M) and the radii r1,…,rk>0r_{1},\ldots,r_{k}>0
of the balls from the beginning. Let r¯:=(r1,…,rk){\bar{r}}:=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{k}).
In Section 5 we prove 3.1
and
9.
Namely, we define the following spaces:
- The space 𝑃𝑎𝑖𝑟𝑠𝒞0{\it Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} formed by pairs (η,f)(\eta,f), where η\eta is a Kähler-type symplectic form on MM
isotopic to ω\omega and f:⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→(M,η)f:\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to(M,\eta) is a Kähler-type embedding favoring 𝒞0{\mathcal{C}}_{0}.
- The Teichmüller space 𝙿𝚊𝚒𝚛𝚜𝒞0:=𝑃𝑎𝑖𝑟𝑠𝒞0/Diff0​(M){\tt Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}:={\it Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}/\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M).
- The space 𝑇𝑟𝑖𝑝𝑙𝑒𝑠𝒞0{\it Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} formed by triples (I,𝕩,h)(I,{\mathbbm{x}},h), where I∈Diff0​(M)⋅𝐶𝑚𝑝𝑡⁡(M,ω)I\in\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M)\cdot{\it Cmpt}(M,\omega), 𝕩=(x1,…,xk)∈M^k{\mathbbm{x}}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\in{\hat{M}}^{k}, so that the pair (I,𝕩)(I,{\mathbbm{x}}) lies in the set 𝒦𝒞0​(r¯){\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}({\bar{r}}) defined in 3.1, and
h:=⨆i=1khi:⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→(M,I)h:=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}h_{i}:\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to(M,I)
is a holomorphic embedding such that hi​(0)=xih_{i}(0)=x_{i}, i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k.
- The Teichmüller space 𝚃𝚛𝚒𝚙𝚕𝚎𝚜𝒞0:=𝑇𝑟𝑖𝑝𝑙𝑒𝑠𝒞0/Diff0​(M){\tt Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}:={\it Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}/\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M).
We then construct a continuous surjective map Φ:𝚃𝚛𝚒𝚙𝚕𝚎𝚜𝒞0→𝙿𝚊𝚒𝚛𝚜𝒞0\Phi:{\tt Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}\to{\tt Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}. The construction uses
the symplectic blow-up and blow-down operations similar to the ones used in [McDP]. Let us note that
for the local constructions on which these operations are based we use
the regularized maximum of plurisubharmonic functions while in [McDP] a different analytic technique is used.
We also need the regularized maximum tool to construct a Kähler form on (M~I,𝕩,I~)({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}},{\widetilde{I}}) in the class
Π∗​[ω]−π​∑i=1kri2​ei∈H2​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ)\Pi^{*}[\omega]-\pi\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}^{2}e_{i}\in H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}}) which is standard on a neighborhood of each
exceptional divisor – this is needed to obtain a Kähler form on MM by the symplectic blow-down construction (unlike in
[McDP], where the goal was to produce a symplectic, not necessarily Kähler, form on MM, and this was done much
easier, using Moser’s method for forms on M~I,𝕩{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}}).
The symplectic blow-down construction, enhanced as we have just described, and a relative version of Moser’s method are used to construct Φ\Phi, and the symplectic blow-up is used to verify that
Φ\Phi is surjective. The continuity of Φ\Phi
is verified using the observation that the set 𝙿𝚊𝚒𝚛𝚜𝒞0{\tt Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} is discrete and therefore it suffices
to check that the preimage of a point is open, which is then proved using again a relative version of Moser’s method.
We then prove 3.1 as follows.
The mere existence of Φ\Phi implies that if 𝒦𝒞0​(r¯){\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}({\bar{r}}) (and hence 𝚃𝚛𝚒𝚙𝚕𝚎𝚜𝒞0{\tt Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}) is non-empty, there exists a Kähler-type embedding ⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→(M,ω)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to(M,\omega) favoring 𝒞0{\mathcal{C}}_{0}.
The connectivity of the set 𝙺𝒞0​(r¯){\tt{K}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}({\bar{r}}) (see 3.1) yields the transitivity of the
Symp​(M)∩Diff0​(M)\mathrm{Symp}(M)\cap\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M)-action on the space of Kähler-type embeddings ⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→(M,ω)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to(M,\omega) favoring 𝒞0{\mathcal{C}}_{0} as follows. Using methods of complex geometry (the Kodaira-Spencer stability and the Demailly-Paun description of the Kähler cones of fibers of an analytic deformation family)
we show that the connectivity of 𝙺𝒞0​(r¯){\tt{K}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}({\bar{r}}) is equivalent to the connectivity of 𝒦𝒞0​(r¯){\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}({\bar{r}}). The latter
easily implies the connectivity of 𝚃𝚛𝚒𝚙𝚕𝚎𝚜𝒞0{\tt Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}. Since Φ\Phi is continuous and surjective, we get that 𝙿𝚊𝚒𝚛𝚜𝒞0{\tt Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} is
connected, and since it is discrete, it is just a point. This easily implies the transitivity of the Symp​(M)∩Diff0​(M)\mathrm{Symp}(M)\cap\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M)-action.
In Section 9 we consider the case of a closed symplectic manifold for which
the symplectic form is compatible with a so-called Campana-simple complex structure – i.e., a Kähler-type complex structure admitting “few” complex subvarieties. Examples of such manifolds include tori and smooth manifolds underlying complex K3 surfaces equipped with irrational Kähler-type symplectic forms. The general results on Kähler-type embeddings of balls into such manifolds, proved in Section 9, are used further in the paper to deduce the results on such embeddings into tori
and K3 surfaces stated in Section 3.4.
The key existence result on such embeddings is based on the observation, made already in [LatMS], [EV1],
that for any Campana-simple complex structure II compatible
with ω\omega and any Campana-generic x1,…,xk∈(M,I)x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in(M,I), 𝕩:=(x1,…,xk){\mathbbm{x}}:=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}), we have (I,𝕩)∈𝒦⁡(r¯)(I,{\mathbbm{x}})\in{\mathcal{K}}({\bar{r}}), as long as
Vol⁡(⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri))<Vol⁡(M,ω){\rm{Vol}\,}(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i}))<{\rm{Vol}\,}(M,\omega). This follows from the Demailly-Paun description of the Kähler cone
of a closed Kähler manifold [DP].
In Sections 6, 7, 8,
10 we
discuss the cases
of the specific MM: complex projective spaces and their products, Hirzebruch surfaces, tori and K3 surfaces. In each of these cases we discuss the structure of the Teichmüller
space of Kähler-type complex structures on MM and of its subset formed by equivalence classes of complex structures
compatible with a given symplectic form on MM, as well as the Kähler cones of the complex blow-ups of MM.
We use this information in order to apply our general results about Kähler-type embeddings of balls
and deduce the results in
Section 3
about these specific MM.
In Section 11 we discuss the proofs of the results in Section 3.4.
concerning tame embeddings of arbitrary domains.
The strategy of the proof of
3.4
is the same as for a similar claim in [EV1].
Namely, with MM being either 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n} or a smooth manifold underlying a K3 surface,
the group Diff+​(M)\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(M) of orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms of MM acts on the space
𝑆𝐾1​(M)\mathit{SK}_{1}(M)
of Kähler-type forms on MM of
total volume 11. The function
ω↦νT,ε​(M,ω,𝕎)\omega\mapsto\nu_{T,\varepsilon}(M,\omega,{\mathbb{W}}) is clearly invariant under the action. We will show
that this function is lower semicontinuous (with respect to the
C1C^{1}-topology on 𝑆𝐾⁡(M)\mathit{SK}(M)) – the proof is a modification of the proof of a similar claim in [EV1].
Then we use the result from [EV1] saying that
the orbit of a form ω∈𝑆𝐾1​(M)\omega\in\mathit{SK}_{1}(M) under the
action of Diff+​(M)\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(M) is dense in 𝑆𝐾1​(M)\mathit{SK}_{1}(M), as long as ω\omega is irrational. Since the orbits
of both ω1\omega_{1} and ω2\omega_{2} are dense in 𝑆𝐾1​(M)\mathit{SK}_{1}(M), we get, by the
lower semicontinuity, that
νT,ε​(M,ω1,𝕎)≤νT,ε​(M,ω2,𝕎)\nu_{T,\varepsilon}(M,\omega_{1},{\mathbb{W}})\leq\nu_{T,\varepsilon}(M,\omega_{2},{\mathbb{W}}) and
νT,ε​(M,ω1,𝕎)≥νT,ε​(M,ω2,𝕎)\nu_{T,\varepsilon}(M,\omega_{1},{\mathbb{W}})\geq\nu_{T,\varepsilon}(M,\omega_{2},{\mathbb{W}}),
which means that
νT,ε​(M,ω1,𝕎)=νT,ε​(M,ω2,𝕎)\nu_{T,\varepsilon}(M,\omega_{1},{\mathbb{W}})=\nu_{T,\varepsilon}(M,\omega_{2},{\mathbb{W}}).
Then the proofs of 3.4, 3.4 and 3.4 follow the same lines as the proof of [EV1, Cor. 3.3]
where the existence of symplectic embeddings of polydisks into tori was proved.
In Section 12 we discuss how the proofs in the K3 case can be modified (or just carried over without any changes) in order to prove the results in the hyperkähler case stated in Section 3.5.
Finally, in Section 13 (the Appendix) we discuss various well-known facts concerning the dependence of the Hodge decomposition on the
complex structure, the deformations of complex structures, Moser’s method, and Alexander’s trick that we use in the paper and
for which we could not find a direct reference in the literature.
5 Kähler-type embeddings of balls and blow-ups
As before, let MM, dimℝM=2​n\dim_{\mathbb{R}}M=2n, be a closed connected manifold admitting Kähler structures. Let ω\omega be a Kähler-type symplectic form on MM.
Let k∈ℤ>0k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}, r1,…,rk>0r_{1},\ldots,r_{k}>0, r¯=(r1,…,rk){\bar{r}}=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{k}).
Let 𝒞0{\mathcal{C}}_{0} be a connected component of 𝐶𝐾⁡(M)\mathit{CK}(M).
From now on,
all the spaces of maps between manifolds and of differential forms and complex structures on manifolds will be endowed with the C∞C^{\infty}-topologies. All the product/quotient spaces will be endowed with the product/quotient topologies.
5.1 The Symp⁡(M,ω)∩Diff0​(M)\mathrm{Symp}(M,\omega)\cap\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M)-action on the space of
Kähler-type embeddings
Denote:
EmbM,ω​(⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri))\textit{Emb}_{M,\omega}\left(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\right) – the space of the symplectic embeddings
⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→(M,ω)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to(M,\omega);
EKM,ω​(⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri))⊂EmbM,ω​(⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri))\textit{EK}_{M,\omega}\left(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\right)\subset\textit{Emb}_{M,\omega}\left(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\right) –
the space of the Kähler-type embeddings
⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→(M,ω)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to(M,\omega);
EKM,ω,𝒞0​(⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri))\textit{EK}_{M,\omega,{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}\left(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\right) –
the space of the Kähler-type
embeddings
⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→(M,ω)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to(M,\omega)
favoring 𝒞0{\mathcal{C}}_{0}.
Given a symplectic embedding f:⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→(M,ω)f:\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to(M,\omega), any symplectic embedding f′:⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→(M,ω)f^{\prime}:\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to(M,\omega) sufficiently C∞C^{\infty}-close to ff can be mapped in ff by an element of Symp0​(M,ω)\mathrm{Symp}_{0}(M,\omega), and moreover, this element can be chosen arbitrarily close to the identity, provided f′f^{\prime} is sufficiently C∞C^{\infty}-close to ff. This fact is standard and follows from the well-known Alexander trick and the symplectic isotopy extension results – see 13.4 for details.
Since symplectomorphisms of (M,ω)(M,\omega) map Kähler-type embeddings into Kähler-type embeddings, this yields that
EKM,ω​(⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri))\textit{EK}_{M,\omega}\left(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\right)
is an open Symp⁡(M,ω)\mathrm{Symp}(M,\omega)-invariant subset of EmbM,ω​(⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri))\textit{Emb}_{M,\omega}\left(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\right)
(in the C∞C^{\infty}-topology). This yields the following corollary.
Proposition 5.1
The space
EKM,ω​(⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri))Symp0​(M,ω),\frac{\textit{EK}_{M,\omega}\left(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\right)}{\mathrm{Symp}_{0}(M,\omega)},
and consequently the space
EKM,ω​(⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri))Symp⁡(M,ω)∩Diff0​(M),\frac{\textit{EK}_{M,\omega}\left(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\right)}{\mathrm{Symp}(M,\omega)\cap\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M)},
and its subspace
EKM,ω,𝒞0​(⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri))Symp⁡(M,ω)∩Diff0​(M),\frac{\textit{EK}_{M,\omega,{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}\left(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\right)}{\mathrm{Symp}(M,\omega)\cap\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M)},
are discrete.
Definition 5.2
Define 𝑃𝑎𝑖𝑟𝑠𝒞0{\it Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} as the set of pairs (η,f)(\eta,f), where η∈Diff0​(M)⋅ω\eta\in\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M)\cdot\omega and
f:⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→(M,η)f:\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to(M,\eta) is Kähler-type
embedding into (M,η)(M,\eta) favoring 𝒞0{\mathcal{C}}_{0}. (Note that the embedding here is into (M,η)(M,\eta), not (M,ω)(M,\omega)!)
Define
𝙿𝚊𝚒𝚛𝚜𝒞0:=𝑃𝑎𝑖𝑟𝑠𝒞0/Diff0​(M).{\tt Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}:={\it Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}/\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M).
For each (η,f)∈𝑃𝑎𝑖𝑟𝑠𝒞0(\eta,f)\in{\it Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}, denote by {η,f}∈𝙿𝚊𝚒𝚛𝚜𝒞0\{\eta,f\}\in{\tt Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} the element of
𝙿𝚊𝚒𝚛𝚜𝒞0{\tt Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} represented by (η,f)(\eta,f) – i.e., the Diff0​(M)\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M)-orbit of (η,f)(\eta,f).
The following proposition follows easily from the definitions.
Proposition 5.3
The natural map
EKM,ω,𝒞0​(⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri))Symp⁡(M,ω)∩Diff0​(M)→𝙿𝚊𝚒𝚛𝚜𝒞0\frac{\textit{EK}_{M,\omega,{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}\left(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\right)}{\mathrm{Symp}(M,\omega)\cap\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M)}\to{\tt Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}
is a homeomorphism. In particular, in both cases 𝙿𝚊𝚒𝚛𝚜𝒞0{\tt Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} is discrete.
A discrete topological space is connected if and only if it is a point. Together with 5.1, this immediately yields the following corollary.
Corollary 5.4
The Symp⁡(M,ω)∩Diff0​(M)\mathrm{Symp}(M,\omega)\cap\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M)-action on EKM,ω,𝒞0​(⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri))\textit{EK}_{M,\omega,{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}\left(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\right)
is transitive if and only if
𝙿𝚊𝚒𝚛𝚜𝒞0{\tt Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} is connected.
Proposition 5.5
Assume (ηt,ft)(\eta_{t},f_{t}), 0≤t≤10\leq t\leq 1, is a smooth family in 𝑃𝑎𝑖𝑟𝑠𝒞0{\it Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}.
Then all (ηt,ft)(\eta_{t},f_{t}), 0≤t≤10\leq t\leq 1, lie in the same orbit of the Diff0​(M)\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M)-action on 𝑃𝑎𝑖𝑟𝑠𝒞0{\it Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}.
Proof of 5.1:
The forms ηt\eta_{t}, 0≤t≤10\leq t\leq 1, form a smooth family of cohomologous symplectic forms. Hence, by Moser’s theorem [Mos], all these forms can be identified by the Diff0​(M)\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M)-action with η0\eta_{0}. Under such an identification,
{ft}0≤t≤1\{f_{t}\}_{0\leq t\leq 1} becomes
a smooth family of symplectic embeddings
{ft:⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→(M,η0)}0≤t≤1\{f_{t}:\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to(M,\eta_{0})\}_{0\leq t\leq 1}. By the symplectic isotopy extension result (see e.g. [McDS, Thm. 3.3.1]), all such ftf_{t} lie in the same Symp0​(M,η0)\mathrm{Symp}_{0}(M,\eta_{0})-orbit. This proves the proposition.
5.2 From holomorphic to Kähler-type embeddings of balls
As before, for each 𝕩:=(x1,…,xk)∈M^k{\mathbbm{x}}:=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\in{\hat{M}}^{k}
and each complex structure II on MM, let M~I,𝕩{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}}
denote the complex blow-up of (M,I)(M,I) at x1,…,xkx_{1},\ldots,x_{k}.
For any II and 𝕩{\mathbbm{x}} we will use the same notation for the following objects (suppressing the dependence on II and 𝕩{\mathbbm{x}}):
- The lift of II to M~I,𝕩{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}} will be denoted by I~{\widetilde{I}}. This complex structure is
of Kähler-type (see e.g. [Voi, Vol I, Prop. 3.24]). If I′I^{\prime} is another complex structure on MM coinciding with
II near each xix_{i}, i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k, then it also lifts to M~I,𝕩{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}} and its lift will be denoted by I~′{\widetilde{I}}^{\prime}.
- Π:M~I,𝕩→M\Pi:{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}}\to M will denote the natural projection.
- Ei:=Π−1​(xi)E_{i}:=\Pi^{-1}(x_{i}), i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k, is an exceptional divisor.
- The cohomology classes Poincaré-dual to the fundamental homology classes of EiE_{i}, i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k,
will be denoted by e1,…,ek∈H2​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ)e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}\in H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}}).
For all II and 𝕩{\mathbbm{x}} the groups H2​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ)H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}}) can be canonically identified (preserving the numbering of the classes e1,…,ene_{1},\ldots,e_{n}) and we will use these identifications without further mention.
Definition 5.6
Define 𝑇𝑟𝑖𝑝𝑙𝑒𝑠𝒞0{\it Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} as the set of triples (I,𝕩,h)(I,{\mathbbm{x}},h), where
•
(I,𝕩)∈𝒦𝒞0​(r¯)(I,{\mathbbm{x}})\in{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}({\bar{r}}), meaning that
I∈Diff0​(M)⋅(𝐶𝑚𝑝𝑡⁡(M,ω)∩𝒞0),I\in\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M)\cdot\left({\it Cmpt}(M,\omega)\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{0}\right),
𝕩:=(x1,…,xk)∈M^k,{\mathbbm{x}}:=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\in{\hat{M}}^{k},
and
the cohomology class Π∗​[ω]−π​∑i=1kri2​ei∈H2​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ)\Pi^{*}[\omega]-\pi\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}^{2}e_{i}\in H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}}) is Kähler with respect to I~{\widetilde{I}}.
•
h=⨆i=1khi:⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→(M,I)h=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}h_{i}:\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to(M,I)
is a holomorphic embedding into (M,I)(M,I) so that hi​(0)=xih_{i}(0)=x_{i}, i=1,…,xki=1,\ldots,x_{k}.
Set 𝚃𝚛𝚒𝚙𝚕𝚎𝚜𝒞0:=𝑇𝑟𝑖𝑝𝑙𝑒𝑠𝒞0/Diff0​(M){\tt Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}:={\it Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}/\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M).
For each (I,𝕩,h)∈𝑇𝑟𝑖𝑝𝑙𝑒𝑠𝒞0(I,{\mathbbm{x}},h)\in{\it Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}, denote by {I,𝕩,h}∈𝚃𝚛𝚒𝚙𝚕𝚎𝚜𝒞0\{I,{\mathbbm{x}},h\}\in{\tt Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} the element of
𝚃𝚛𝚒𝚙𝚕𝚎𝚜𝒞0{\tt Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} represented by (I,𝕩,h)(I,{\mathbbm{x}},h) – i.e., the Diff0​(M)\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M)-orbit of (I,𝕩,h)(I,{\mathbbm{x}},h).
For each κ>0\kappa>0 define
Rκ:ℝ2​n→ℝ2​n,ℛκ​(x):=κ​x.R_{\kappa}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2n},\ {\mathcal{R}}_{\kappa}(x):=\kappa x.
The proof of 3.1 is based on the following key propositions.
Proposition 5.7
There exists a continuous surjective map Φ:𝚃𝚛𝚒𝚙𝚕𝚎𝚜𝒞0→𝙿𝚊𝚒𝚛𝚜𝒞0\Phi:{\tt Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}\to{\tt Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}
with the following properties:
1. For each (I,𝕩,h)∈𝑇𝑟𝑖𝑝𝑙𝑒𝑠𝒞0(I,{\mathbbm{x}},h)\in{\it Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}},
Φ⁡({I,𝕩,h})={η,h∘Rκ}\Phi\left(\left\{I,{\mathbbm{x}},h\right\}\right)=\left\{\eta,h\circ R_{\kappa}\right\}
for a symplectic form η\eta compatible with II and cohomologous to ω\omega and for a sufficiently small 0<κ≤10<\kappa\leq 1, so that
the embedding h∘Rκ:⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→Mh\circ R_{\kappa}:\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to M
is symplectic with respect to η\eta and holomorphic with respect to II.
2. If η\eta is a symplectic form on MM compatible with a complex structure II and cohomologous to ω\omega and
f=⨆i=1kfi:⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→(M,η)f=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}:\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to(M,\eta) is a symplectic
embedding into (M,η)(M,\eta) holomorphic with respect to II,
then, for xi:=fi​(0)x_{i}:=f_{i}(0), i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k, and 𝕩:=(x1,…,xk){\mathbbm{x}}:=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}), we have
(I,𝕩,f)∈𝑇𝑟𝑖𝑝𝑙𝑒𝑠𝒞0(I,{\mathbbm{x}},f)\in{\it Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} and Φ⁡({I,𝕩,f})={η,f}\Phi\left(\left\{I,{\mathbbm{x}},f\right\}\right)=\{\eta,f\}.
Proposition 5.8
The space 𝚃𝚛𝚒𝚙𝚕𝚎𝚜𝒞0{\tt Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} is connected
if only if the set 𝙺𝒞0​(r¯){\tt{K}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}({\bar{r}}) (see 3.1) is connected.
For the proof of 5.2 see Section 5.5. The proof of 5.2 will be given in Section 5.6.
Let us now prove
3.1. For convenience, we restate it here.
Theorem 5.9 (=3.1)
Let MM be a closed manifold equipped with a Kähler-type symplectic form ω\omega.
(I)
The set ⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i}) admits a Kähler-type embedding into (M,ω)(M,\omega) if and only if
for some I∈𝐶𝑚𝑝𝑡⁡(M,ω)I\in{\it Cmpt}(M,\omega) and some 𝕩∈M^k{\mathbbm{x}}\in{\hat{M}}^{k} the cohomology class Π∗​[ω]−π​∑i=1kri2​ei∈H2​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ)\Pi^{*}[\omega]-\pi\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}^{2}e_{i}\in H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}}), is Kähler with respect to I~{\widetilde{I}}.
More precisely, assume II is a complex structure on MM compatible with ω\omega and Σ⊂(M,I)\Sigma\subset(M,I) is a proper (possibly empty) complex submanifold. If M=ℂ​Pn1×ℂ​P1M={\mathbb{C}}P^{n_{1}}\times{\mathbb{C}}P^{1} (with the product complex structure), we also allow
Σ=(ℂ​Pn1−1×ℂ​P1)∪(ℂ​Pn1×pt)\Sigma=({\mathbb{C}}P^{n_{1}-1}\times{\mathbb{C}}P^{1})\cup({\mathbb{C}}P^{n_{1}}\times\textrm{pt}).
Then the following conditions are equivalent:
1.
There exists 𝕩=(x1,…,xk)∈M^k{\mathbbm{x}}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\in{\hat{M}}^{k}, x1,…,xk∈M∖Σx_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in M\setminus\Sigma, so that the cohomology class Π∗​[ω]−π​∑i=1kri2​ei∈H2​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ)\Pi^{*}[\omega]-\pi\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}^{2}e_{i}\in H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}}) is Kähler with respect to I~{\widetilde{I}}.
2.
There exists an [I][I]-Kähler-type embedding f:⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→(M∖Σ,ω)f:\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to(M\setminus\Sigma,\omega)
which is holomorphic with respect to a complex structure ϕ1∗​I\phi_{1}^{*}I compatible with ω\omega, where
{ϕt}0≤t≤1⊂Diff0​(M)\{\phi_{t}\}_{0\leq t\leq 1}\subset\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M), ϕ0=I​d\phi_{0}=Id, is an isotopy such that ϕt​(Σ)=Σ\phi_{t}(\Sigma)=\Sigma for all t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1].
(II)
Let 𝒞0{\mathcal{C}}_{0} be a connected component of 𝐶𝐾⁡(M)\mathit{CK}(M).
Assume that
the set 𝙺𝒞0​(r¯){\tt{K}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}({\bar{r}}) (or, equivalently, the set 𝒦𝒞0​(r¯){\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}({\bar{r}}))
is connected.
Then any two Kähler-type embeddings ⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→(M,ω)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to(M,\omega) favoring 𝒞0{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
lie in the same Symp⁡(M,ω)∩Diff0​(M)\mathrm{Symp}(M,\omega)\cap\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M)-orbit. In particular, there exists [I]∈𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M,ω)[I]\in{\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M,\omega) such that
both embeddings are of [I][I]-Kähler type.
If, in addition, SympH​(M)\mathrm{Symp}_{H}(M) acts transitively on the set of connected components of 𝐶𝐾⁡(M)\mathit{CK}(M) compatible with ω\omega, then
any two Kähler-type embeddings ⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→(M,ω)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to(M,\omega)
lie in the same SympH​(M,ω)\mathrm{Symp}_{H}(M,\omega)-orbit.
Proof of 5.2 (=3.1):
Let us prove part (I) of 5.2.
Let 𝒞0{\mathcal{C}}_{0} be a connected component of 𝐶𝐾⁡(M)\mathit{CK}(M) containing the complex structure II.
Define the set Y⊃ΣY\supset\Sigma as follows: If Σ\Sigma is a complex submanifold of (M,I)(M,I), set Y=ΣY=\Sigma.
If M=ℂ​Pn1×ℂ​P1M={\mathbb{C}}P^{n_{1}}\times{\mathbb{C}}P^{1} and
Σ=(ℂ​Pn1−1×ℂ​P1)∪(ℂ​Pn1×pt)\Sigma=({\mathbb{C}}P^{n_{1}-1}\times{\mathbb{C}}P^{1})\cup({\mathbb{C}}P^{n_{1}}\times\textrm{pt}), let YY be the union of
Σ\Sigma and of the closure of an open neighborhood of (ℂ​Pn1−1×ℂ​P1)∩(ℂ​Pn1×pt)({\mathbb{C}}P^{n_{1}-1}\times{\mathbb{C}}P^{1})\cap({\mathbb{C}}P^{n_{1}}\times\textrm{pt}).
Let us prove 1⟹21\Longrightarrow 2. Assume that 𝕩=(x1,…,xk)∈M^k{\mathbbm{x}}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\in{\hat{M}}^{k}, x1,…,xk∈M∖Σx_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in M\setminus\Sigma,
so that the cohomology class Π∗​[ω]−π​∑i=1kri2​ei∈H2​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ)\Pi^{*}[\omega]-\pi\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}^{2}e_{i}\in H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}}) is Kähler with respect to I~{\widetilde{I}}. Choose a holomorphic embedding h=⨆i=1khi:⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri+ϵ)→(M,I)h=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}h_{i}:\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i}+\epsilon)\to(M,I),
for some ϵ>0\epsilon>0,
so that hi​(0)=xih_{i}(0)=x_{i}, i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k, and Im​h∩Y=∅{\rm{Im}\,}h\cap Y=\emptyset.
Then (I,𝕩,h)∈𝑇𝑟𝑖𝑝𝑙𝑒𝑠𝒞0(I,{\mathbbm{x}},h)\in{\it Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}.
By part 1 of 5.2,
there exist a symplectic form η\eta compatible with II and cohomologous to ω\omega and a sufficiently small 0<κ≤10<\kappa\leq 1, so that the embedding h∘Rκ:⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→Mh\circ R_{\kappa}:\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to M is symplectic with respect to η\eta and holomorphic with respect to II. In particular, Im⁡(h∘Rκ)∩Y=∅{\rm{Im}\,}(h\circ R_{\kappa})\cap Y=\emptyset. The linear family of 2-forms connecting η\eta
and ω\omega is formed by cohomologous symplectic forms compatible with II and, in particular, their restrictions to Σ\Sigma are non-degenerate
(in the case M=ℂ​Pn1×ℂ​P1M={\mathbb{C}}P^{n_{1}}\times{\mathbb{C}}P^{1} and
Σ=(ℂ​Pn1−1×ℂ​P1)∪(ℂ​Pn1×pt)\Sigma=({\mathbb{C}}P^{n_{1}-1}\times{\mathbb{C}}P^{1})\cup({\mathbb{C}}P^{n_{1}}\times\textrm{pt}), the restrictions of the forms to both
ℂ​Pn1−1×ℂ​P1{\mathbb{C}}P^{n_{1}-1}\times{\mathbb{C}}P^{1} and ℂ​Pn1×pt{\mathbb{C}}P^{n_{1}}\times\textrm{pt} are non-degenerate).
By a relative version of Moser’s theorem (see 13.3, parts II and III),
there exists an isotopy {ϕt}0≤t≤1⊂Diff0​(M)\{\phi_{t}\}_{0\leq t\leq 1}\subset\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M), ϕ0=I​d\phi_{0}=Id, ϕt​(Σ)=Σ\phi_{t}(\Sigma)=\Sigma for all t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1], so that (ϕ1−1)∗​ω=η(\phi_{1}^{-1})^{*}\omega=\eta outside YY.
Then ϕ−1∘h∘Rκ\phi^{-1}\circ h\circ R_{\kappa} is an embedding of ⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i}) into M∖ΣM\setminus\Sigma
which is symplectic with respect to ω\omega and holomorphic with respect to the complex structure ϕ∗​I\phi^{*}I on MM compatible with ω\omega.
Now let us prove 2⟹12\Longrightarrow 1. Assume f=⨆i=1kfi:⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→(M∖Σ,ω)f=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}:\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to(M\setminus\Sigma,\omega) is a Kähler-type embedding holomorphic with
respect to a complex structure J:=ϕ1∗​IJ:=\phi_{1}^{*}I, which is compatible with ω\omega, where {ϕt}0≤t≤1⊂Diff0​(M)\{\phi_{t}\}_{0\leq t\leq 1}\subset\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M)
is an isotopy such that ϕ0=I​d\phi_{0}=Id, ϕt​(Σ)=Σ\phi_{t}(\Sigma)=\Sigma for all t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1].
Let xi:=fi​(0)x_{i}:=f_{i}(0), i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k. Set 𝕩:=(x1,…,xk){\mathbbm{x}}:=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}).
Then, by property 2 of Φ\Phi listed in 5.2,
(J,𝕩,f)∈𝑇𝑟𝑖𝑝𝑙𝑒𝑠𝒞0(J,{\mathbbm{x}},f)\in{\it Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}},
meaning, in particular, that the cohomology class Π∗​[ω]−π​∑i=1kri2​ei\Pi^{*}[\omega]-\pi\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}^{2}e_{i} in H2​(M~J,𝕩,ℝ)H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{J,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}}) is Kähler with respect to J~{\widetilde{J}}. Since x1,…,xk∈M∖Σx_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in M\setminus\Sigma and ϕ1​(Σ)=Σ\phi_{1}(\Sigma)=\Sigma,
we get that the cohomology class Π∗​[ω]−π​∑i=1kri2​ei\Pi^{*}[\omega]-\pi\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}^{2}e_{i} in H2​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ)H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}}) is Kähler with respect to I~{\widetilde{I}}.
This finishes the proof of part (I) of 5.2.
Let us prove part (II) of 5.2.
Assume that the set 𝙺𝒞0​(r¯){\tt{K}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}({\bar{r}})
is connected.
By 5.2, this means that
𝚃𝚛𝚒𝚙𝚕𝚎𝚜𝒞0{\tt Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} is connected. Since, by 5.2,
Φ:𝚃𝚛𝚒𝚙𝚕𝚎𝚜𝒞0→𝙿𝚊𝚒𝚛𝚜𝒞0\Phi:{\tt Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}\to{\tt Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} is a surjective continuous map, we get that
𝙿𝚊𝚒𝚛𝚜𝒞0{\tt Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} is connected. Hence, 5.1
implies that Symp⁡(M,ω)∩Diff0​(M)\mathrm{Symp}(M,\omega)\cap\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M) acts transitively on EKM,ω,𝒞0​(⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri))\textit{EK}_{M,\omega,{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}\left(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\right).
In particular, by 2.3, there exists [I]∈𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M,ω)[I]\in{\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M,\omega) such that
both embeddings are of [I][I]-Kähler type.
Assume, in addition, that SympH​(M)\mathrm{Symp}_{H}(M) acts transitively on the set of connected components of 𝐶𝐾⁡(M)\mathit{CK}(M) compatible with ω\omega.
Then any element of EKM,ω​(⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri))\textit{EK}_{M,\omega}\left(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\right)
can be mapped by SympH​(M)\mathrm{Symp}_{H}(M) into an element of the space EKM,ω,𝒞0​(⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri))\textit{EK}_{M,\omega,{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}\left(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\right).
The transitivity of the Symp⁡(M,ω)∩Diff0​(M)\mathrm{Symp}(M,\omega)\cap\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M)-action on the latter space yields that
any two Kähler-type embeddings
of ⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i}) into (M,ω)(M,\omega)
can be mapped into each other by an element of SympH​(M,ω)\mathrm{Symp}_{H}(M,\omega).
This finishes the proof of part (II) of 5.2.
5.3 Regularized maximum
Given a (1,1)(1,1)-form θ\theta on a complex manifold with a complex structure II, we write θ>0\theta>0 if θ⁡(v,I​v)>0\theta(v,Iv)>0 for any non-zero tangent vector vv of the manifold. If θ′\theta^{\prime} is another (1,1)(1,1)-form on the same complex manifold, we write θ>θ′\theta>\theta^{\prime} if θ−θ′>0\theta-\theta^{\prime}>0.
Recall from [Dem, Lemma 5.18] that
for any smooth functions Q1,Q2Q_{1},Q_{2} on a complex manifold and any δ>0\delta>0, one can define their regularized maximum maxδ⁡{Q1,Q2}\max_{\delta}\{Q_{1},Q_{2}\}. It is a smooth function on the same manifold, depending smoothly on δ\delta, and satisfying the following conditions:
1. max⁡{Q1,Q2}≤maxδ⁡{Q1,Q2}≤max⁡{Q1,Q2}+δ\max\{Q_{1},Q_{2}\}\leq\max_{\delta}\{Q_{1},Q_{2}\}\leq\max\{Q_{1},Q_{2}\}+\delta;
2. maxδ⁡{Q1,Q2}​(y)=Qj​(y)\max_{\delta}\{Q_{1},Q_{2}\}(y)=Q_{j}(y), if Qi​(y)+2​δ≤Qj​(y)Q_{i}(y)+2\delta\leq Q_{j}(y), i,j=1,2i,j=1,2, i≠ji\neq j.
3. If ϑ\vartheta is a
Hermitian form on the manifold and −1​∂∂¯​Qi>−ϑ\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}Q_{i}>-\vartheta, i=1,2i=1,2, then
−1​∂∂¯​maxδ​{Q1,Q2}>−ϑ.\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}\max_{\delta}\{Q_{1},Q_{2}\}>-\vartheta.
5.4 Blow-up – local constructions
First, we recall some standard local constructions concerning the blow-up of ℂn{\mathbb{C}}^{n} at the origin.
Denote by z1,…,znz_{1},\ldots,z_{n} the complex coordinates on ℂn{\mathbb{C}}^{n}.
For z=(z1,…,zn)∈ℂnz=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n} denote
|z|:=|z1|2+…+|zn|2.|z|:=\sqrt{|z_{1}|^{2}+\ldots+|z_{n}|^{2}}.
As before, let ω0\omega_{0} be the standard symplectic form on ℂn≅ℝ2​n{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\cong{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}. It can be written as
ω0=−1​∂∂¯​|z|2/2.\omega_{0}=\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}|z|^{2}/2.
(5.1)
Let σ\sigma be the Fubini-Study symplectic form on ℂ​Pn−1{\mathbb{C}}P^{n-1} normalized by ∫ℂ​P1σ=π\int_{{\mathbb{C}}P^{1}}\sigma=\pi.
Let ℒ⊂ℂn×ℂ​Pn−1{\mathcal{L}}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{C}}P^{n-1} be the incidence relation, i.e. ℒ:={(z,𝔩)|z∈𝔩}{\mathcal{L}}:=\{(z,{\mathfrak{l}})\ |\ z\in{\mathfrak{l}}\}. It is the complex blow-up of ℂn{\mathbb{C}}^{n} at the origin. Let p​r1:ℒ→ℂnpr_{1}:{\mathcal{L}}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{n}, p​r2:ℒ→ℂ​Pn−1pr_{2}:{\mathcal{L}}\to{\mathbb{C}}P^{n-1} be the natural projections.
For r>0r>0, denote ℒ⁡(r):=p​r1−1​(B2​n​(r)){\mathcal{L}}(r):=pr_{1}^{-1}(B^{2n}(r)).
We will also write ℒ⁡(0):=p​r1−1​(0){\mathcal{L}}(0):=pr_{1}^{-1}(0).
Given c,r>0c,r>0, define a differential 2-form ρ⁡(c,r)\rho(c,r) on ℒ{\mathcal{L}} as
ρ⁡(c,r):=c2​p​r1∗​ω0+r2​p​r2∗​σ.\rho(c,r):=c^{2}pr_{1}^{*}\omega_{0}+r^{2}pr_{2}^{*}\sigma.
The restriction of p​r1pr_{1} to ℒ∖p​r1−1​(0){\mathcal{L}}\setminus pr_{1}^{-1}(0) is a diffeomorphism ℒ∖p​r1−1​(0)→ℂn∖0{\mathcal{L}}\setminus pr_{1}^{-1}(0)\to{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\setminus 0. One can easily see that
it identifies the form ρ⁡(c,r)\rho(c,r) on ℒ∖ℒ⁡(0){\mathcal{L}}\setminus{\mathcal{L}}(0) with the form (p​r1)∗​ρ​(c,r)(pr_{1})_{*}\rho(c,r) on ℂn∖0{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\setminus 0 that can be written as
(p​r1)∗​ρ​(c,r)=−12​∂∂¯​(c2​|z|2+r2​log⁡|z|2+d)(pr_{1})_{*}\rho(c,r)=\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2}\partial\bar{\partial}\left(c^{2}|z|^{2}+r^{2}\log|z|^{2}+d\right)
(5.2)
for any d∈ℝd\in{\mathbb{R}}.
The following two propositions and their proofs describe local constructions needed further on to define symplectic blow-up and blow-down operations. Here we use the regularized maximum for these constructions – cf. e.g. [McDP], [McDS],
where different tools were used.
Proposition 5.10
Let r,ϵ>0r,\epsilon>0.
Then for any sufficiently small c>0c>0 there exists a Kähler form ϱc\varrho^{c} on ℂn∖0{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\setminus 0, depending smoothly on cc, so that
•
ϱc=ω0\varrho^{c}=\omega_{0} on a neighborhood of ℂn∖I​n​t​B2​n​(r+ϵ){\mathbb{C}}^{n}\setminus Int\,B^{2n}(r+\epsilon),
•
ϱc=(p​r1)∗​ρ​(c,r)\varrho^{c}=(pr_{1})_{*}\rho(c,r) on a neighborhood of B2​n​(r)∖0B^{2n}(r)\setminus 0.
Proof of 5.4:
The functions s2−r2s^{2}-r^{2} and r2​log⁡((s/r)2)r^{2}\log\left((s/r)^{2}\right) (as functions of ss) coincide with their first derivatives at s=rs=r. The first function is convex while the second one is concave and s2−r2>r2​log⁡((s/r)2)s^{2}-r^{2}>r^{2}\log\left((s/r)^{2}\right) for all s>0s>0, s≠rs\neq r.
Therefore for any sufficiently small c>0c>0 and a sufficiently small σ>0\sigma>0 (depending on cc) the functions F⁡(s):=s2−r2−σF(s):=s^{2}-r^{2}-\sigma and G⁡(s):=c​s2+r2​log⁡s2G(s):=cs^{2}+r^{2}\log s^{2} have exactly two points
s1,s2s_{1},s_{2}, 0<s1<r<s2<r+ϵ0<s_{1}<r<s_{2}<r+\epsilon, where they coincide, so that F⁡(s)<G⁡(s)F(s)<G(s) on [s1,r)[s_{1},r), while
F⁡(s)>G⁡(s)F(s)>G(s) on (s2,r+ϵ](s_{2},r+\epsilon].
Choose δ>0\delta>0 so that F⁡(r)<G⁡(r)−2​δF(r)<G(r)-2\delta
and F⁡(r+ϵ)>G⁡(r+ϵ)+2​δF(r+\epsilon)>G(r+\epsilon)+2\delta.
Define H:ℂn∖0→ℝH:{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\setminus 0\to{\mathbb{R}} by H⁡(z):=maxδ⁡{F⁡(|z2|),G⁡(|z2|)}H(z):=\max_{\delta}\{F(|z^{2}|),G(|z^{2}|)\}, where maxδ\max_{\delta} is the regularized maximum mentioned in Section 5.3.
It follows from (5.1)
and (5.2) and the properties of the regularized maximum (see Section 5.3) that
(−1/2)⋅∂∂¯​H(\sqrt{-1}/2)\cdot\partial\bar{\partial}H is a Kähler form on ℂn∖0{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\setminus 0 equal to
(−1/2)⋅∂∂¯​F=ω0(\sqrt{-1}/2)\cdot\partial\bar{\partial}F=\omega_{0} on a neighborhood ℂn∖I​n​t​B2​n​(r+ϵ){\mathbb{C}}^{n}\setminus Int\,B^{2n}(r+\epsilon),
and to (−1/2)⋅∂∂¯​G=(p​r1)∗​ρ​(c,r)(\sqrt{-1}/2)\cdot\partial\bar{\partial}G=(pr_{1})_{*}\rho(c,r) on a neighborhood of the sphere ∂B2​n​(r)\partial B^{2n}(r).
Define the form ϱc\varrho^{c} on ℂn∖0{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\setminus 0 as (p​r1)∗​ρ​(c,r)(pr_{1})_{*}\rho(c,r) on B2​n​(r)∖0B^{2n}(r)\setminus 0, (−1/2)⋅∂∂¯​G(\sqrt{-1}/2)\cdot\partial\bar{\partial}G on the neighborhood of ∂B2​n​(r)\partial B^{2n}(r) and (−1/2)⋅∂∂¯​H(\sqrt{-1}/2)\cdot\partial\bar{\partial}H outside
∂B2​n​(r)\partial B^{2n}(r). It is a well-defined form. The parameters σ,s1,s2,δ\sigma,s_{1},s_{2},\delta in the construction can be chosen to depend smoothly on cc
and thus ϱc\varrho^{c} can be assumed to depend smoothly on cc.
One easily sees that ϱc\varrho^{c} satisfies the bulleted properties listed in the proposition.
This finishes the proof.
Proposition 5.11
Let r,ϵ>0r,\epsilon>0.
Then for any c>0c>0 there exists a Kähler form ϱc\varrho_{c} on ℂn{\mathbb{C}}^{n}, depending smoothly on cc, so that
•
ϱc=ω0\varrho_{c}=\omega_{0} on a neighborhood of B2​n​(r)B^{2n}(r),
•
ϱc=(p​r1)∗​ρ​(c,r)\varrho_{c}=(pr_{1})_{*}\rho(c,r) on a neighborhood of ℂn∖I​n​t​B2​n​(r+ϵ){\mathbb{C}}^{n}\setminus Int\,B^{2n}(r+\epsilon).
Proof of 5.4:
For an appropriate constant d∈ℝd\in{\mathbb{R}} the functions F⁡(s):=s2+dF(s):=s^{2}+d and G⁡(s):=c2​s2+r2​log⁡((s/r)2)G(s):=c^{2}s^{2}+r^{2}\log\left((s/r)^{2}\right) (as functions of ss) are equal at some point s1∈(r,r+ϵ)s_{1}\in(r,r+\epsilon), so that F⁡(s)>G⁡(s)F(s)>G(s) on [r,s1)[r,s_{1}), F⁡(s)<G⁡(s)F(s)<G(s) on (s1,s2](s_{1},s_{2}] for some s2∈(s1,r+ϵ)s_{2}\in(s_{1},r+\epsilon).
Having chosen such s1,s2s_{1},s_{2},
pick δ>0\delta>0 so that F⁡(r)>G⁡(r)+2​δF(r)>G(r)+2\delta
and F⁡(s2)<G⁡(s2)−2​δF(s_{2})<G(s_{2})-2\delta.
Define H:ℂn∖0→ℝH:{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\setminus 0\to{\mathbb{R}} by H⁡(z):=maxδ⁡{F⁡(|z2|),G⁡(|z2|)}H(z):=\max_{\delta}\{F(|z^{2}|),G(|z^{2}|)\}, where maxδ\max_{\delta} is the regularized maximum mentioned in Section 5.3.
It follows from (5.1)
and (5.2) and the properties of the regularized maximum (see
Section 5.3) that (−1/2)⋅∂∂¯​H(\sqrt{-1}/2)\cdot\partial\bar{\partial}H is a Kähler form on ℂn∖0{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\setminus 0 equal to (−1/2)⋅∂∂¯​F=ω0(\sqrt{-1}/2)\cdot\partial\bar{\partial}F=\omega_{0} on
B2​n​(r+σ)∖I​n​t​B2​n​(r)B^{2n}(r+\sigma)\setminus Int\,B^{2n}(r) for some sufficiently small σ>0\sigma>0, and to
(−1/2)⋅∂∂¯​G=(p​r1)∗​ρ​(c,r)(\sqrt{-1}/2)\cdot\partial\bar{\partial}G=(pr_{1})_{*}\rho(c,r) on a neighborhood of ∂B2​n​(s2)\partial B^{2n}(s_{2}).
Define a form ϱc\varrho_{c} on ℂn{\mathbb{C}}^{n} as ω0\omega_{0} on B2​n​(r)B^{2n}(r), as (−1/2)⋅∂∂¯​H(\sqrt{-1}/2)\cdot\partial\bar{\partial}H on B2​n​(s2)∖B2​n​(r)B^{2n}(s_{2})\setminus B^{2n}(r) and as (−1/2)⋅∂∂¯​G=(p​r1)∗​ρ​(c,r)(\sqrt{-1}/2)\cdot\partial\bar{\partial}G=(pr_{1})_{*}\rho(c,r) on ℂn∖B2​n​(s2){\mathbb{C}}^{n}\setminus B^{2n}(s_{2}).
It is a well-defined smooth form. The parameters d,s1,s2,δd,s_{1},s_{2},\delta in the construction can be chosen to depend smoothly on cc
and thus ϱc\varrho_{c} can be assumed to depend smoothly on cc.
One easily sees that ϱc\varrho_{c} satisfies the bulleted properties listed in the proposition.
This finishes the proof.
Lemma 5.12 (∂∂¯\partial\bar{\partial}-lemma for a complex blow-up of a ball)
Let B⊂ℂnB\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n} be a ball centered at the origin 0∈ℂn0\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n}. Let B~{\widetilde{B}} be the complex blow-up of BB at 00.
Then any exact (1,1)(1,1)-form υ\upsilon on B~{\widetilde{B}} can be written as υ=−1​∂∂¯​Q\upsilon=\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}Q for a smooth real-valued function QQ on B~{\widetilde{B}}.
Proof of 5.4:
Let π:B~→B\pi:{\widetilde{B}}\to B be the natural projection. Let 𝒪B~{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{B}} be the structure sheaf (i.e., the sheaf of germs of holomorphic functions) on B~{\widetilde{B}}.
Note that
Ri​π∗​𝒪B~=0R^{i}\pi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{B}}=0 for all i>1i>1
(see [Ue, Prop. 2.14],
cf. [Hi, (2), p.144]).
Consequently, H1​(B~,𝒪B~)=H1​(B,π∗​𝒪B~)=0H^{1}({\widetilde{B}},{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{B}})=H^{1}(B,\pi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{B}})=0.
By the Dolbeault theorem, this implies that any ∂¯\bar{\partial}-closed (0,1)(0,1)-form on B~{\widetilde{B}} is ∂¯\bar{\partial}-exact. The latter fact yields, by the complex conjugation symmetry, that any ∂\partial-closed (1,0)(1,0)-form on B~{\widetilde{B}} is ∂\partial-exact.
Since υ\upsilon is exact, it can be written as υ=d​σ\upsilon=d\sigma for some 1-form σ\sigma on B~{\widetilde{B}}. Write σ\sigma as a sum of its (1,0)(1,0) and (0,1)(0,1)-parts: σ=σ1,0+σ0,1\sigma=\sigma^{1,0}+\sigma^{0,1}. Then the 1-form σ0,1\sigma^{0,1} is ∂¯\bar{\partial}-closed and hence ∂¯\bar{\partial}-exact, meaning that σ0,1=∂¯​S\sigma^{0,1}=\bar{\partial}S for a smooth function SS on B~{\widetilde{B}}. One readily checks that the form σ−d​S\sigma-dS is of type (1,0)(1,0) and ∂\partial-closed, hence ∂\partial-exact,
meaning that σ−d​S=∂(−−1​Q)\sigma-dS=\partial(-\sqrt{-1}Q) for a smooth function QQ on B~{\widetilde{B}}. Finally, a direct computation yields
υ=∂¯​∂(−−1​Q)=−1​∂∂¯​Q\upsilon=\bar{\partial}\partial(-\sqrt{-1}Q)=\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}Q. Since the form υ\upsilon is real, the function QQ can be also chosen to be real-valued. This finishes the proof of the lemma.
5.5 Symplectic forms on complex blow-ups of MM
Let II be a complex structure on MM and let 𝕩=(x1,…,xk)∈M^k{\mathbbm{x}}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\in{\hat{M}}^{k}.
Fix an
II-holomorphic embedding h=⨆i=1khi:⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→Mh=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}h_{i}:\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to M, such that hi​(0)=xih_{i}(0)=x_{i}, i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k, and hh extends to a holomorphic embedding
h=⨆i=1khi:⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri+ϵ)→Mh=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}h_{i}:\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i}+\epsilon)\to M for some ϵ>0\epsilon>0.
Each map hi:B2​n​(ri+ϵ)→Mh_{i}:B^{2n}(r_{i}+\epsilon)\to M, i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k,
lifts to the unique map
h~i:ℒ⁡(ri+ϵ)→M~I,𝕩{\widetilde{h}}_{i}:{\mathcal{L}}(r_{i}+\epsilon)\to{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}} such that
hi∘p​r1=Π∘h~ih_{i}\circ pr_{1}=\Pi\circ{\widetilde{h}}_{i},
where p​r1:ℒ→ℂnpr_{1}:{\mathcal{L}}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{n} is the projection defined in Section 5.4.
For any c,r>0c,r>0 the map h~i{\widetilde{h}}_{i}, i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k, identifies the form ρ⁡(c,r)\rho(c,r) on ℒ⁡(ri+ϵ){\mathcal{L}}(r_{i}+\epsilon) with a form on a neighborhood of the exceptional divisor Ei⊂M~I,𝕩E_{i}\subset{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}} that will be denoted by ρxi​(c,r)\rho_{x_{i}}(c,r). Each ρxi​(c,r)\rho_{x_{i}}(c,r) is a symplectic form compatible with I~{\widetilde{I}}.
Given a cohomology class α∈H2​(M,ℝ)\alpha\in H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}}), define the cohomology class α~r¯∈H2​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ){\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bar{r}}\in H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}}) by
α~r¯:=Π∗​α−π​∑i=1kri2​ei.{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bar{r}}:=\Pi^{*}\alpha-\pi\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}^{2}e_{i}.
(5.3)
Proposition 5.13
Let α∈H2​(M,ℝ)\alpha\in H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}}).
Assume that the cohomology class α~r¯∈H2​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ){\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bar{r}}\in H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}})
is Kähler with respect to I~{\widetilde{I}}.
Then there exists a symplectic form θ~{\widetilde{\theta}} on M~I,𝕩{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}} which is compatible with I~{\widetilde{I}}, represents α~r¯{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bar{r}}, and equals to
ρxi​(1,ri)\rho_{x_{i}}(1,r_{i}) on a neighborhood of EiE_{i} for each i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k.
Remark 5.14
If one drops the requirement that θ~{\widetilde{\theta}} is compatible with I~{\widetilde{I}}, the existence of a symplectic form θ~{\widetilde{\theta}}, [θ~]=α~r¯[{\widetilde{\theta}}]={\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bar{r}}, coinciding with ρxi​(1,ri)\rho_{x_{i}}(1,r_{i}) on a neighborhood of each EiE_{i} can be proved much easier using Moser’s method, as in [McDP, Pf. of Prop. 2.1.C]. The need to make θ~{\widetilde{\theta}} compatible with I~{\widetilde{I}} comes from the fact that otherwise the blow-down construction (see [McDP, Sec. 5]) applied to θ~{\widetilde{\theta}} will produce only a symplectic, but not necessarily a holomorphic, embedding of balls. Unlike Moser’s method – which can be applied, as in [McDP, Pf. of Prop. 2.1.C], also when II is not necessarily integrable outside neighborhoods of the base points x1,…,xkx_{1},\ldots,x_{k} – here we do rely on the integrability of II on the whole MM, as we use methods of complex geometry.
Proof of 5.5:
The argument below uses a result
based on an idea of Demailly and
published in [OV, Thm. 4.1] and, moreover, mimics the idea of its proof.
Since the cohomology class α~r¯∈H2​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ){\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bar{r}}\in H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}})
is Kähler, we can pick a symplectic form η~{\widetilde{\eta}} that represents it and that is compatible with I~{\widetilde{I}}.
For each i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k denote:
Uxi:=h⁡(B2​n​(ri+ϵ)),U_{x_{i}}:=h\left(B^{2n}(r_{i}+\epsilon)\right),
Uxi′:=h⁡(B2​n​(ri+ϵ/2)),U^{\prime}_{x_{i}}:=h\left(B^{2n}(r_{i}+\epsilon/2)\right),
U~xi:=h~i​(ℒ⁡(ri+ϵ))=Π−1​(Uxi),{\widetilde{U}}_{x_{i}}:={\widetilde{h}}_{i}\left({\mathcal{L}}(r_{i}+\epsilon)\right)=\Pi^{-1}(U_{x_{i}}),
U~xi′:=h~i​(ℒ⁡(ri))=Π−1​(Uxi′).{\widetilde{U}}^{\prime}_{x_{i}}:={\widetilde{h}}_{i}\left({\mathcal{L}}(r_{i})\right)=\Pi^{-1}(U^{\prime}_{x_{i}}).
Thus, Uxi′⊂UxiU^{\prime}_{x_{i}}\subset U_{x_{i}}, U~xi′⊂U~xi{\widetilde{U}}^{\prime}_{x_{i}}\subset{\widetilde{U}}_{x_{i}}.
Let δ>0\delta>0.
Let E:=⋃i=1kEiE:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}E_{i} be the union of the exceptional divisors of M~I,𝕩{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}}.
Since EE is a complex submanifold of (M~I,𝕩,I~)({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}},{\widetilde{I}}), by a result of Demailly-Paun [DP, Lem. 2.1], there exists a smooth real-valued function GG on M~I,𝕩∖E{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}}\setminus E so that
−1​∂∂¯​G>−η~\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}G>-{\widetilde{\eta}}
(5.4)
and GG has logarithmic poles along EiE_{i}, i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k.
The set U~xi{\widetilde{U}}_{x_{i}} is biholomorphic to the blow-up of the ball at the origin and ρxi​(1,ri)−η~\rho_{x_{i}}(1,r_{i})-{\widetilde{\eta}}
is an exact (1,1)(1,1)-form on U~xi{\widetilde{U}}_{x_{i}}. Therefore, by 5.4,
ρxi​(1,ri)−η~|U~xi=−1​∂∂¯​Fi\rho_{x_{i}}(1,r_{i})-{\widetilde{\eta}}|_{{\widetilde{U}}_{x_{i}}}=\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}F_{i}
(5.5)
for
a smooth function Fi:U~xi→ℝF_{i}:{\widetilde{U}}_{x_{i}}\to{\mathbb{R}}. Adding, if necessary, a constant to FiF_{i}, we may assume
without loss of generality that
Fi<G−2δon an open neigborhood ofU~xi∖U~′xi,i=1,…,k.F_{i}<G-2\delta\ \textrm{on an open neigborhood of}\ {\widetilde{U}}_{x_{i}}\setminus{\widetilde{U}}^{\prime}_{x_{i}},i=1,\ldots,k.
(5.6)
Since
the function GG tends to −∞-\infty as its argument converges to EE, for any i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k
there exists a neighborhood Vxi⊂Uxi′V_{x_{i}}\subset U^{\prime}_{x_{i}} of xix_{i}, whose preimage Π−1​(Vxi)⊂U~xi′\Pi^{-1}(V_{x_{i}})\subset{\widetilde{U}}^{\prime}_{x_{i}} will be denoted
by V~xi:=Π−1​(Vxi){\widetilde{V}}_{x_{i}}:=\Pi^{-1}(V_{x_{i}}), so that
G+2δ<FionV~xi,i=1,…,k.G+2\delta<F_{i}\ \textrm{on}\ {\widetilde{V}}_{x_{i}},\ i=1,\ldots,k.
(5.7)
Define the function K:M~I,𝕩→ℝK:{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}}\to{\mathbb{R}} as follows
K:=maxδ⁡{Fi,G},on​U~xi,i=1,…,k,K:=\max_{\delta}\{F_{i},G\},\ \textrm{on}\ {\widetilde{U}}_{x_{i}},i=1,\ldots,k,
K:=G,on​M~I,𝕩∖⋃i=1kU~xi′.K:=G,\ \textrm{on}\ {\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}}\setminus\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}{\widetilde{U}}^{\prime}_{x_{i}}.
By (5.6), (5.7)
and the properties of the regularized maximum (see Section 5.3),
KK is a well-defined smooth function on M~I,𝕩{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}}, equal to
FiF_{i} on V~xi{\widetilde{V}}_{x_{i}}, to GG on M~I,𝕩∖⋃i=1kU~i′{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}}\setminus\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}{\widetilde{U}}^{\prime}_{i}, and to
maxδ⁡{Fi,G}\max_{\delta}\{F_{i},G\} on U~xi′∖V~xi{\widetilde{U}}^{\prime}_{x_{i}}\setminus{\widetilde{V}}_{x_{i}}, i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k.
Define the smooth 2-form θ~{\widetilde{\theta}} on M~I,𝕩{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}} by
θ~:=η~+−1​∂∂¯​K.{\widetilde{\theta}}:={\widetilde{\eta}}+\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}K.
The form θ~{\widetilde{\theta}} is of type (1,1)(1,1) with respect to I~{\widetilde{I}}.
Let us verify that θ~{\widetilde{\theta}} is positive – i.e. symplectic. We need to verify
that
−1​∂∂¯​K>−η~​everywhere on​M~I,𝕩.\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}K>-{\widetilde{\eta}}\ \textrm{everywhere on}\ {\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}}.
(5.8)
Let us verify the latter inequality case by case
for the points of V~xi{\widetilde{V}}_{x_{i}}, U~xi′∖V~xi{\widetilde{U}}^{\prime}_{x_{i}}\setminus{\widetilde{V}}_{x_{i}}, i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k, and M~I,𝕩∖⋃i=1kU~xi′{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}}\setminus\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}{\widetilde{U}}^{\prime}_{x_{i}}.
Consider first the case of V~xi{\widetilde{V}}_{x_{i}}, i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k. By
(5.5), and since ρxi​(1,ri)>0\rho_{x_{i}}(1,r_{i})>0, we have
−1​∂∂¯​Fi=−η~+ρxi​(1,ri)>−η~,on​U~xi,i=1,…,k,\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}F_{i}=-{\widetilde{\eta}}+\rho_{x_{i}}(1,r_{i})>-{\widetilde{\eta}},\ \textrm{on}\ {\widetilde{U}}_{x_{i}},i=1,\ldots,k,
(5.9)
Hence, for the points of V~xi⊂U~xi{\widetilde{V}}_{x_{i}}\subset{\widetilde{U}}_{x_{i}}, i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k,
−1​∂∂¯​K=−1​∂∂¯​Fi>−η~,\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}K=\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}F_{i}>-{\widetilde{\eta}},
meaning that the inequality (5.8) is satisfied in this case.
For the points of U~xi′∖V~xi{\widetilde{U}}^{\prime}_{x_{i}}\setminus{\widetilde{V}}_{x_{i}}, i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k, we have
−1​∂∂¯​Fi>−η~\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}F_{i}>-{\widetilde{\eta}}
by (5.9), and
−1​∂∂¯​G>−η~\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}G>-{\widetilde{\eta}}
by (5.4).
Hence, for such points
−1​∂∂¯​K=−1​∂∂¯​maxδ​{Fi,G}>−η~,\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}K=\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}\max_{\delta}\{F_{i},G\}>-{\widetilde{\eta}},
by the third property of the regularized maximum (see Section 5.3).
Thus, the inequality (5.8) is satisfied in this case too.
Finally, for the points of M~I,𝕩∖⋃i=1kU~xi′{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}}\setminus\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}{\widetilde{U}}^{\prime}_{x_{i}},
−1​∂∂¯​G>−η~\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}G>-{\widetilde{\eta}}
by (5.4).
Hence,
−1​∂∂¯​K=−1​∂∂¯​G>−η~,\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}K=\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}G>-{\widetilde{\eta}},
meaning that the inequality (5.8) is satisfied also in this case.
This finishes the verification of (5.8).
We have shown that θ~{\widetilde{\theta}} is a positive (1,1)(1,1)-form with respect to I~{\widetilde{I}}, which means that it is compatible with I~{\widetilde{I}}. It is cohomologous to η~{\widetilde{\eta}}, meaning that it represents the cohomology class α~r¯{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bar{r}}. By
(5.5),
θ~=η~+−1∂∂¯K=η~+−1∂∂¯Fi=ρxi(1,ri)onV~xi,i=1,…,k.{\widetilde{\theta}}={\widetilde{\eta}}+\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}K={\widetilde{\eta}}+\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}F_{i}=\rho_{x_{i}}(1,r_{i})\ \textrm{on}\ {\widetilde{V}}_{x_{i}},i=1,\ldots,k.
This finishes the proof
of the proposition.
Recall that for each κ>0\kappa>0 we define
Rκ:ℝ2​n→ℝ2​n,Rκ​(x):=κ​x.R_{\kappa}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2n},\ R_{\kappa}(x):=\kappa x.
The map RκR_{\kappa} induces a diffeomorphism of ℒ⊂ℂn×ℂ​Pn−1{\mathcal{L}}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{C}}P^{n-1} that will be denoted by R~κ{\widetilde{R}}_{\kappa}:
R~κ:ℒ→ℒ.{\widetilde{R}}_{\kappa}:{\mathcal{L}}\to{\mathcal{L}}.
For κ∈(0,1]\kappa\in(0,1], denote by h∘Rκh\circ R_{\kappa} the holomorphic embedding
h∘Rκ:=⨆i=1khi∘Rκ:⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→(M,I).\textstyle h\circ R_{\kappa}:=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}h_{i}\circ R_{\kappa}:\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to(M,I).
For each i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k and each 0<κ≤10<\kappa\leq 1 denote
Uxi,κ:=hi∘Rκ​(B2​n​(ri+ϵ))=hi​(B2​n​(κ⁡(ri+ϵ))).U_{x_{i},\kappa}:=h_{i}\circ R_{\kappa}\left(B^{2n}\left(r_{i}+\epsilon\right)\right)=h_{i}\left(B^{2n}\left(\kappa\left(r_{i}+\epsilon\right)\right)\right).
The following proposition describes the exact version of the symplectic blow-down operation
that we need for our purposes.
Proposition 5.15
Let α∈H2​(M,ℝ)\alpha\in H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}}). Let 0<κ≤10<\kappa\leq 1, c1,…,ck>0c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}>0.
Assume that the cohomology class α~r¯∈H2​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ){\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bar{r}}\in H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}}) is represented by
a symplectic form θ~{\widetilde{\theta}} coincides with
ρxi​(ci,ri)\rho_{x_{i}}(c_{i},r_{i}) on the neighborhood Π−1​(Uxi,κ)\Pi^{-1}(U_{x_{i},\kappa})
of EiE_{i}, i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k.
Then there exists a symplectic form ηκ,θ~\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}} on MM with the
following properties:
•
[ηκ,θ~]=α[\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}}]=\alpha,
•
If θ~{\widetilde{\theta}} is compatible with I~{\widetilde{I}}, then ηκ,θ~\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}} is compatible with II; if I′I^{\prime} is a complex structure
on MM coinciding with II on ⋃i=1kUxi,κ\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}U_{x_{i},\kappa}
and such that θ~{\widetilde{\theta}} tames I~′{\widetilde{I}}^{\prime}, then ηκ,θ~\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}} tames I′I^{\prime}.
•
The embedding h∘Rκ:⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→Mh\circ R_{\kappa}:\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to M is symplectic with respect to ηκ,θ~\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}} (and holomorphic with respect to II).
•
ηκ,θ~\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}} depends smoothly on κ\kappa.
Proof of 5.5:
Assume κ>0\kappa>0 is sufficiently small so that θ~=ρxi​(ci,ri){\widetilde{\theta}}=\rho_{x_{i}}(c_{i},r_{i}) on Π−1​(Uxi,κ)\Pi^{-1}(U_{x_{i},\kappa}) for all i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k. Then R~κ∗∘h~i∗​θ~=ρ⁡(κ​ci,ri){\widetilde{R}}_{\kappa}^{*}\circ{\widetilde{h}}_{i}^{*}{\widetilde{\theta}}=\rho(\kappa c_{i},r_{i}) on ℒ⁡(ri+ϵ){\mathcal{L}}(r_{i}+\epsilon) for all i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k.
For each i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k we can use 5.4 in order to construct a form ϱκ​ci\varrho_{\kappa c_{i}} on B2​n​(ri+ϵ)B^{2n}(r_{i}+\epsilon), depending smoothly on κ\kappa, that equals ω0\omega_{0} on a neighborhood of B2​n​(ri)B^{2n}(r_{i}),
and (p​r1)∗​ρ​(κ​ci,ri)(pr_{1})_{*}\rho(\kappa c_{i},r_{i}) on a neighborhood of ∂B2​n​(ri+ϵ)\partial B^{2n}(r_{i}+\epsilon). Then the form
(hi)∗​ϱκ​ci(h_{i})_{*}\varrho_{\kappa c_{i}} is defined on Uxi,κU_{x_{i},\kappa}
and coincides with Π∗​θ~=Π∗​(ρxi​(κ​ci,ri))\Pi_{*}{\widetilde{\theta}}=\Pi_{*}\left(\rho_{x_{i}}(\kappa c_{i},r_{i})\right) near ∂Uxi,κ\partial U_{x_{i},\kappa}.
Consequently, we can define a 2-form ηκ,θ~\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}} on MM by setting it equal to
Π∗​θ~\Pi_{*}{\widetilde{\theta}} outside ⋃i=1kUxi,κ\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}U_{x_{i},\kappa} and to (hi)∗​ϱκ​ci(h_{i})_{*}\varrho_{\kappa c_{i}} on each
Uxi,κU_{x_{i},\kappa}, i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k.
One easily checks that ηκ,θ~\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}} is symplectic and that the bulleted properties in the proposition are satisfied.
The following proposition is a version of the symplectic blow-up construction – cf. e.g. [McDP], [McDS].
Proposition 5.16
Assume that η\eta is a symplectic form on MM such that the embedding hh is symplectic with respect to η\eta and η\eta is compatible with II on the image of hh.
Then for any sufficiently small c>0c>0 there exists a symplectic form θ~c{\widetilde{\theta}}_{c} on M~I,𝕩{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}} with the following properties:
1. θ~c=ρxi​(c,ri){\widetilde{\theta}}_{c}=\rho_{x_{i}}(c,r_{i}) on Π−1​(hi​(B⁡(ri)))\Pi^{-1}\left(h_{i}\left(B(r_{i})\right)\right), i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k,
2. Π∗​θ~c=η\Pi_{*}{\widetilde{\theta}}_{c}=\eta outside ⨆i=1kh⁡(B2​n​(ri+ϵ))\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}h\left(B^{2n}(r_{i}+\epsilon)\right),
3. θ~c{\widetilde{\theta}}_{c} depends smoothly on cc,
4. If η\eta is compatible with, respectively tames, II on the whole MM, then θ~c{\widetilde{\theta}}_{c} is compatible with,
respectively tames, I~{\widetilde{I}} on the whole M~I,𝕩{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}}.
5. [θ~c]=Π∗​[η]−π​∑i=1kri2​ei[{\widetilde{\theta}}_{c}]=\Pi^{*}[\eta]-\pi\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}^{2}e_{i}.
Proof of 5.5:
Using 5.4 for each B2​n​(ri)B^{2n}(r_{i}), i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k,
for any sufficiently small c>0c>0
we can construct a form θ~c{\widetilde{\theta}}_{c} on M~I,𝕩{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}} with properties 1-4.
Properties 1 and 2 imply that θ~c{\widetilde{\theta}}_{c} is a symplectic form satisfying also property 5.
This finishes the proof.
Proof of 5.2:
Let α:=[ω]∈H2​(M,ℝ)\alpha:=[\omega]\in H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}}).
Consider a triple (I,𝕩,h)∈𝑇𝑟𝑖𝑝𝑙𝑒𝑠𝒞0(I,{\mathbbm{x}},h)\in{\it Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}, where 𝕩=(x1,…,xk){\mathbbm{x}}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}). By 5.2,
the cohomology class α~r¯∈H2​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ){\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bar{r}}\in H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}})
is Kähler with respect to II.
Hence, by 5.5,
there exists a symplectic form θ~{\widetilde{\theta}} on M~I,𝕩{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}} which is compatible with I~{\widetilde{I}}, represents α~r¯{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bar{r}}, and equals to
ρxi​(1,ri)\rho_{x_{i}}(1,r_{i}) on a neighborhood of EiE_{i} for each i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k.
Consequently, 5.5 implies that
for any sufficiently small κ>0\kappa>0 there exists a symplectic form ηκ,θ~\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}} on MM with the
following properties:
•
[ηκ,θ~]=α[\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}}]=\alpha,
•
ηκ,θ~\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}} is compatible with II,
•
h∘Rκ:⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→Mh\circ R_{\kappa}:\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to M is symplectic with respect to ηκ,θ~\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}} (and holomorphic with respect to II),
•
ηκ,θ~\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}} depends smoothly on κ\kappa.
By 5.2, there exists ϕ∈Diff0​(M)\phi\in\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M) such that ϕ∗​I\phi^{*}I is compatible with ω\omega.
Then ϕ∗​ηκ,θ~\phi^{*}\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}} and ω\omega are cohomologous symplectic forms compatible with ϕ∗​I\phi^{*}I. The linear family of 2-forms
connecting ϕ∗​ηκ,θ~\phi^{*}\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}} and ω\omega is then a family of cohomologous symplectic forms.
Hence, by Moser’s theorem [Mos], ϕ∗​ηκ,θ~\phi^{*}\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}} lies in the Diff0​(M)\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M)-orbit of ω\omega, and consequently so does ηκ,θ~\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}}.
Thus, for any sufficiently small κ>0\kappa>0 we have
(ηκ,θ~,h∘Rκ)∈𝑃𝑎𝑖𝑟𝑠𝒞0.(\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}},h\circ R_{\kappa})\in{\it Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}.
Define the map
Φ:𝚃𝚛𝚒𝚙𝚕𝚎𝚜𝒞0→𝙿𝚊𝚒𝚛𝚜𝒞0\Phi:{\tt Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}\to{\tt Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}
by
Φ⁡({I,𝕩,h}):={ηκ,θ~,h∘Rκ}.\Phi\left(\left\{I,{\mathbbm{x}},h\right\}\right):=\left\{\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}},h\circ R_{\kappa}\right\}.
We need to show that the map Φ\Phi is well-defined and has the required properties.
Φ\Phi is well-defined:
Let us first show that {ηκ,θ~,h∘Rκ}\left\{\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}},h\circ R_{\kappa}\right\}
does not depend on the choice of a sufficiently small κ>0\kappa>0 (for fixed θ~{\widetilde{\theta}}). Given such 0<κ1<κ20<\kappa_{1}<\kappa_{2},
we have a smooth family of pairs (ηκ,θ~,h∘Rκ)(\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}},h\circ R_{\kappa}), κ∈[κ1,κ2]\kappa\in[\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}], lying in 𝑃𝑎𝑖𝑟𝑠𝒞0{\it Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}. By 5.1, this family lies in the same
Diff0​(M)\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M)-orbit on 𝑃𝑎𝑖𝑟𝑠𝒞0{\it Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}. Thus, {ηκ,θ~,h∘Rκ}\left\{\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}},h\circ R_{\kappa}\right\} does not depend on
the choice of κ\kappa.
Let us verify that {ηκ,θ~,h∘Rκ}\left\{\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}},h\circ R_{\kappa}\right\}
does not depend on the choice of θ~{\widetilde{\theta}}. Assume that θ~′{\widetilde{\theta}}^{\prime} is another symplectic form
which is compatible with I~{\widetilde{I}}, represents α~r¯{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bar{r}}, and equals
ρxi​(1,ri)\rho_{x_{i}}(1,r_{i}) on a neighborhood of EiE_{i} for each i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k.
Pick a sufficiently small κ>0\kappa>0 so that θ~=θ~′=ρxi​(ci,ri){\widetilde{\theta}}={\widetilde{\theta}}^{\prime}=\rho_{x_{i}}(c_{i},r_{i}) on Π−1​(hi​(B2​n​(κ⁡(ri+ϵ))))\Pi^{-1}\left(h_{i}\left(B^{2n}\left(\kappa\left(r_{i}+\epsilon\right)\right)\right)\right).
Then ηκ,θ~\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}} and ηκ,θ~′\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}^{\prime}} are cohomologous symplectic forms compatible with ω\omega and
coinciding on hi​(B2​n​(κ⁡(ri+ϵ)))h_{i}\left(B^{2n}\left(\kappa\left(r_{i}+\epsilon\right)\right)\right). By a relative version of Moser’s theorem (see 13.3, part I), there exists ϕ∈Diff0​(M)\phi\in\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M) that fixes hi​(B2​n​(κ⁡(ri+ϵ)))h_{i}\left(B^{2n}\left(\kappa\left(r_{i}+\epsilon\right)\right)\right) pointwise (thus mapping h∘Rκh\circ R_{\kappa} to itself) and maps ηκ,θ~\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}} to
ηκ,θ~′\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}^{\prime}}.
Thus, {ηκ,θ~,h∘Rκ}\left\{\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}},h\circ R_{\kappa}\right\} does not depend
on the choice of θ~{\widetilde{\theta}}.
Finally, a direct check shows that Φ⁡({I,𝕩,h})\Phi\left(\left\{I,{\mathbbm{x}},h\right\}\right) does not depend on a representative
(I,𝕩,h)(I,{\mathbbm{x}},h) of {I,𝕩,h}∈𝚃𝚛𝚒𝚙𝚕𝚎𝚜𝒞0\left\{I,{\mathbbm{x}},h\right\}\in{\tt Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}: acting by ϕ∈Diff0​(M)\phi\in\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M) on
(I,𝕩,h)(I,{\mathbbm{x}},h) and applying the previous construction to the resulting element of 𝑇𝑟𝑖𝑝𝑙𝑒𝑠𝒞0{\it Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} we get a pair in 𝑃𝑎𝑖𝑟𝑠𝒞0{\it Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} obtained from (ηκ,θ~,h∘Rκ)\left(\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}},h\circ R_{\kappa}\right)
by the action of the same ϕ\phi. (Here we identify the complex blow-ups of (M,I)(M,I) at x1,…,xkx_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
and of (M,ϕ∗​I)(M,\phi_{*}I) at ϕ⁡(x1),…,ϕ⁡(xk)\phi(x_{1}),\ldots,\phi(x_{k}) by the diffeomorphism induced by ϕ\phi.)
Thus, we have shown that Φ\Phi is well-defined.
Φ\Phi is continuous:
By 5.1, the space 𝙿𝚊𝚒𝚛𝚜𝒞0{\tt Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} is discrete. Thus, showing that
Φ:𝚃𝚛𝚒𝚙𝚕𝚎𝚜𝒞0→𝙿𝚊𝚒𝚛𝚜𝒞0\Phi:{\tt Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}\to{\tt Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} is continuous is equivalent to showing that the preimage of a point in 𝙿𝚊𝚒𝚛𝚜𝒞0{\tt Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}
under Φ\Phi is open in 𝚃𝚛𝚒𝚙𝚕𝚎𝚜𝒞0{\tt Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}, or, in other words, that for any (I′,𝕩′,h′)∈𝑇𝑟𝑖𝑝𝑙𝑒𝑠𝒞0(I^{\prime},{\mathbbm{x}}^{\prime},h^{\prime})\in{\it Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} sufficiently close to
(I,𝕩,h)(I,{\mathbbm{x}},h) its Diff0​(M)\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M)-orbit {(I′,𝕩′,h′)}∈𝚃𝚛𝚒𝚙𝚕𝚎𝚜𝒞0\{(I^{\prime},{\mathbbm{x}}^{\prime},h^{\prime})\}\in{\tt Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} is mapped by Φ\Phi to the same point in 𝙿𝚊𝚒𝚛𝚜𝒞0{\tt Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} as {(I,𝕩,h)}\{(I,{\mathbbm{x}},h)\}.
Given (I′,𝕩′,h′)∈𝑇𝑟𝑖𝑝𝑙𝑒𝑠𝒞0(I^{\prime},{\mathbbm{x}}^{\prime},h^{\prime})\in{\it Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}, one easily constructs ϕ∈Diff0​(M)\phi\in\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M) so that
•
ϕ\phi sends 𝕩′{\mathbbm{x}}^{\prime} into 𝕩{\mathbbm{x}},
•
ϕ∘h′\phi\circ h^{\prime} coincides with hh near each xix_{i}, i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k.
If hh and h′h^{\prime}, and 𝕩{\mathbbm{x}} and 𝕩′{\mathbbm{x}}^{\prime}, are sufficiently C∞C^{\infty}-close, then ϕ\phi can be chosen to be close to the identity and therefore it will send
(I′,𝕩′,h′)(I^{\prime},{\mathbbm{x}}^{\prime},h^{\prime}) into a triple close to (I,𝕩,h)(I,{\mathbbm{x}},h). Thus, without loss of generality we may replace
(I′,𝕩′,h′)(I^{\prime},{\mathbbm{x}}^{\prime},h^{\prime}) by a triple (I′,𝕩,h′)(I^{\prime},{\mathbbm{x}},h^{\prime}) that is close to (I,𝕩,h)(I,{\mathbbm{x}},h) and satisfies h∘Rκ=h′∘Rκh\circ R_{\kappa}=h^{\prime}\circ R_{\kappa}
for any sufficiently small κ>0\kappa>0. In particular, for any such κ\kappa the complex structures II and I′I^{\prime} coincide on each hi​(B2​n​(κ⁡(ri+ϵ)))h_{i}\left(B^{2n}\left(\kappa\left(r_{i}+\epsilon\right)\right)\right), i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k.
Arguing as above, we can construct a symplectic form θ~{\widetilde{\theta}} on M~I,𝕩=M~I′,𝕩{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}}={\widetilde{M}}_{I^{\prime},{\mathbbm{x}}} which is compatible with I~′{\widetilde{I}}^{\prime}, represents α~r¯{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bar{r}}, and for any sufficiently small κ>0\kappa>0 equals to
ρxi​(1,ri)\rho_{x_{i}}(1,r_{i}) on the neighborhood Π−1​(hi​(B2​n​(κ⁡(ri+ϵ))))\Pi^{-1}\left(h_{i}\left(B^{2n}\left(\kappa\left(r_{i}+\epsilon\right)\right)\right)\right) of EiE_{i} for each i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k.
Consequently,
for any sufficiently small κ>0\kappa>0 we have a well-defined symplectic form ηκ,θ~′\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}^{\prime}} on MM.
This form is compatible with I′I^{\prime}, represents the class α\alpha and coincides with ηκ,θ~\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}}
on each hi​(B2​n​(κ⁡(ri+ϵ)))h_{i}\left(B^{2n}\left(\kappa\left(r_{i}+\epsilon\right)\right)\right), i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k.
Assume that I′I^{\prime} is sufficiently close to II so that it is tamed by the form ηκ,θ~\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}} (which is compatible with II).
Then ηκ,θ~\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}} and ηκ,θ~′\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}^{\prime}} are cohomologous symplectic forms on MM that both tame I′I^{\prime} and coincide
on each hi​(B2​n​(κ⁡(ri+ϵ)))h_{i}\left(B^{2n}\left(\kappa\left(r_{i}+\epsilon\right)\right)\right), i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k. A relative version of
Moser’s theorem (see 13.3, part I) implies then that there exists φ∈Diff0​(M)\varphi\in\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M) that maps ηκ,θ~′\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}^{\prime}}
into ηκ,θ~\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}} and
fixes pointwise
each hi​(B2​n​(κ⁡(ri+ϵ)))h_{i}\left(B^{2n}\left(\kappa\left(r_{i}+\epsilon\right)\right)\right), i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k. Hence, φ\varphi
maps (ηκ,θ~′,h′∘Rκ)∈𝑃𝑎𝑖𝑟𝑠𝒞0\left(\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}^{\prime}},h^{\prime}\circ R_{\kappa}\right)\in{\it Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} into
(ηκ,θ~,h∘Rκ)\left(\eta_{\kappa,{\widetilde{\theta}}},h\circ R_{\kappa}\right).
Thus, for any (I′,𝕩′,h′)∈𝑇𝑟𝑖𝑝𝑙𝑒𝑠𝒞0(I^{\prime},{\mathbbm{x}}^{\prime},h^{\prime})\in{\it Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} sufficiently close to
(I,𝕩,h)(I,{\mathbbm{x}},h), its Diff0​(M)\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M)-orbit
{I′,𝕩′,h′}∈𝚃𝚛𝚒𝚙𝚕𝚎𝚜𝒞0\{I^{\prime},{\mathbbm{x}}^{\prime},h^{\prime}\}\in{\tt Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} is mapped by Φ\Phi to the same point in 𝙿𝚊𝚒𝚛𝚜𝒞0{\tt Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} as {I,𝕩,h}∈𝚃𝚛𝚒𝚙𝚕𝚎𝚜𝒞0\{I,{\mathbbm{x}},h\}\in{\tt Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}. This finishes the proof of the claim that Φ\Phi is continuous.
Φ\Phi is surjective:
The proof uses the well-known fact (see e.g. [McDP]) that the symplectic blow-down is an “inverse” of the symplectic blow-up, up to an isotopy.
Namely, let
(η,f)∈𝑃𝑎𝑖𝑟𝑠𝒞0(\eta,f)\in{\it Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}},
where f=⨆i=1kfi:⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→(M,η)f=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}:\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to(M,\eta) is a symplectic embedding holomorphic with respect to
a complex structure II on MM compatible with η\eta. Let us show that {η,f}∈𝙿𝚊𝚒𝚛𝚜𝒞0\{\eta,f\}\in{\tt Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} lies in the image of Φ\Phi.
Set
xi:=fi(0),i=1,…,k,x_{i}:=f_{i}(0),i=1,\ldots,k,
𝕩:=(x1,…,xk).{\mathbbm{x}}:=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}).
By 5.5,
the cohomology class α~r¯∈H2​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ){\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bar{r}}\in H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}}) is Kähler with respect to I~{\widetilde{I}}.
Thus, by 5.2, (I,𝕩,f)∈𝑇𝑟𝑖𝑝𝑙𝑒𝑠𝒞0(I,{\mathbbm{x}},f)\in{\it Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}.
Moreover,
by 5.5, the class α~r¯{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bar{r}}
can be represented, for a sufficiently small c>0c>0, by a symplectic form θ~c{\widetilde{\theta}}_{c} on M~I,𝕩{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}} with the following properties:
•
θ~c=ρxi​(c,ri){\widetilde{\theta}}_{c}=\rho_{x_{i}}(c,r_{i}) on Π−1​(fi​(B⁡(ri)))\Pi^{-1}\left(f_{i}\left(B(r_{i})\right)\right), i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k,
•
[θ~c]=Π∗​[η]−π​∑i=1kri2​ei[{\widetilde{\theta}}_{c}]=\Pi^{*}[\eta]-\pi\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}^{2}e_{i},
•
θ~c{\widetilde{\theta}}_{c} is compatible with I~{\widetilde{I}}.
Consequently, 5.5 implies that
for κ=1\kappa=1 there exists a symplectic form η1​(θ~c)\eta_{1}({\widetilde{\theta}}_{c}) on MM with the
following properties:
•
[η1​(θ~c)]=α[\eta_{1}({\widetilde{\theta}}_{c})]=\alpha,
•
η1​(θ~c)\eta_{1}({\widetilde{\theta}}_{c}) is compatible with II,
•
The f∘R1=f:⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→Mf\circ R_{1}=f:\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to M is symplectic with respect to η1​(θ~c)\eta_{1}({\widetilde{\theta}}_{c}) (and holomorphic with respect to II).
This yields that
1. α~r¯∈H2​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ){\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bar{r}}\in H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}}) is Kähler with respect to I~{\widetilde{I}}.
Thus, by 5.2, (I,𝕩,f)∈𝑇𝑟𝑖𝑝𝑙𝑒𝑠𝒞0(I,{\mathbbm{x}},f)\in{\it Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}.
2. η1​(θ~c)\eta_{1}({\widetilde{\theta}}_{c}) coincides with η\eta on each fi​(B2​n​(κ​ri))f_{i}\left(B^{2n}\left(\kappa r_{i}\right)\right), i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k.
A relative version of
Moser’s theorem (see 13.3, part I) implies then that there exists ψ∈Diff0​(M)\psi\in\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M) that maps η1​(θ~c)\eta_{1}({\widetilde{\theta}}_{c})
into η\eta and
fixes pointwise each
each fi​(B2​n​(κ​ri))f_{i}\left(B^{2n}\left(\kappa r_{i}\right)\right), i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k. Hence,
ψ⁡(η1​(θ~c),f)=(η,f).\psi\left(\eta_{1}\left({\widetilde{\theta}}_{c}\right),f\right)=(\eta,f).
Thus,
Φ⁡({I,𝕩,f})={η1​(θ~c),f}={η,f}∈𝙿𝚊𝚒𝚛𝚜𝒞0.\Phi\left(\left\{I,{\mathbbm{x}},f\right\}\right)=\left\{\eta_{1}\left({\widetilde{\theta}}_{c}\right),f\right\}=\left\{\eta,f\right\}\in{\tt Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}.
This shows that {η,f}∈𝙿𝚊𝚒𝚛𝚜𝒞0\{\eta,f\}\in{\tt Pairs}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} lies in the image of Φ\Phi. Hence, Φ\Phi is surjective.
We have shown that Φ\Phi is well-defined, continuous and surjective. By construction, it clearly satisfies the
properties 1 and 2 listed in the statement of
5.2.
This finishes the proof of 5.2.
The tools developed above allow us also to prove the following existence result for tame embeddings of balls, which is an
analogue of part (I) of 3.1.
Proposition 5.17
The set ⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i}) admits a tame embedding into (M,ω)(M,\omega) if and only if
for some I∈𝐶𝐾⁡(M,ω)I\in\mathit{CK}(M,\omega), tamed by ω\omega, and some 𝕩∈M^k{\mathbbm{x}}\in{\hat{M}}^{k}, the class Π∗​[ω]−π​∑i=1kri2​ei∈H2​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ)\Pi^{*}[\omega]-\pi\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}^{2}e_{i}\in H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}}), can be represented by a form taming I~{\widetilde{I}} and equal to ρxi​(ci,ri)\rho_{x_{i}}(c_{i},r_{i}), for some ci>0c_{i}>0, near each EiE_{i},
i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k.
More precisely, given I∈𝐶𝐾⁡(M,ω)I\in\mathit{CK}(M,\omega), tamed by ω\omega, the following conditions are equivalent:
•
There exists 𝕩=(x1,…,xk)∈M^k{\mathbbm{x}}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\in{\hat{M}}^{k} so that the cohomology class Π∗​[ω]−π​∑i=1kri2​ei∈H2​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ)\Pi^{*}[\omega]-\pi\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}^{2}e_{i}\in H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}}) can be represented by a form taming I~{\widetilde{I}} and equal to ρxi​(ci,ri)\rho_{x_{i}}(c_{i},r_{i}), for some ci>0c_{i}>0, near each EiE_{i},
i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k.
•
There exist a tame embedding f:⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→(M,ω)f:\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to(M,\omega)
