and ϕ∈Diff0​(M)\phi\in\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M), so that ff is holomorphic with respect to the complex structure ϕ∗​I\phi^{*}I on MM tamed by ω\omega.
Proof of 5.5:
Assume that for some I∈𝐶𝐾⁡(M,ω)I\in\mathit{CK}(M,\omega), tamed by ω\omega, and some 𝕩∈M^k{\mathbbm{x}}\in{\hat{M}}^{k} the cohomology class
Π∗​[ω]−π​∑i=1kri2​ei∈H2​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ)\Pi^{*}[\omega]-\pi\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}^{2}e_{i}\in H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}}) can be represented by a form taming I~{\widetilde{I}} and equal to ρxi​(ci,ri)\rho_{x_{i}}(c_{i},r_{i}), for some ci>0c_{i}>0, near each Ei=Π−1​(xi)E_{i}=\Pi^{-1}(x_{i}),
i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k. Then one can deduce from 5.5
that there exist a symplectic form η\eta on MM, cohomologous to ω\omega and taming II, and an embedding g:⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→Mg:\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to M symplectic with respect to η\eta and holomorphic with respect to II.
The linear family of closed 2-forms connecting ω\omega and η\eta is formed by cohomologous forms each of which tames II
and is therefore symplectic. Therefore, by Moser’s theorem [Mos], ω=ϕ∗​η\omega=\phi^{*}\eta for some ϕ∈Diff0​(M)\phi\in\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M).
The embedding f:=ϕ−1∘g:⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→(M,ω)f:=\phi^{-1}\circ g:\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to(M,\omega) is symplectic with respect to ω\omega
and holomorphic with respect to the complex structure ϕ∗​I\phi^{*}I on MM tamed by ω\omega, meaning that ff is tame.
This proves one of the implications in the required equivalence.
Let us now prove the opposite implication.
Assume that f=⨆i=1kfi:⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→(M,ω)f=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}:\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to(M,\omega) is a symplectic embedding
holomorphic with respect to the complex structure J:=ϕ∗​IJ:=\phi^{*}I, ϕ∈Diff0​(M)\phi\in\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M), tamed by ω\omega.
Set yi:=fi​(0)y_{i}:=f_{i}(0), i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k, and 𝕪:=(y1,…,yk){\mathbbm{y}}:=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k}).
By 5.5,
there exists a symplectic form θ~{\widetilde{\theta}} on M~J,𝕪{\widetilde{M}}_{J,{\mathbbm{y}}} with the following properties:
1. θ~=ρyi​(c,ri){\widetilde{\theta}}=\rho_{y_{i}}(c,r_{i}), for a sufficiently small c>0c>0,
on a neighborhood of the exceptional divisor Π−1​(yi)⊂M~J,𝕪\Pi^{-1}(y_{i})\subset{\widetilde{M}}_{J,{\mathbbm{y}}}, i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k,
2. θ~{\widetilde{\theta}} tames J~{\widetilde{J}},
3. [θ~]=Π∗​[ω]−π​∑i=1kri2​ei∈H2​(M~J,𝕪,ℝ)[{\widetilde{\theta}}]=\Pi^{*}[\omega]-\pi\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}^{2}e_{i}\in H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{J,{\mathbbm{y}}};{\mathbb{R}}).
Set xi:=ϕ⁡(yi)x_{i}:=\phi(y_{i}), i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k, and 𝕩:=(x1,…,xk){\mathbbm{x}}:=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}).
The diffeomorphism ϕ:M→M\phi:M\to M induces a diffeomorphism M~J,𝕪→M~I,𝕩{\widetilde{M}}_{J,{\mathbbm{y}}}\to{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}}
identifying the form θ~{\widetilde{\theta}} on M~J,𝕪{\widetilde{M}}_{J,{\mathbbm{y}}} with a form on M~I,𝕩{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}}
taming I~{\widetilde{I}}, equal to ρxi​(c,ri)\rho_{x_{i}}(c,r_{i}) near each Ei=Π−1​(xi)⊂M~I,𝕩E_{i}=\Pi^{-1}(x_{i})\subset{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}},
i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k, and lying in the cohomology class Π∗​[ω]−π​∑i=1kri2​ei∈H2​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ)\Pi^{*}[\omega]-\pi\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}^{2}e_{i}\in H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}}).
This finishes the proof of the opposite implication and of the proposition.
5.6 Connectedness of 𝚃𝚛𝚒𝚙𝚕𝚎𝚜𝒞0{\tt Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}
Let II be a Kähler-type complex structure on MM. Equip M^k{\hat{M}}^{k} and M^k×M{\hat{M}}^{k}\times M with the product complex structures induced by II.
Denote by Ξ⊂M^k×M\Xi\subset{\hat{M}}^{k}\times M
the following incidence variety:
Ξ:={((x1,…,xk),x)∈M^k×M|x=xifor somei=1,…,k}.\Xi:=\left\{\ \left(\left(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\right),x\right)\in{\hat{M}}^{k}\times M\ \big|\ x=x_{i}\ \textrm{for some}\ i=1,\ldots,k\ \right\}.
It is a smooth complex submanifold of M^k×M{\hat{M}}^{k}\times M. Denote by ℳ{\mathcal{M}} the complex blow-up of M^k×M{\hat{M}}^{k}\times M along Ξ\Xi. It is a complex manifold that projects holomorphically to M^k×M{\hat{M}}^{k}\times M and consequently to M^k{\hat{M}}^{k}.
Denote the latter projection by π:ℳ→M^k\pi:{\mathcal{M}}\to{\hat{M}}^{k}. This projection defines a complex analytic family of
complex manifolds equipped with Kähler-type complex structures.
The fiber of π\pi over each 𝕩=(x1,…,xk)∈M^k{\mathbbm{x}}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\in{\hat{M}}^{k}
can be canonically identified with the complex blow-up M~I,𝕩{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}} of (M,I)(M,I) at x1,…,xkx_{1},\ldots,x_{k}. The holonomy of the Gauss-Manin connection on π\pi is trivial and the homology/cohomology groups of all the fibers of π\pi are then identified with the homology/cohomology groups of the complex blow-up of MM at kk distinct ordered points (the identification does not depend on the choice of the points, since the homology/cohomology groups of the complex blow-ups for different choices can be canonically identified). The identifications preserve the Hodge decompositions and, in particular, cohomology classes of type (1,1)(1,1) of one fiber are identified with the (1,1)(1,1)-classes of another fiber.
Proposition 5.18
Assume that β\beta is a (1,1)(1,1)-cohomology class of the complex blow-up of (M,I)(M,I) at kk points.
Denote by ℬβ⊂M^k{\mathcal{B}}_{\beta}\subset{\hat{M}}^{k} the set of all 𝕩∈M^k{\mathbbm{x}}\in{\hat{M}}^{k}
such that β∈HI~1,1​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ)\beta\in H_{\widetilde{I}}^{1,1}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}}) is Kähler.
Then ℬβ{\mathcal{B}}_{\beta} is connected.
Proof of 5.6:
If ℬβ{\mathcal{B}}_{\beta} is empty, the claim is trivial. Assume it is non-empty. Since β∈HI~1,1​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ)\beta\in H_{\widetilde{I}}^{1,1}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}}) for all 𝕩∈M^k{\mathbbm{x}}\in{\hat{M}}^{k}, the Kodaira-Spencer stability theorem implies (see 13.2) that
the set ℬβ{\mathcal{B}}_{\beta} is open.
A theorem of Demailly-Paun [DP, Thm. 0.9] about the Kähler cones of the fibers of a complex analytic deformation family implies in our situation that there exists a countable union ⋃νSν\bigcup_{\nu}S_{\nu} of proper
analytic subsets Sν⫋M^kS_{\nu}\subsetneqq{\hat{M}}^{k} such that the Kähler cones
of the fibers π−1​(𝕩)\pi^{-1}({\mathbbm{x}}) coincide for all 𝕩∈M^k∖⋃νSν{\mathbbm{x}}\in{\hat{M}}^{k}\setminus\bigcup_{\nu}S_{\nu} under our identifications of the cohomologies of the fibers.
Since the set ℬβ⊂M^k{\mathcal{B}}_{\beta}\subset{\hat{M}}^{k} is open, it intersects M^k∖⋃νSν{\hat{M}}^{k}\setminus\bigcup_{\nu}S_{\nu}.
Therefore β∈HI~1,1​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ)\beta\in H_{\widetilde{I}}^{1,1}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}}) is Kähler for all 𝕩∈M^k∖⋃νSν{\mathbbm{x}}\in{\hat{M}}^{k}\setminus\bigcup_{\nu}S_{\nu},
meaning that M^k∖⋃νSν⊂ℬβ{\hat{M}}^{k}\setminus\bigcup_{\nu}S_{\nu}\subset{\mathcal{B}}_{\beta}. Being the complement of a countable union of proper analytic subsets of a connected complex manifold, the set M^k∖⋃νSν{\hat{M}}^{k}\setminus\bigcup_{\nu}S_{\nu} is connected and dense in M^k{\hat{M}}^{k}. Hence,
the set ℬβ{\mathcal{B}}_{\beta} is connected too.
Corollary 5.19
The fibers of the projection pr:𝒦⁡(r¯)→𝐶𝐾⁡(M){\textit{pr}\,}:{\mathcal{K}}({\bar{r}})\to\mathit{CK}(M) are connected.
Proof of 5.6:
For each I∈Diff0​(M,ω)⋅𝐶𝑚𝑝𝑡⁡(M,ω)I\in\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M,\omega)\cdot{\it Cmpt}(M,\omega) and each 𝕩∈M^k{\mathbbm{x}}\in{\hat{M}}^{k} the cohomology class
α~r¯=Π∗​[ω]−π​∑i=1kri2​ei∈H2​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ){\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bar{r}}=\Pi^{*}[\omega]-\pi\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}^{2}e_{i}\in H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}})
is of type (1,1)(1,1). The image of pr lies in Diff0​(M,ω)⋅𝐶𝑚𝑝𝑡⁡(M,ω)\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M,\omega)\cdot{\it Cmpt}(M,\omega) and
the fiber of pr over I∈Im​prI\in{\rm{Im}\,}{\textit{pr}\,} is the set of all 𝕩∈M^k{\mathbbm{x}}\in{\hat{M}}^{k}
such that the (1,1)(1,1)-class α~r¯{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bar{r}} is Kähler.
By 5.6, applied to β=α~r¯\beta={\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bar{r}}, this fiber is connected.
Proposition 5.20
Assume II is a complex structure on MM, x∈Mx\in M, r>0r>0.
Then the space of holomorphic embeddings h:B2​n​(r)→(M,I)h:B^{2n}(r)\to(M,I), such that h⁡(0)=xh(0)=x, is path-connected.
Proof of 5.6:
Assume h:B2​n​(r)→(M,I)h:B^{2n}(r)\to(M,I), h⁡(0)=xh(0)=x, is a holomorphic embedding. Let g:B2​n​(r)→(M,I)g:B^{2n}(r)\to(M,I), g⁡(0)=xg(0)=x, be another holomorphic
embedding. We want to show that gg can be connected to hh by a smooth path of holomorphic embeddings B2​n​(r)→(M,I)B^{2n}(r)\to(M,I) mapping 00 to xx.
We can assume without loss of generality that Im​g⊂Im​h{\rm{Im}\,}g\subset{\rm{Im}\,}h. Indeed,
for a sufficiently small κ>0\kappa>0 we have Im⁡(g∘Rκ)⊂Im​h{\rm{Im}\,}(g\circ R_{\kappa})\subset{\rm{Im}\,}h and g=g∘R1g=g\circ R_{1} can be
connected to g∘Rκg\circ R_{\kappa} by the family {g∘Rt}t∈[κ,1]\{g\circ R_{t}\}_{t\in[\kappa,1]}.
Assuming Im​g⊂Im​h{\rm{Im}\,}g\subset{\rm{Im}\,}h, in order to connect gg to hh by a smooth path of holomorphic embeddings B2​n​(r)→(M,I)B^{2n}(r)\to(M,I) mapping 00 to xx it suffices to show that the holomorphic embedding
f:=h−1∘g:B2​n​(r)→B2​n​(r)f:=h^{-1}\circ g:B^{2n}(r)\to B^{2n}(r), h−1∘g⁡(0)=0h^{-1}\circ g(0)=0, can be connected to the identity by a smooth path of holomorphic embeddings B2​n​(r)→B2​n​(r)B^{2n}(r)\to B^{2n}(r) mapping 00 to 00.
The latter result is standard and is proved by a version of the Alexander trick – see 13.4, part II.
This finishes the proof of the proposition.
Proof of 5.2:
The space 𝚃𝚛𝚒𝚙𝚕𝚎𝚜𝒞0=𝑇𝑟𝑖𝑝𝑙𝑒𝑠𝒞0/Diff0​(M){\tt Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}={\it Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}/\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M) is connected if only if the space
𝑇𝑟𝑖𝑝𝑙𝑒𝑠𝒞0{\it Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} is connected.
The projection (I,𝕩,h)↦(I,𝕩)(I,{\mathbbm{x}},h)\mapsto(I,{\mathbbm{x}}) defines a surjective continuous map 𝑇𝑟𝑖𝑝𝑙𝑒𝑠𝒞0→𝒦𝒞0​(r¯){\it Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}\to{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}({\bar{r}}).
In view of 5.6, the fibers of this map are connected.
Hence, 𝑇𝑟𝑖𝑝𝑙𝑒𝑠𝒞0{\it Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} is connected if and only 𝒦𝒞0​(r¯){\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}({\bar{r}})
is connected.
By 5.6, the fibers of the projection pr:𝒦𝒞0​(r¯)→𝒞0{\textit{pr}\,}:{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}({\bar{r}})\to{\mathcal{C}}_{0} are connected. Thus,
𝒦𝒞0​(r¯){\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}({\bar{r}}) is connected if and only if pr​(𝒦𝒞0​(r¯)){\textit{pr}\,}({\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}({\bar{r}}))
is connected.
The set pr​(𝒦𝒞0​(r¯)){\textit{pr}\,}({\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}({\bar{r}})) is connected if and only if the set 𝙺𝒞0​(r¯)=pr​(𝒦𝒞0​(r¯)/Diff0​(M)CLOSE{\tt{K}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}({\bar{r}})={\textit{pr}\,}({\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}({\bar{r}})/\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M) is connected.
Summing up, we see that 𝚃𝚛𝚒𝚙𝚕𝚎𝚜𝒞0{\tt Triples}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}} is connected if and only if 𝙺𝒞0​(r¯){\tt{K}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}({\bar{r}}) is connected.
This finishes the proof of the proposition.
6 The case of complex projective spaces and their products
As above, we denote by Is​tI_{st} the standard complex structure on ℂ​Pn{\mathbb{C}}P^{n} and by ωF​S\omega_{FS}, or by ωF​S,n\omega_{FS,n}, the standard Fubini-Study symplectic form on ℂ​Pn{\mathbb{C}}P^{n} normalized so that ∫ℂ​P1ωF​S=π\int_{{\mathbb{C}}P^{1}}\omega_{FS}=\pi. Let h∈H2​(ℂ​Pn,ℤ)h\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n};{\mathbb{Z}}) be the generator of H2​(ℂ​Pn,ℤ)H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n};{\mathbb{Z}}) so that [ωF​S]=π​h[\omega_{FS}]=\pi h.
Let us prove 3.2. For convenience, we restate it here.
Theorem 6.1 (= 3.2)
Consider the manifold M:=ℂ​Pn1×…×ℂ​PnmM:={\mathbb{C}}P^{n_{1}}\times\ldots\times{\mathbb{C}}P^{n_{m}}, n1,…,nm>0n_{1},\ldots,n_{m}>0, n1+…+nm=:nn_{1}+\ldots+n_{m}=:n, endowed with the symplectic form ω𝕔:=c1​ωF​S,n1⊕…⊕cm​ωF​S,nm\omega_{\mathbbm{c}}:=c_{1}\omega_{FS,n_{1}}\oplus\ldots\oplus c_{m}\omega_{FS,n_{m}}, 𝕔:=(c1,…,cm)∈(ℝ>0)m{\mathbbm{c}}:=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{m})\in({\mathbb{R}}_{>0})^{m}. Let II be the complex
structure on MM which is the product of the standard complex structures on the factors.
Let l1,…,lm∈ℤ>0l_{1},\ldots,l_{m}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0} so that [l1:…:lm]=[c1:…:cm]∈ℝPm[l_{1}:\ldots:l_{m}]=[c_{1}:\ldots:c_{m}]\in{\mathbb{R}}P^{m}.
Let
k:=(n1+…​nm)!n1!⋅…⋅nm!​l1n1⋅…⋅lmnm.k:=\frac{(n_{1}+\ldots n_{m})!}{n_{1}!\cdot\ldots\cdot n_{m}!}l_{1}^{n_{1}}\cdot\ldots\cdot l_{m}^{n_{m}}.
Let Σ⊂(M,I)\Sigma\subset(M,I) be a proper (possibly empty) complex submanifold. If M=ℂ​Pn1×ℂ​P1M={\mathbb{C}}P^{n_{1}}\times{\mathbb{C}}P^{1}, we also allow
Σ=(ℂ​Pn1−1×ℂ​P1)∪(ℂ​Pn1×pt)\Sigma=({\mathbb{C}}P^{n_{1}-1}\times{\mathbb{C}}P^{1})\cup({\mathbb{C}}P^{n_{1}}\times\textrm{pt}).
Then Kähler-type embeddings of disjoint unions of kk equal balls into (M∖Σ,ω𝕔)(M\setminus\Sigma,\omega_{\mathbbm{c}})
are unobstructed.
More precisely, if k​Vol​(B2​n​(r),ω0)<Vol⁡(M,ω)k{\rm{Vol}\,}(B^{2n}(r),\omega_{0})<{\rm{Vol}\,}(M,\omega), then for each proper (possibly empty) complex submanifold Σ⊂(M,I)\Sigma\subset(M,I), there exists an [I][I]-Kähler-type embedding of ⨆i=1kB2​n​(r)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r)
into (M,ω)(M,\omega) which is holomorphic with respect to a complex structure on MM that is compatible with ω\omega
and isotopic to II by an isotopy preserving Σ\Sigma (as a set).
Proof of 6 (= 3.2):
The proof mimics the proof of [McDP, Thm. 1.5.A].
Namely, as it is shown in [McDP, Pf. of Thm. 1.5.A], there exist x1,…,xk∈Mx_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in M, 𝕩:=(x1,…,xk)∈M^k{\mathbbm{x}}:=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\in{\hat{M}}^{k}, and a holomorphic map F:M~I,𝕩→(ℂ​Pn−1,Is​t)F:{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}}\to({\mathbb{C}}P^{n-1},I_{st}) so that
•
FF induces a biholomorphism on each exceptional divisor,
•
Π∗​[ω]−F∗​[ωF​S,n−1]∈H2​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ)\Pi^{*}[\omega]-F^{*}[\omega_{FS,n-1}]\in H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}}) is a linear combination of the cohomology classes e1,…,eke_{1},\ldots,e_{k} Poincaré-dual to the fundamental homology classes of the exceptional divisors of M~I,𝕩{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}}. Here Π:M~I,𝕩→M\Pi:{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}}\to M is the blow-up projection, as before.
Let c>0c>0. A direct check using the above-mentioned properties of FF
shows that the form ω~c:=(1+c)−1​(Π∗​ω+c​F∗​ωF​S,n−1){\widetilde{\omega}}_{c}:=(1+c)^{-1}(\Pi^{*}\omega+cF^{*}\omega_{FS,n-1})
is a symplectic form on M~I,𝕩{\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}} compatible with I~{\widetilde{I}} and representing the cohomology class
Π∗​[ω]−π​∑i=1k(1+c)−1​c​ei∈H2​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ)\Pi^{*}[\omega]-\pi\sum_{i=1}^{k}(1+c)^{-1}ce_{i}\in H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}}).
Therefore, by part (I) of 3.1 (=5.2), for rc:=(1+c)−1​cr_{c}:=\sqrt{(1+c)^{-1}c},
there exists a symplectic embedding ⋃i=1kB⁡(rc)→(M∖Σ,ω)\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}B(r_{c})\to(M\setminus\Sigma,\omega)
and an isotopy {ϕt}0≤t≤1⊂Diff0​(M)\{\phi_{t}\}_{0\leq t\leq 1}\subset\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M), ϕ0=I​d\phi_{0}=Id, ϕt​(Σ)=Σ\phi_{t}(\Sigma)=\Sigma for all t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1], so that ff is holomorphic with respect to the complex structure ϕ1∗​I\phi_{1}^{*}I which is compatible with ω\omega.
As it is shown in [McDP, Pf. of Thm. 1.5.A], as c→+∞c\to+\infty, the symplectic volume
of ⋃i=1kB⁡(rc)\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}B(r_{c}) tends from below to the symplectic volume of (M,ω)(M,\omega).
This readily yields the claim of the theorem.
Let us know consider the case M=ℂ​PnM={\mathbb{C}}P^{n}.
The following result is classical – see 6 for the references.
Theorem 6.2
(I) Any Kähler-type complex structure on ℂ​Pn{\mathbb{C}}P^{n} can be identified with Is​tI_{st} by a diffeomorphism lying in DiffH​(ℂ​Pn)\mathrm{Diff}_{H}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n}).
(II) Any two Kähler-type complex structures on ℂ​Pn{\mathbb{C}}P^{n} lying in the same component of 𝐶𝐾⁡(ℂ​Pn)\mathit{CK}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n}) can be
mapped into each other by an element of Diff0​(ℂ​Pn)\mathrm{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n}).
Remark 6.3
1. Part (I) of 6 is due to Hirzebruch and Kodaira [HiK] for odd nn and to Yau [Yau1] for even nn – see [Tos] for an exposition and a proof. (The result in [Tos] does not state explicitly that the diffeomorphism in part (I) of the theorem acts trivially on homology but this can be easily deduced from the proof there.)
2.
It is not known whether each complex structure on ℂ​Pn{\mathbb{C}}P^{n}, n≥3n\geq 3, is of Kähler type. For n=1n=1 this is obviously true.
For n=2n=2 this is true by a deep result of Yau [Yau1].
3. Part (II) of the theorem implies that DiffH​(ℂ​Pn)=Diff0​(ℂ​Pn)\mathrm{Diff}_{H}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n})=\mathrm{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n}) if and only if 𝐶𝐾⁡(ℂ​Pn)\mathit{CK}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n})
is connected.
It is known that DiffH​(ℂ​Pn)≠Diff0​(ℂ​Pn)\mathrm{Diff}_{H}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n})\neq\mathrm{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n}) for n=3n=3 [KrS, Thm. 1.2] and n=4n=4 [Bru, Rem. II.11]. Thus, 𝐶𝐾⁡(ℂ​Pn)\mathit{CK}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n}), n=3,4n=3,4, has more than one connected component. We do not know whether the same facts hold for n=2n=2 and n>4n>4.
4.
Part (II) of 6 follows from a theorem of Fröhlicher and Nijenhuis [FroN], cf. [KoS1, Thm. 6.3].
Corollary 6.4
Each connected component of the space 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑⁡(ℂ​Pn){\tt Teich}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n}) is a point. The DiffH​(ℂ​Pn)\mathrm{Diff}_{H}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n})-action
on 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑⁡(ℂ​Pn){\tt Teich}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n}) is transitive.
Corollary 6.5
The group SympH​(ℂ​Pn,ωF​S)⊂DiffH​(ℂ​Pn)\mathrm{Symp}_{H}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n},\omega_{FS})\subset\mathrm{Diff}_{H}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n}) acts transitively on the set of connected components of
𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑⁡(ℂ​Pn){\tt Teich}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n}) compatible with ωF​S\omega_{FS}.
Proof of 6:
Assume JJ is a complex structure on ℂ​Pn{\mathbb{C}}P^{n} compatible with ωF​S\omega_{FS}. Then, by part (I) of
6, there exists ϕ∈DiffH​(ℂ​Pn)\phi\in\mathrm{Diff}_{H}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n}) such that ϕ∗​J=Is​t\phi^{*}J=I_{st}.
Then ϕ∗​ωF​S\phi^{*}\omega_{FS} is a symplectic form compatible with Is​tI_{st} and cohomologous to ωF​S\omega_{FS}.
Therefore the straight path in the space of 2-forms connecting these forms is formed by cohomologous symplectic forms, and consequently, by Moser’s theorem [Mos], there exists ψ∈Diff0​(ℂ​Pn)\psi\in\mathrm{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n}) such that ψ∗​ϕ∗​ωF​S=ωF​S\psi^{*}\phi^{*}\omega_{FS}=\omega_{FS}. Then ϕ​ψ∈SympH​(ℂ​Pn,ωF​S)\phi\psi\in\mathrm{Symp}_{H}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n},\omega_{FS}) and (ϕ​ψ)∗​J=ψ∗​Is​t(\phi\psi)^{*}J=\psi^{*}I_{st} lies in the Diff0​(ℂ​Pn)\mathrm{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n})-orbit
of Is​tI_{st}. This means that the connected component of 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑⁡(ℂ​Pn){\tt Teich}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n}) containing [J][J] is mapped under the action of ϕ​ψ∈SympH​(ℂ​Pn,ωF​S)\phi\psi\in\mathrm{Symp}_{H}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n},\omega_{FS}) into the connected component of 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑⁡(ℂ​Pn){\tt Teich}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n}) containing [Is​t][I_{st}]. This yields the proposition.
We are ready to prove 3.2 – let us first recall it here.
Theorem 6.6 (=3.2)
A. For each l∈ℤ>0l\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0},
Kähler-type embeddings of disjoint unions of lnl^{n} equal balls into (ℂ​Pn,ωF​S)({\mathbb{C}}P^{n},\omega_{FS}) are unobstructed.
More precisely,
if ln​Vol​(B2​n​(r),ω0)<Vol⁡(ℂ​Pn,ωF​S)l^{n}{\rm{Vol}\,}(B^{2n}(r),\omega_{0})<{\rm{Vol}\,}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n},\omega_{FS}), then for any complex structure JJ on ℂ​Pn{\mathbb{C}}P^{n} compatible with ωF​S\omega_{FS} (and, in particular, for the standard complex structure Is​tI_{st}) and for each proper (possibly empty) complex submanifold Σ⊂(ℂ​Pn,J)\Sigma\subset({\mathbb{C}}P^{n},J), there exists a [J][J]-Kähler-type embedding of ⨆i=1lnB2​n​(r)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{l^{n}}B^{2n}(r)
into (ℂ​Pn,ωF​S)({\mathbb{C}}P^{n},\omega_{FS}) which is holomorphic with respect to a complex structure on ℂ​Pn{\mathbb{C}}P^{n} that is compatible with ωF​S\omega_{FS}
and isotopic to JJ by an isotopy preserving Σ\Sigma (as a set).
B. The group SympH​(ℂ​Pn,ωF​S)\mathrm{Symp}_{H}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n},\omega_{FS}) acts transitively on the set of connected components of 𝐶𝐾⁡(ℂ​Pn)\mathit{CK}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n}) compatible with
ωF​S\omega_{FS}.
C.
For any k∈ℤ>0k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0} and r1,…,rk>0r_{1},\ldots,r_{k}>0, any two Kähler-type embeddings ⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→(ℂ​Pn,ωF​S)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to({\mathbb{C}}P^{n},\omega_{FS}) (if they exist!) lie in the same orbit of the SympH​(ℂ​Pn,ωF​S)\mathrm{Symp}_{H}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n},\omega_{FS})-action.
They lie in the same orbit of the Symp⁡(ℂ​Pn,ωF​S)∩Diff0​(ℂ​Pn)\mathrm{Symp}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n},\omega_{FS})\cap\mathrm{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n})-action
if and only if they favor a common connected component of 𝐶𝐾⁡(ℂ​Pn)\mathit{CK}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n}). In the latter case there exists [I]∈𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑⁡(ℂ​Pn,ωF​S)[I]\in{\tt Teich}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n},\omega_{FS}) such that both embeddings are of [I][I]-Kähler type.
Proof of 6 (=3.2):
As we already explained in Section 3.2, we only need to prove parts B and C.
Part B is exactly
6 that we have just proved.
Let us prove part C. By 6, for each connected component 𝒞0{\mathcal{C}}_{0} of 𝐶𝐾⁡(ℂ​Pn)\mathit{CK}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n})
the corresponding connected component 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(ℂ​Pn){\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n}) of 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑⁡(ℂ​Pn){\tt Teich}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n}) is just a point. Consequently, for any r¯{\bar{r}} the set 𝙺𝒞0​(r¯)⊂𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(ℂ​Pn){\tt{K}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}({\bar{r}})\subset{\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}({\mathbb{C}}P^{n}) is connected. Now applying 3.1
along with part B of this theorem we get part C.
This finishes the proof.
Now consider the case of ℂ​P2{\mathbb{C}}P^{2}. Similarly to our previous notation, we write (ℂ​P2^)k(\widehat{{\mathbb{C}}P^{2}})^{k} for the set of all ordered kk-tuples of pairwise distinct points in ℂ​P2{\mathbb{C}}P^{2}.
As before, given 𝕩=(x1,…,xk)∈(ℂ​P2^)k{\mathbbm{x}}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\in(\widehat{{\mathbb{C}}P^{2}})^{k}, the manifold ℂ​P2~Is​t,𝕩\widetilde{{\mathbb{C}}P^{2}}_{I_{st},{\mathbbm{x}}}
is the complex blow-up of (ℂ​P2,Is​t)({\mathbb{C}}P^{2},I_{st}) at x1,…,xkx_{1},\ldots,x_{k}.
Given r¯=(r1,…,rk)∈(ℝ>0)k{\bar{r}}=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{k})\in({\mathbb{R}}_{>0})^{k} and J∈𝐶𝐾⁡(ℂ​P2)J\in\mathit{CK}({\mathbb{C}}P^{2}), set α:=[ω]\alpha:=[\omega] and
α~r¯:=Π∗​[ω]−π​∑i=1kri2​ei∈H2​(ℂ​P2~J,𝕩,ℝ).{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bar{r}}:=\Pi^{*}[\omega]-\pi\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}^{2}e_{i}\in H^{2}(\widetilde{{\mathbb{C}}P^{2}}_{J,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}}).
Definition 6.7
Assume that k∈ℤk\in{\mathbb{Z}}, 1≤k≤81\leq k\leq 8.
We say that kk points in ℂ​P2{\mathbb{C}}P^{2} are in general position if
(i) no three points are on a line;
(ii) no six points are on a conic;
(iii) no cubic passes through the points with one of the points being a singular
point.
Proposition 6.8
Let 1≤k≤81\leq k\leq 8.
Then the set of tuples 𝕩=(x1,…,xk)∈(ℂ​P2^)k{\mathbbm{x}}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\in(\widehat{{\mathbb{C}}P^{2}})^{k} such that x1,…,xkx_{1},\ldots,x_{k} are in general position is connected
and dense in (ℂ​P2^)k(\widehat{{\mathbb{C}}P^{2}})^{k}.
Proof of 6:
Consider the product complex structure on (ℂ​P2^)k⊂(ℂ​P2)k(\widehat{{\mathbb{C}}P^{2}})^{k}\subset({\mathbb{C}}P^{2})^{k}.
In view of 6,
the kk-tuples (x1,…,xk)∈(ℂ​P2^)k(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\in(\widehat{{\mathbb{C}}P^{2}})^{k} such that x1,…,xkx_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
are not in general position lie in a proper analytic subset of
(ℂ​P2^)k(\widehat{{\mathbb{C}}P^{2}})^{k}. This means that the set of kk-tuples (x1,…,xk)∈(ℂ​P2^)k(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\in(\widehat{{\mathbb{C}}P^{2}})^{k}
such that x1,…,xkx_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
are in general position
contains the complement of a proper analytic subset of (ℂ​P2^)k(\widehat{{\mathbb{C}}P^{2}})^{k}. Thus, it is connected (because (ℂ​P2^)k(\widehat{{\mathbb{C}}P^{2}})^{k} itself is connected) and
dense (and open) in (ℂ​P2^)k(\widehat{{\mathbb{C}}P^{2}})^{k}.
Proposition 6.9
Assume that 1≤k≤81\leq k\leq 8.
Let r¯=(r1,…,rk)∈(ℝ>0)k{\bar{r}}=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{k})\in({\mathbb{R}}_{>0})^{k}, r1≥r2≥…≥rkr_{1}\geq r_{2}\geq\ldots\geq r_{k}. Let 𝒞0{\mathcal{C}}_{0} be the connected component of 𝐶𝐾⁡(ℂ​P2)\mathit{CK}({\mathbb{C}}P^{2}) containing Is​tI_{st}.
Then for any 𝕩=(x1,…,xk)∈(ℂ​P2^)k{\mathbbm{x}}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\in(\widehat{{\mathbb{C}}P^{2}})^{k} such that x1,…,xk∈ℂ​P2x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in{\mathbb{C}}P^{2} are in general position,
the class α~r¯∈H2​(ℂ​P2~Is​t,𝕩,ℝ){\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bar{r}}\in H^{2}(\widetilde{{\mathbb{C}}P^{2}}_{I_{st},{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}}) is Kähler with respect to I~s​t{\widetilde{I}}_{st} if and only if the numbers r1,…,rkr_{1},\ldots,r_{k} satisfy the following inequalities (listed in [McDP, Cor. 1.3G]):
(v) ∑i=1kri4<1\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}^{4}<1,
(c1) r12+r22<1r_{1}^{2}+r_{2}^{2}<1, if 2≤k≤82\leq k\leq 8,
(c2) r12+…+r52<2r_{1}^{2}+\ldots+r_{5}^{2}<2, if 5≤k≤85\leq k\leq 8,
(c3) 2​r12+∑i=27ri2<32r_{1}^{2}+\sum_{i=2}^{7}r_{i}^{2}<3, if 7≤k≤87\leq k\leq 8,
(c4) 2​r12+2​r22+2​r32+r42+…+r82<42r_{1}^{2}+2r_{2}^{2}+2r_{3}^{2}+r_{4}^{2}+\ldots+r_{8}^{2}<4, if k=8k=8,
(c5) 2​∑i=16ri2+r72+r82<52\sum_{i=1}^{6}r_{i}^{2}+r_{7}^{2}+r_{8}^{2}<5, if k=8k=8,
(c6) 3​r12+2​∑i=28ri2<63r_{1}^{2}+2\sum_{i=2}^{8}r_{i}^{2}<6, if k=8k=8.
Proof of 6:
If 1≤k≤81\leq k\leq 8 and the points x1,…,xk∈ℂ​P2x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in{\mathbb{C}}P^{2} are in general position (such points exist by 6), then (ℂ​P2~Is​t,𝕩,I~s​t)(\widetilde{{\mathbb{C}}P^{2}}_{I_{st},{\mathbbm{x}}},{\widetilde{I}}_{st}),
for 𝕩=(x1,…,xk){\mathbbm{x}}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}), is a del Pezzo surface (see e.g. [Dol, Thm. 8.1.25]).
As such, it is a good generic surface in the sense of
[FriM, Def. 2.1] (see [FriM, Rem. 2.9]). The Kähler cone of (ℂ​P2~Is​t,𝕩,I~s​t)(\widetilde{{\mathbb{C}}P^{2}}_{I_{st},{\mathbbm{x}}},{\widetilde{I}}_{st})
is the cone of classes in H2​(ℂ​P2~Is​t,𝕩,ℝ)H^{2}(\widetilde{{\mathbb{C}}P^{2}}_{I_{st},{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}}) that have a positive square and
evaluate positively on the homology classes of holomorphically
embedded 2-spheres with self-intersection index equal to −1-1 and on the class
3​[ℂ​P1]−∑i=1k[Ei]∈H2​(ℂ​P2~Is​t,𝕩,ℤ)3[{\mathbb{C}}P^{1}]-\sum_{i=1}^{k}[E_{i}]\in H_{2}(\widetilde{{\mathbb{C}}P^{2}}_{I_{st},{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{Z}}) [FriM].
Together with the information on the homology classes of holomorphically
embedded 2-spheres with self-intersection index equal to −1-1 given in [Dmz],
this yields that α~r¯{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bar{r}} is Kähler
with respect to I~s​t{\widetilde{I}}_{st} if and only if the numbers r1,…,rkr_{1},\ldots,r_{k} satisfy the inequalities (v), (c1)-(c6) – see [McDP, Cor. 1.3G].
This finishes the proof of the proposition.
Let us recall the following result of Gromov.
Proposition 6.10 [Gro]
The group Symp⁡(ℂ​P2,ωF​S)\mathrm{Symp}({\mathbb{C}}P^{2},\omega_{FS}) is homotopy equivalent to P​U​(3)PU(3) and, in particular, connected.
Recall 3.2:
Proposition 6.11 (=3.2)
There is only one connected component of 𝐶𝐾⁡(ℂ​P2)\mathit{CK}({\mathbb{C}}P^{2}) compatible with ωF​S\omega_{FS} – it is the connected component
of 𝐶𝐾⁡(ℂ​P2)\mathit{CK}({\mathbb{C}}P^{2}) containing Is​tI_{st}. Any two complex structures in that connected component are isotopic.
Proof of 6 (= 3.2):
The proposition follows immediately from 6, 6.
and part (II) of 6.
Now we are ready to prove 3.2 – let us recall it here.
Theorem 6.12 (=3.2)
A. Assume that 1≤k≤81\leq k\leq 8 and r1≥r2≥…≥rk>0r_{1}\geq r_{2}\geq\ldots\geq r_{k}>0.
Then for any proper (possibly empty) complex submanifold Σ⊂(ℂ​P2,Is​t)\Sigma\subset({\mathbb{C}}P^{2},I_{st}),
any symplectic embedding ⨆i=1kB4​(ri)→(ℂ​P2∖Σ,ωF​S)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{4}(r_{i})\to({\mathbb{C}}P^{2}\setminus\Sigma,\omega_{FS}) is, in fact, of [Is​t][I_{st}]-Kähler type:
it is holomorphic with respect to a complex structure on ℂ​P2{\mathbb{C}}P^{2} that is compatible with ωF​S\omega_{FS} and isotopic
to Is​tI_{st} by an isotopy fixing Σ\Sigma (as a set).
Accordingly, by part A of 3.2, such a Kähler-type embedding ⨆i=1kB4​(ri)→(ℂ​P2∖Σ,ωF​S)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{4}(r_{i})\to({\mathbb{C}}P^{2}\setminus\Sigma,\omega_{FS})
exists if and only if the radii r1,…,rkr_{1},\ldots,r_{k} satisfy the inequalities (v), (c1)-(c6) listed in part A of 3.2.
B. For any proper (possibly empty) complex submanifold Σ⊂(ℂ​P2,Is​t)\Sigma\subset({\mathbb{C}}P^{2},I_{st}) and any k=l2k=l^{2}, l∈ℤ>0l\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0},
any symplectic embedding ⨆i=1kB4​(r)→(ℂ​P2∖Σ,ωF​S)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{4}(r)\to({\mathbb{C}}P^{2}\setminus\Sigma,\omega_{FS}) is, in fact, of [Is​t][I_{st}]-Kähler type:
it is holomorphic with respect to a complex structure on ℂ​P2{\mathbb{C}}P^{2} that is compatible with ωF​S\omega_{FS}
and isotopic to Is​tI_{st} by an isotopy preserving Σ\Sigma (as a set).
Accordingly, by part B of 3.2, such Kähler-type embeddings ⨆i=1kB4​(r)→(ℂ​P2∖Σ,ωF​S)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{4}(r)\to({\mathbb{C}}P^{2}\setminus\Sigma,\omega_{FS}) are unobstructed: they exist if and only if k​Vol​(B4​(r),ω0)<Vol⁡(ℂ​P2,ωF​S)k{\rm{Vol}\,}(B^{4}(r),\omega_{0})<{\rm{Vol}\,}({\mathbb{C}}P^{2},\omega_{FS}).
C. Let Σ⊂(ℂ​P2,ωF​S)\Sigma\subset({\mathbb{C}}P^{2},\omega_{FS})
be either of the following:
(1) the empty set;
(2) a finite union of closed compact symplectic submanifolds (without boundary) of real dimension 2
whose pairwise intersections (if they exist) are transverse and ω\omega–orthogonal;
(3) a Lagrangian submanifold which is diffeomorphic to either 𝕊2{\mathbb{S}}^{2} or ℝ​P2{\mathbb{R}}P^{2}.
For any k∈ℤ>0k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0} and r1,…,rk>0r_{1},\ldots,r_{k}>0, any two Kähler-type embeddings ⨆i=1kB4​(ri)→(ℂ​P2∖Σ,ωF​S)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{4}(r_{i})\to({\mathbb{C}}P^{2}\setminus\Sigma,\omega_{FS}) (if they exist!) lie in the same orbit of the Symp0c​(ℂ​P2∖Σ,ωF​S)\mathrm{Symp}^{c}_{0}({\mathbb{C}}P^{2}\setminus\Sigma,\omega_{FS})-action, meaning that
the space of Kähler-type embeddings ⨆i=1kB4​(ri)→(ℂ​P2∖Σ,ωF​S)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{4}(r_{i})\to({\mathbb{C}}P^{2}\setminus\Sigma,\omega_{FS}) is connected.
Proof of 6 (=3.2):
As we already explained in Section 3.2,
part B of 3.2 follows from
3.2
and from part (II) of 3.1,
while part C of 3.2 follows directly from part (I) of
3.1.
Let us prove part A.
Assume f:⨆i=1kB4​(ri)→(ℂ​P2,ωF​S)f:\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{4}(r_{i})\to({\mathbb{C}}P^{2},\omega_{FS}) is a symplectic embedding. Then, by 3.2,
the radii r1,…,rkr_{1},\ldots,r_{k} satisfy the conditions (v), (c1)-(c6).
Consequently, 6 together with part (I) of 3.1 (=5.2) yields that there exists an [Is​t][I_{st}]-Kähler-type embedding f′:⨆i=1kB4​(ri)→(ℂ​P2,ωF​S)f^{\prime}:\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{4}(r_{i})\to({\mathbb{C}}P^{2},\omega_{FS}). By part (II) of 3.1, this implies that ff is of [Is​t][I_{st}]-Kähler type too.
This finishes the proof of part A and of the theorem.
7 The case of ℂ​P2​♯​ℂ​P2¯{\mathbb{C}}P^{2}\sharp\overline{{\mathbb{C}}P^{2}}
Let M=ℂ​P2​♯​ℂ​P2¯M={\mathbb{C}}P^{2}\sharp\overline{{\mathbb{C}}P^{2}}. As a smooth manifold, MM will be identified with the complex blow-up ℂ​P~Is​t,x2\widetilde{{\mathbb{C}}P}^{2}_{I_{st},x}
of (ℂ​P2,Is​t)({\mathbb{C}}P^{2},I_{st}) at a point x∈ℂ​P2x\in{\mathbb{C}}P^{2}. Let J1J_{1} be the complex structure on MM identified in this way with the blow-up complex structure
I~s​t{\widetilde{I}}_{st} on ℂ​P~Is​t,x2\widetilde{{\mathbb{C}}P}^{2}_{I_{st},x}.

Let [ℂ​P1]∈H2​(M,ℤ)[{\mathbb{C}}P^{1}]\in H_{2}(M;{\mathbb{Z}}) be the homology class of a projective line ℂ​P1⊂M{\mathbb{C}}P^{1}\subset M. Denote by h,e∈H2​(M,ℤ)h,e\in H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{Z}}) the cohomology classes Poincaré-dual to the homology classes of [ℂ​P1][{\mathbb{C}}P^{1}] and [E][E].

⟨[ωλ],[E]⟩=λ\langle[\omega_{\lambda}],[E]\rangle=\lambda.
Thus, [ωλ]=π​h−λ​e[\omega_{\lambda}]=\pi h-\lambda e.
Further on in this section we fix

R1≥R2≥…≥Rk−1>0,R_{1}\geq R_{2}\geq\ldots\geq R_{k-1}>0,
and set

For each (x1,…,xk−1)∈M^k−1(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k-1})\in{\hat{M}}^{k-1}

ΠM∗​[ωλ]−π​∑i=1k−1Ri2​ei,M∈H2​(M~J1,x1,…,xk−1,ℝ)\Pi_{M}^{*}[\omega_{\lambda}]-\pi\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}R_{i}^{2}e_{i,M}\in H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{J_{1},x_{1},\ldots,x_{k-1}};{\mathbb{R}})

Now we are ready to prove 3.3 – let us recall it here.
Theorem 7.3 (=3.3)
Let M=ℂ​P2​♯​ℂ​P2¯M={\mathbb{C}}P^{2}\sharp\overline{{\mathbb{C}}P^{2}} and let ωλ\omega_{\lambda} be a Kähler-type form on MM as above.
Then the following claims hold:
A. Let 2≤k≤82\leq k\leq 8, R1≥R2≥…≥Rk−1>0R_{1}\geq R_{2}\geq\ldots\geq R_{k-1}>0. Assume that
r1:=R1≥…≥ri−1:=Ri−1≥ri:=λ/π≥ri+1:=Ri≥…≥rk:=Rk−1r_{1}:=R_{1}\geq\ldots\geq r_{i-1}:=R_{i-1}\geq r_{i}:=\sqrt{\lambda/\pi}\geq r_{i+1}:=R_{i}\geq\ldots\geq r_{k}:=R_{k-1}.
Then for any proper (possibly empty) complex submanifold Σ⊂(M,J1)\Sigma\subset(M,J_{1}),
the following conditions are equivalent:
•
There exists a [J1][J_{1}]-Kähler-type embedding
⨆i=1k−1B4​(Ri)→(M∖Σ,ωλ)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k-1}B^{4}(R_{i})\to(M\setminus\Sigma,\omega_{\lambda}) holomorphic with respect to a complex structure on MM that is
compatible with ωλ\omega_{\lambda} and isotopic to J1J_{1} by an isotopy preserving Σ\Sigma (as a set).
•
The numbers r1,…,rkr_{1},\ldots,r_{k} satisfy the inequalities (v), (c1)-(c6) in part A of
3.2.
For r1,…,rkr_{1},\ldots,r_{k} satisfying the inequalities (v), (c1)-(c6) in part A of 3.2, any symplectic embedding
⨆i=1k−1B4​(Ri)→(M∖Σ,ωλ)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k-1}B^{4}(R_{i})\to(M\setminus\Sigma,\omega_{\lambda}) is, in fact, of [J1][J_{1}]-Kähler type.
B.
For any k∈ℤ>0k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0} and R1,…,Rk>0R_{1},\ldots,R_{k}>0,
any two Kähler-type embeddings ⨆i=1kB4​(Ri)→(M,ωλ)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{4}(R_{i})\to(M,\omega_{\lambda}) (if they exist!) lie in the same orbit of the Symp0​(M,ωλ)\mathrm{Symp}_{0}(M,\omega_{\lambda})-action, meaning that
the space of Kähler-type embeddings ⨆i=1kB4​(Ri)→(M,ωλ)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{4}(R_{i})\to(M,\omega_{\lambda}) is connected.
Proof of 7 (=3.3):
As we already explained in Section 3.3, part B follows from part (I) of 3.1.
Let us prove part A.
Assume that the numbers r1,…,rkr_{1},\ldots,r_{k} satisfy the conditions (v), (c1)-(c6). Then, by 7, combined with part (I) of 3.1, the union ⨆i=1k−1B4​(ri)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k-1}B^{4}(r_{i}) admits a
Kähler-type embedding into (M,ωλ)(M,\omega_{\lambda}) which is holomorphic with respect to a complex structure on MM isotopic to J1J_{1} and compatible with ωλ\omega_{\lambda}, meaning that the embedding is of [J1][J_{1}]-Kähler type.
By part (II) of 3.1, this implies that
any symplectic embedding ⨆i=1k−1B4​(ri)→(M,ωλ)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k-1}B^{4}(r_{i})\to(M,\omega_{\lambda}) is of [J1][J_{1}]-Kähler type.
Conversely, assume
there exists a Kähler-type embedding f=⨆i=1k−1fi:⨆i=1k−1B4​(ri)→(M,ωλ)f=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k-1}f_{i}:\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k-1}B^{4}(r_{i})\to(M,\omega_{\lambda}) which is holomorphic with respect to a complex structure II compatible with ωλ\omega_{\lambda} and
such that I=ϕ∗​J1I=\phi^{*}J_{1} for some ϕ∈Diff0​(M)\phi\in\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M).
Set xi:=fi​(0)x_{i}:=f_{i}(0), i=1,…,k−1i=1,\ldots,k-1, and 𝕩:=(x1,…,xk−1){\mathbbm{x}}:=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k-1}), ϕ⁡(𝕩):=(ϕ⁡(x1),…,ϕ⁡(xk−1))\phi({\mathbbm{x}}):=\left(\phi(x_{1}),\ldots,\phi(x_{k-1})\right).
Recall that a Kähler-type embedding of a closed ball is defined as a Kähler-type embedding of a larger open ball. Therefore using
6 and composing ff, if necessary, with a small parallel translation of the balls in ℝ2​n{\mathbb{R}}^{2n},
we may assume without loss of generality that the points ϕ⁡(x1),…,ϕ⁡(xk−1)∈M\phi(x_{1}),\ldots,\phi(x_{k-1})\in M are in general position.
By part (I) of 3.1,
the cohomology class α~r¯∈H2​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ){\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bar{r}}\in H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}}) is Kähler with respect to I~{\widetilde{I}}.
The diffeomorphism ϕ:M→M\phi:M\to M induces a diffeomorphism M~I,𝕩→M~J1,ϕ⁡(𝕩){\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}}\to{\widetilde{M}}_{J_{1},\phi({\mathbbm{x}})} that identifies I~{\widetilde{I}} with J~1{\widetilde{J}}_{1} as well as the classes α~r¯{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bar{r}} in H2​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ)H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}})
and in H2​(M~J1,ϕ⁡(𝕩),ℝ)H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{J_{1},\phi({\mathbbm{x}})};{\mathbb{R}}) (abusing the notation, we use the same letter for both classes).
Therefore α~r¯∈H2​(M~J1,ϕ⁡(𝕩),ℝ){\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bar{r}}\in H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{J_{1},\phi({\mathbbm{x}})};{\mathbb{R}}) is Kähler with respect to J~1{\widetilde{J}}_{1}.
Since ϕ⁡(x1),…,ϕ⁡(xk−1)\phi(x_{1}),\ldots,\phi(x_{k-1}) are in general position,
7 yields that
the conditions (v), (c1)-(c6) are satisfied.
This proves part A and finishes the proof of the theorem.
8 The case of ℂ​P1×ℂ​P1{\mathbb{C}}P^{1}\times{\mathbb{C}}P^{1}
Let M=ℂ​P1×ℂ​P1M={\mathbb{C}}P^{1}\times{\mathbb{C}}P^{1} and J0J_{0} the standard product complex structure on MM.
Let J2​lJ_{2l}, l∈ℤ≥0l\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}, be a complex structure on MM such that (M,J2​l)(M,J_{2l}) is biholomorphic to the Hirzebruch surface
𝔽2​l{\mathbb{F}}_{2l}. In particular, we assume that J0J_{0} is the standard product complex structure on MM.
Let b,f∈H2​(M,ℤ)b,f\in H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{Z}}) be the cohomology classes Poincaré-dual, respectively, to [ℂ​P1×p​t][{\mathbb{C}}P^{1}\times pt] and [F]=[p​t×ℂ​P1][F]=[pt\times{\mathbb{C}}P^{1}].
Let ω0\omega_{0} be the Fubini-Study form on ℂ​P1{\mathbb{C}}P^{1} normalized so that ∫ℂ​P1ω0=π\int_{{\mathbb{C}}P^{1}}\omega_{0}=\pi. For each μ≥1\mu\geq 1 define the symplectic form
ωμ\omega_{\mu} on MM as
ωμ:=μ​ω0⊕ω0.\omega_{\mu}:=\mu\omega_{0}\oplus\omega_{0}.
Thus,
[ωμ]=π⁡(μ​b+f)[\omega_{\mu}]=\pi(\mu b+f).
Further on in this section, we fix
2≤k≤8,2\leq k\leq 8,
R1,R2,…,Rk−2>0,0<Rk−1<1,R_{1},R_{2},\ldots,R_{k-2}>0,0<R_{k-1}<1,
and define r1≥r2≥…≥rkr_{1}\geq r_{2}\geq\ldots\geq r_{k} as the numbers

Abusing the notation, for a blow-up of MM (respectively, of ℂ​P2{\mathbb{C}}P^{2}) at any finite collection of points x1,x2,…x_{1},x_{2},\ldots (respectively, y1,y2,…y_{1},y_{2},\ldots)

Now we are ready to prove 3.3 – let us recall it here.
Theorem 8.3 (=3.3)
Let M=ℂ​P1×ℂ​P1M={\mathbb{C}}P^{1}\times{\mathbb{C}}P^{1} and let ωμ\omega_{\mu} be as above.
Let Σ⊂(M,J0)\Sigma\subset(M,J_{0}) be either a proper (possibly empty) complex submanifold or Σ=(pt×ℂ​P1)∪(ℂ​P1×pt)\Sigma=(\textrm{pt}\times{\mathbb{C}}P^{1})\cup({\mathbb{C}}P^{1}\times\textrm{pt}).
Then the following claims hold:
A. Assume 2≤k≤82\leq k\leq 8,
R1,R2,…,Rk−2>0R_{1},R_{2},\ldots,R_{k-2}>0, 0<Rk−1<10<R_{k-1}<1.
Consider the numbers
R12μ+1−Rk−12,R22μ+1−Rk−12,…,μ−Rk−12μ+1−Rk−12,1−Rk−12μ+1−Rk−12,\frac{R_{1}^{2}}{\mu+1-R_{k-1}^{2}},\frac{R_{2}^{2}}{\mu+1-R_{k-1}^{2}},\ldots,\frac{\mu-R_{k-1}^{2}}{\mu+1-R_{k-1}^{2}},\frac{1-R_{k-1}^{2}}{\mu+1-R_{k-1}^{2}},
sort them in the non-increasing order and denote the resulting kk numbers by r1≥…≥rkr_{1}\geq\ldots\geq r_{k}.
Then the following conditions are equivalent:
•
There exists a [J0][J_{0}]-Kähler-type embedding
⨆i=1k−1B4​(Ri)→(M∖Σ,ωμ)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k-1}B^{4}(R_{i})\to(M\setminus\Sigma,\omega_{\mu}) holomorphic with respect to a complex structure on MM
that is compatible with
ωμ\omega_{\mu} and isotopic to J0J_{0} by an isotopy preserving Σ\Sigma (as a set).
•
The numbers r1,…,rkr_{1},\ldots,r_{k} defined above satisfy the inequalities in part A of
3.2.
For r1,…,rkr_{1},\ldots,r_{k} satisfying the inequalities (v), (c1)-(c6) in part A of 3.2, any symplectic embedding
⨆i=1k−1B4​(Ri)→(M∖Σ,ωμ)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k-1}B^{4}(R_{i})\to(M\setminus\Sigma,\omega_{\mu}) is, in fact, of [J0][J_{0}]-Kähler type.
B. Assume that μ=1\mu=1.
Then for any l∈ℤ>0l\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0},
any symplectic embedding of a disjoint union of 2​l22l^{2} equal balls into (M∖Σ,ω1)(M\setminus\Sigma,\omega_{1})
is, in fact, of [J0][J_{0}]-Kähler type, and such symplectic (or, equivalently, [J0][J_{0}]-Kähler-type) embeddings are unobstructed.
More precisely, if 2​l2​Vol​(B4​(r),ω0)<Vol⁡(M,ω1)2l^{2}{\rm{Vol}\,}(B^{4}(r),\omega_{0})<{\rm{Vol}\,}(M,\omega_{1}), then there exists a Kähler-type embedding of ⨆i=12​l2B4​(r)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{2l^{2}}B^{4}(r)
into (M∖Σ,ω1)(M\setminus\Sigma,\omega_{1}) which is holomorphic with respect to a complex structure on MM that is compatible with ω1\omega_{1}
and isotopic to J0J_{0}
by an isotopy preserving Σ\Sigma (as a set).
C. For any k∈ℤ>0k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0} and R1,…,Rk>0R_{1},\ldots,R_{k}>0,
any two Kähler-type embeddings ⨆i=1kB4​(Ri)→(M∖Σ,ωμ)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{4}(R_{i})\to(M\setminus\Sigma,\omega_{\mu}) (if they exist!) lie in the same orbit of the Symp0c​(M∖Σ,ωμ)\mathrm{Symp}^{c}_{0}(M\setminus\Sigma,\omega_{\mu})-action, meaning that
the space of Kähler-type embeddings ⨆i=1kB4​(Ri)→(M∖Σ,ωμ)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{4}(R_{i})\to(M\setminus\Sigma,\omega_{\mu}) is connected.
Proof of 8 (=3.3):
As we already explained in Section 3.3, part B of the theorem follows from 3.2 and part C follows from part (I) of 3.1.
The proof of part A
follows the proof of part A of 7, with
8
used instead of
7.
9 Existence of Kähler-type embeddings of balls and Campana-simple complex structures
In this section we discuss Kähler-type embeddings of balls and other domains into symplectic manifolds admitting Kähler-type complex structures with “few” complex subvarieties. The relevant claims about such complex structures on tori, K3 surfaces and IHS-hyperkähler manifolds will be discussed in Sections 10 and 12.
The results of Sections 3.4, 3.5 on the existence of
Kähler-type embeddings of disjoint unions of balls into tori, K3 surfaces and IHS-hyperkähler manifolds will then follow as particular cases of the general
result (9) stated further on in this section.
As before, let MM, dimℝ=2n\dim_{\mathbb{R}}=2n, be a closed connected manifold equipped with a Kähler-type symplectic form ω\omega.
Let us recall the definition of a Campana-simple complex structure.
Assume II is a Kähler-type complex structure on MM. Let 𝔘{\mathfrak{U}} be the union of all positive-dimensional proper complex
subvarieties of (M,I)(M,I). The set 𝔘{\mathfrak{U}} is
either a countable union of proper analytic subvarieties of MM (and hence has a dense connected complement) or the whole MM (see e.g. [EV1, Remark 4.2]).
If 𝔘{\mathfrak{U}} is a countable union of proper analytic subvarieties of MM, the complex structure II is called Campana-simple and
the points of M∖𝔘M\setminus{\mathfrak{U}} are called Campana-generic with respect to II.
Examples of manifolds with
a Campana-simple complex structure
include the generic complex tori of complex dimension >1>1
(see 10.1), as well as generic K3 surfaces (see 10.2),
and generic deformations of hyperkähler manifolds [Ver4].
One can show that a torus equipped with a Campana-simple complex structure does not admit any positive-dimensional
proper complex subvarieties (see 10.1), and the same holds for a generic complex K3 surface [Ver5].
Remark 9.1
1. If II is Campana-simple, then the complex manifold (M,I)(M,I) is not projective, since otherwise it would have admitted a globally defined meromorphic function
ff and could have been represented as the union of zero divisors of the functions f−af-a, for all
a∈ℂa\in{\mathbb{C}} and the zero divisor of
f−1f^{-1}. Consequently, the symplectic form ω\omega compatible with a Campana-simple complex structure cannot be rational – otherwise,
for a Campana-simple complex structure II compatible with ω\omega the complex manifold (M,I)(M,I) would have been projective,
by the Kodaira embedding theorem [Ko].
2. Conjecturally (see [Cam, Question 1.4],
[CaDV, Conj. 1.1]), any closed connected manifold equipped with a Campana-simple complex structure is bimeromorphic to a
hyperkähler orbifold or a finite quotient of a torus.
For complex threefolds this was proved in [HöP].
Campana-simple
complex structures on MM form a Diff⁡(M)\mathrm{Diff}(M)-invariant subset of the set of all Kähler-type complex structures on MM.
Definition 9.2
Denote by 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑s​(M)⊂𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑​(M){\tt Teich}^{s}(M)\subset{\tt Teich}(M)
the set of points of
𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑⁡(M){\tt Teich}(M) that can be represented by Campana-simple complex structures.
If 𝒞0{\mathcal{C}}_{0} is a connected component of 𝐶𝐾⁡(M)\mathit{CK}(M), denote by 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0s​(M)⊂𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M){\tt Teich}^{s}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M)\subset{\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M)
the set of points of
𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M){\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M) that can be represented by Campana-simple
complex structures.
In other words,
𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0s​(M)=𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑s​(M)∩𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M).{\tt Teich}^{s}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M)={\tt Teich}^{s}(M)\cap{\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M).
Theorem 9.3
Let 𝒞0{\mathcal{C}}_{0} be
a connected component of 𝐶𝐾⁡(M)\mathit{CK}(M) compatible with ω\omega. Let k∈ℤ>0k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}, r¯=(r1,…,rk)∈(ℝ>0)k{\bar{r}}=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{k})\in({\mathbb{R}}_{>0})^{k}.
Assume Vol⁡(⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri),ω0)<Vol⁡(M,ω){\rm{Vol}\,}\left(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i}),\omega_{0}\right)<{\rm{Vol}\,}(M,\omega).
Then the following claims hold:
(I)
Let MM, dimℝM=2​n\dim_{\mathbb{R}}M=2n, be a closed manifold.
Assume that ω\omega is a symplectic form on MM compatible with a Campana-simple complex structure II on MM.
Then Kähler-type embeddings ⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→(M,ω)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to(M,\omega) are unobstructed.
More precisely, if Vol⁡(⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri),ω0)<Vol⁡(M,ω){\rm{Vol}\,}\left(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i}),\omega_{0}\right)<{\rm{Vol}\,}(M,\omega),
then ⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i}) admits an [I][I]-Kähler-type embedding into (M,ω)(M,\omega).
(II) Assume that
𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M,ω)∩𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0s​(M){\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M,\omega)\cap{\tt Teich}^{s}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M) is a dense connected subset of 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M,ω){\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M,\omega).
Then any two Kähler-type embeddings ⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→(M,ω)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to(M,\omega) favoring 𝒞0{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
lie in the same orbit of the Symp⁡(M,ω)∩Diff0​(M)\mathrm{Symp}(M,\omega)\cap\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M)-action.
In particular, there exists [I]∈𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M,ω)[I]\in{\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M,\omega)
such that both embeddings are of [I][I]-Kähler-type.
If, in addition, SympH​(M)\mathrm{Symp}_{H}(M) acts transitively on the set of connected components of 𝐶𝐾⁡(M)\mathit{CK}(M) compatible with ω\omega, then
any two Kähler-type embeddings ⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri)→(M,ω)\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i})\to(M,\omega)
lie in the same orbit of the SympH​(M,ω)\mathrm{Symp}_{H}(M,\omega)-action.
For the proof of 9
we need the following claim.
Proposition 9.4
Assume II is a Campana-simple complex structure on MM, a cohomology class α∈H2​(M,ℝ)\alpha\in H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}}) is Kähler with respect to II,
and the points x1,…,xk∈Mx_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in M are Campana-generic with respect to II. Set 𝕩:=(x1,…,xk){\mathbbm{x}}:=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}).
Then the cohomology class
α~r¯:=Π∗​α−π​∑i=1kri2​ei∈H2​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ){\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bar{r}}:=\Pi^{*}\alpha-\pi\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}^{2}e_{i}\in H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}})
is Kähler with respect to I~{\widetilde{I}} if and only if α~r¯n>0{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bar{r}}^{n}>0,
or, equivalently,
if and only if
Vol⁡(⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri),ω0)<⟨αn,[M]⟩.{\rm{Vol}\,}\left(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i}),\omega_{0}\right)<\langle\alpha^{n},[M]\rangle.
Proof of 9:
This is proved in [EV1, Thm. 8.6] – the proof is a straightforward application of the Demailly-Paun theorem [DP, Thm. 0.1] describing the Kähler cone of an arbitrary closed manifold equipped with a Kähler-type complex structure.
Now we can prove 9.
Proof of 9:
The complex structure II appearing in the statement of part (I) of the theorem is compatible with ω\omega. Hence,
the cohomology class α:=[ω]\alpha:=[\omega] is Kähler with respect to II.
Pick points x1,…,xk∈M∖Σx_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in M\setminus\Sigma that are Campana-generic with respect to II.
It is possible to pick these points in the complement of Σ\Sigma, because the set of Campana-generic points of (M,I)(M,I) is dense in MM
(see above)
and Σ\Sigma is a proper complex submanifold of (M,I)(M,I).
Set 𝕩:=(x1,…,xk){\mathbbm{x}}:=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}).
Assume that
Vol⁡(⨆i=1kB2​n​(ri),ω0)<Vol⁡(M,ω)=⟨αn,[M]⟩.{\rm{Vol}\,}\left(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}B^{2n}(r_{i}),\omega_{0}\right)<{\rm{Vol}\,}(M,\omega)=\langle\alpha^{n},[M]\rangle.
Then 9 yields that α~r¯:=Π∗​α−π​∑i=1kri2​ei∈H2​(M~I,𝕩,ℝ){\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bar{r}}:=\Pi^{*}\alpha-\pi\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}^{2}e_{i}\in H^{2}({\widetilde{M}}_{I,{\mathbbm{x}}};{\mathbb{R}}) is Kähler with respect to I~{\widetilde{I}}.
Now
the claim
of part (I)
follows from part (I) of 3.1.
Let us prove part (II).
Assume that 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M,ω)∩𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0s​(M){\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M,\omega)\cap{\tt Teich}^{s}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M) is a dense connected subset of 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M,ω){\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M,\omega).
The argument we have just used in the proof of part (I) shows that I∈𝙺𝒞0​(r¯)I\in{\tt{K}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}({\bar{r}}).
Therefore
𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M,ω)∩𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0s​(M)⊂𝙺𝒞0​(r¯).{\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M,\omega)\cap{\tt Teich}^{s}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M)\subset{\tt{K}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}({\bar{r}}).
Hence, 𝙺𝒞0​(r¯){\tt{K}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}({\bar{r}}) is also a dense connected
subset of 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M,ω){\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M,\omega).
Now part (II) of 3.1 yields the needed claim.
This finishes the proof of 9.
10 The case of tori and K3 surfaces
In this section we discuss Campana-simple complex structures on tori and K3 surfaces and show how to deduce 3.4
from
9.
10.1 Campana-simple complex structures on tori
Denote by 𝐿𝑖𝑛⊂𝐶𝐾⁡(𝕋2​n){\it Lin}\subset\mathit{CK}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n}) the set of linear complex structures on 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n} that are compatible with the standard orientation.
It is naturally identified with the space of linear complex structures on ℝ2​n{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}.
It is well-known (see e.g. [McDS, Prop. 2.5.2]) that the space 𝐿𝑖𝑛{\it Lin} can be identified with
G​L+​(2​n,ℝ)/G​L​(n,ℂ)GL^{+}(2n,{\mathbb{R}})/GL(n,{\mathbb{C}}) which is a connected smooth manifold.
Here the group G​L​(n,ℂ)GL(n,{\mathbb{C}}) is embedded in G​L​(2​n,ℝ)GL(2n,{\mathbb{R}}) by the map
C↦(Re​C−Im​CIm​CRe​C).C\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\textrm{Re}\ C&-\textrm{Im}\ C\\
\textrm{Im}\ C&\textrm{Re}\ C\\
\end{array}\right).
The tangent space TJ​𝐿𝑖𝑛T_{J}{\it Lin} of 𝐿𝑖𝑛{\it Lin} at a point
J∈𝐿𝑖𝑛J\in{\it Lin} is the space of all real 2​n×2​n2n\times 2n matrices RR satisfying R​J+J​R=0RJ+JR=0.
The complex structure on TJ​𝐿𝑖𝑛T_{J}{\it Lin} is given by R↦J​RR\mapsto JR (since G​L+​(2​n,ℝ)/G​L​(n,ℂ)GL^{+}(2n,{\mathbb{R}})/GL(n,{\mathbb{C}}) is symmetric space, this
almost complex structure on 𝐿𝑖𝑛{\it Lin} is indeed integrable
[Bes]).
Thus, further on 𝐿𝑖𝑛{\it Lin} will be viewed as a connected complex manifold.
The following claim is an easy exercise (cf. [EV1, Sec. 6]).
Proposition 10.1
The space T2​n×𝐿𝑖𝑛T^{2n}\times{\it Lin}
can be equipped with a complex structure so that
T2​n×𝐿𝑖𝑛→𝐿𝑖𝑛T^{2n}\times{\it Lin}\to{\it Lin}
is a complex-analytic deformation family such that for each J∈𝐿𝑖𝑛J\in{\it Lin} the complex structure on the fiber over JJ is JJ itself.
For l=1,…,nl=1,\ldots,n denote by Λ2​l\Lambda^{2l} be the space of exterior 2​l2l-forms on ℝ2​n{\mathbb{R}}^{2n} and by Λℚ2​l⊂Λ2​l\Lambda^{2l}_{\mathbb{Q}}\subset\Lambda^{2l}
the space of exterior 2​l2l-forms on ℝ2​n{\mathbb{R}}^{2n} with rational coefficients.
Let ω\omega be a linear symplectic form on 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n}. Denote by ω¯∈Λ2{\overline{\omega}}\in\Lambda^{2} its lift to ℝ2​n{\mathbb{R}}^{2n} – it is a linear symplectic form on ℝ2​n{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}.
For a linear complex structure JJ on 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n} denote by J¯{\overline{J}} the linear complex structure on ℝ2​n{\mathbb{R}}^{2n} which is the lift of JJ.
Proposition 10.2
The cohomology class [ω]∈H2​(𝕋2​n,ℝ)[\omega]\in H^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n};{\mathbb{R}}) of the linear symplectic form ω\omega is Kähler with respect to a linear complex structure JJ on 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n} if and only if ω\omega is compatible with JJ.
Proof of 10.1:
If ω\omega is compatible with JJ then, obviously, [ω]∈H2​(𝕋2​n,ℝ)[\omega]\in H^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n};{\mathbb{R}}) is Kähler with respect to JJ.
Assume [ω]∈H2​(𝕋2​n,ℝ)[\omega]\in H^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n};{\mathbb{R}}) is Kähler with respect to JJ and let us prove that ω\omega is compatible with JJ – i.e., ω\omega is Kähler on (𝕋2​n,J)({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},J). Since [ω]∈H2​(𝕋2​n,ℝ)[\omega]\in H^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n};{\mathbb{R}}) is Kähler with respect to JJ, there exists a Kähler form η\eta on (𝕋2​n,J)({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},J) cohomologous to ω\omega. Viewing 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n} as a compact Lie group and averaging η\eta over 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n}, we get a 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n}-invariant – hence, linear – form ζ\zeta which is Kähler on (𝕋2​n,J)({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},J) and cohomologous to ω\omega. Since there is only one linear
form representing a given cohomology class, we get that ζ=ω\zeta=\omega, which implies that ω\omega is Kähler on (𝕋2​n,J)({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},J).
This finishes the proof of the proposition.
Denote by 𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω){\it Lin}(\omega)
the subset of 𝐿𝑖𝑛{\it Lin} formed by the linear complex structures on 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n} compatible with the linear symplectic form ω\omega.
It is naturally identified with the space of the linear complex structures on ℝ2​n{\mathbb{R}}^{2n} compatible with ω¯{\overline{\omega}}.
For each l=1,…,nl=1,\ldots,n and each a∈H2​l​(𝕋2​n,ℝ)a\in H^{2l}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n};{\mathbb{R}}), a≠0a\neq 0, denote by 𝒯a⊂𝐿𝑖𝑛{\mathcal{T}}_{a}\subset{\it Lin} the set of J∈𝐿𝑖𝑛J\in{\it Lin} such that
a∈HJl,l​(𝕋2​n)a\in H_{J}^{l,l}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n}). It can also be viewed as the Hodge locus of aa for the complex-analytic deformation
family T2​n×𝐿𝑖𝑛→𝐿𝑖𝑛T^{2n}\times{\it Lin}\to{\it Lin} given by 10.1.
Consequently, 𝒯a{\mathcal{T}}_{a} is a complex subvariety of 𝐿𝑖𝑛{\it Lin} – see e.g. [Voi, Vol. 2, Lem. 5.13].
Example 10.3
Let ω\omega be a linear symplectic form on 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n}.
Let a=[ω]∈H2​(𝕋2​n,ℝ)a=[\omega]\in H^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n};{\mathbb{R}}). Then the set 𝒯a{\mathcal{T}}_{a} is the set of linear complex structures on 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n} preserving ω\omega (because a real cohomology class of degree 2 is of type (1,1)(1,1) with respect to a complex structure if and only if it is preserved by it). In this case 𝒯a{\mathcal{T}}_{a} is a smooth complex submanifold of 𝐿𝑖𝑛{\it Lin} and this can be verified by elementary means.
Indeed, first of all, 𝒯a{\mathcal{T}}_{a} is smooth (see e.g. [McDS, Lem. 2.5.5]). If J∈𝒯aJ\in{\mathcal{T}}_{a}, then TJ​𝒯aT_{J}{\mathcal{T}}_{a}
is formed by 2​n×2​n2n\times 2n matrices RR satisfying the two conditions: R​J+J​R=0RJ+JR=0 and Rt​A​R=0R^{t}AR=0, where AA is the matrix of the linear symplectic form ω¯{\overline{\omega}} on ℝ2​n{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}. Since Jt​A​J=AJ^{t}AJ=A (because J∈𝒯aJ\in{\mathcal{T}}_{a}), J​RJR satisfies the same two conditions meaning that TJ​𝒯aT_{J}{\mathcal{T}}_{a} is a complex vector subspace of TJ​𝐿𝑖𝑛T_{J}{\it Lin}. This means that 𝒯a{\mathcal{T}}_{a} is a complex submanifold of 𝐿𝑖𝑛{\it Lin}.
The set 𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω){\it Lin}(\omega) is then an open contractible submanifold of 𝒯a{\mathcal{T}}_{a} (see e.g. [McDS, Lem. 2.5.5]).
Proposition 10.4
Any Campana-simple complex structure on 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n} admits no proper positive-dimensional complex subvarieties.
Proof of 10.1:
A Campana-simple complex structure on 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n} is of Kähler type and therefore can be mapped by a diffeomorphism
of 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n} into a linear complex structure (see [EV1, Prop. 6.1]),
which is also Campana-simple. Thus, it suffices to prove the proposition for a Campana-simple linear complex structure
JJ.
Assume, by contradiction, that such a JJ
admits a positive-dimensional proper complex subvariety.
The parallel translations of 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n} preserve JJ and therefore any point of 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n} is contained in the image of that subvariety under a parallel translation, which contradicts the assumption that JJ is Campana-simple.
This finishes the proof of the proposition.
Denote by 𝐿𝑖𝑛s⊂𝐿𝑖𝑛{\it Lin}^{s}\subset{\it Lin} the space of linear Campana-simple complex structures on 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n}.
Proposition 10.5
Let ω\omega be a linear symplectic form on 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n}.
Then
ω\omega is compatible with a Campana-simple linear complex structure (i.e., 𝐿𝑖𝑛s∩𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω)≠∅{\it Lin}^{s}\cap{\it Lin}(\omega)\neq\emptyset) if and only if ω\omega is irrational. Moreover, in this case 𝐿𝑖𝑛s∩𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω){\it Lin}^{s}\cap{\it Lin}(\omega) is a dense connected subset of 𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω){\it Lin}(\omega).
Proof of 10.1:
Assume ω\omega is rational.
Then, by part 1 of
9, there are no Campana-simple complex structures compatible with ω\omega.
Now assume ω\omega is irrational, or, equivalently, that ω¯{\overline{\omega}} is not a real multiple of a form in Λℚ2\Lambda^{2}_{\mathbb{Q}}.
Let us prove that 𝐿𝑖𝑛s∩𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω){\it Lin}^{s}\cap{\it Lin}(\omega) is a dense connected subset of 𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω){\it Lin}(\omega).
We start with the following lemma.
Lemma 10.6
Assume that a linear symplectic form η\eta is not a real multiple of a form in Λℚ2\Lambda^{2}_{\mathbb{Q}}.
Then for all l=1,…,n−1l=1,\ldots,n-1 the form ηl\eta^{l} is not a real multiple of a form in Λℚ2​l\Lambda^{2l}_{\mathbb{Q}}.
Proof of 10.1:
Assume by contradiction that ηl\eta^{l} is a real multiple of a form in Λℚ2\Lambda^{2}_{\mathbb{Q}}. Since η\eta is symplectic, ηl≠0\eta^{l}\neq 0 and we can assume without loss of generality that ηl∈Λℚ2​l\eta^{l}\in\Lambda^{2l}_{\mathbb{Q}}.
Consider the Galois group Gal⁡(ℂ/ℚ)\operatorname{Gal}({\mathbb{C}}/{\mathbb{Q}}) of ℂ{\mathbb{C}} over ℚ{\mathbb{Q}} (i.e., the group of field automorphisms of ℂ{\mathbb{C}}) and its action on the coefficients of η\eta with respect to the standard basis of ℝ2​n{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}. It is proved in [Lee] that if ηl=ξl\eta^{l}=\xi^{l} for some ξ∈Λ2\xi\in\Lambda^{2} and l=1,…,n−1l=1,\ldots,n-1, then ξ=±η\xi=\pm\eta. This implies that the action of each element of Gal⁡(ℂ/ℚ)\operatorname{Gal}({\mathbb{C}}/{\mathbb{Q}}) either fixes all the coefficients of η\eta or multiplies each of them by −1-1. The complex numbers preserved by the action of the whole group Gal⁡(ℂ/ℚ)\operatorname{Gal}({\mathbb{C}}/{\mathbb{Q}}) are exactly the rationals (a proof of this well-known fact can be easily extracted e.g. from [Yale]). Therefore the square of each coefficient of η\eta lies in ℚ{\mathbb{Q}}. This easily implies that there exists a positive r∈ℚr\in{\mathbb{Q}} such that the field ℚ⁡[r]{\mathbb{Q}}[\sqrt{r}] contains all the coefficients of η\eta.
If all elements Gal⁡(ℂ/ℚ)\operatorname{Gal}({\mathbb{C}}/{\mathbb{Q}}) fix all the coefficients of η\eta, we get that η∈Λℚ2\eta\in\Lambda^{2}_{\mathbb{Q}}, in contradiction to the hypothesis of the lemma. Therefore there exists an element of Gal⁡(ℂ/ℚ)\operatorname{Gal}({\mathbb{C}}/{\mathbb{Q}}) whose action multiplies all the coefficients of η\eta by −1-1, meaning that all these coefficients are rational multiples of r\sqrt{r}. Therefore, η\eta is a positive real multiple of a form in Λℚ2\Lambda^{2}_{\mathbb{Q}}, which contradicts again the hypothesis of the lemma. Thus, the assumption that ηl\eta^{l} is a real multiple of a form in Λℚ2\Lambda^{2}_{\mathbb{Q}} has led us to a contradiction, which means that it is false. This proves the lemma.
Since
ω¯{\overline{\omega}} is not a real multiple of a form in Λℚ2\Lambda^{2}_{\mathbb{Q}}, 10.1 yields that for each l=1,…,n−1l=1,\ldots,n-1 the form ω¯l∈Λ2​l{\overline{\omega}}^{l}\in\Lambda^{2l} is not a real multiple of a form in Λℚ2​l\Lambda^{2l}_{\mathbb{Q}}.
Let a∈H2​l​(𝕋2​n,ℚ)a\in H^{2l}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n};{\mathbb{Q}}), a≠0a\neq 0, l=1,…,n−1l=1,\ldots,n-1, and let us view a∈H2​l​(𝕋2​n,ℚ)a\in H^{2l}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n};{\mathbb{Q}}) as an exterior 2​l2l-form on ℝ2​n{\mathbb{R}}^{2n} with rational coefficients: a∈Λℚ2​la\in\Lambda^{2l}_{\mathbb{Q}}. We also view 𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω){\it Lin}(\omega) as the set of linear complex structures on ℝ2​n{\mathbb{R}}^{2n} compatible with ω¯{\overline{\omega}}.
The set 𝒯a∩𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω){\mathcal{T}}_{a}\cap{\it Lin}(\omega) is a complex subvariety of
the complex manifold 𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω){\it Lin}(\omega).
We claim that this complex subvariety is proper.
Indeed, assume, by contradiction, that it is not. Then, since 𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω){\it Lin}(\omega) is connected, 𝒯a∩𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω)=𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω){\mathcal{T}}_{a}\cap{\it Lin}(\omega)={\it Lin}(\omega),
meaning that any linear complex structure on ℝ2​n{\mathbb{R}}^{2n} compatible with ω¯{\overline{\omega}} also preserves the exterior 2​l2l-form aa.
Denote by G⊂S​L​(2​n,ℝ)G\subset SL(2n,{\mathbb{R}}) the group of linear automorphisms of ℝ2​n{\mathbb{R}}^{2n} preserving ω¯{\overline{\omega}}. It is isomorphic, as a real Lie group, to S​p​(2​n,ℝ)Sp(2n,{\mathbb{R}}). The set 𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω){\it Lin}(\omega) is invariant under conjugations in GG and thus generates a normal subgroup HH of GG. But G≅S​p​(2​n,ℝ)G\cong Sp(2n,{\mathbb{R}}) is a simple Lie group and therefore each normal
subgroup of GG has to either lie in its center (equal to {I​d,−I​d}\{Id,-Id\}) or coincide with the whole GG (see e.g. [Rag]). The former option is clearly not satisfied for HH and therefore we get that H=GH=G, meaning that aa is preserved by the whole group GG. By a theorem of Weyl [Weyl, Ch. VI, Sec. 1] (see e.g. [GV, Sect. 5.3.2] for a modern presentation), the only exterior 2​l2l-forms preserved by the whole GG are the real multiples of ω¯2​l{\overline{\omega}}^{2l}. Thus, a∈Λℚ2​la\in\Lambda^{2l}_{\mathbb{Q}} is a real multiple of ω¯2​l{\overline{\omega}}^{2l} which, as we have seen above, is a not a real multiple of any form with rational coefficients. We have obtained a contradiction, and this proves the claim that
𝒯a∩𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω){\mathcal{T}}_{a}\cap{\it Lin}(\omega) is a proper complex subvariety of
of the complex manifold 𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω){\it Lin}(\omega).
The claim we have proved implies that the
complement in the connected complex manifold 𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω){\it Lin}(\omega) of the countable union of proper complex subvarieties 𝒯a∩𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω){\mathcal{T}}_{a}\cap{\it Lin}(\omega), a∈H2​l​(𝕋2​n,ℚ)a\in H^{2l}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n};{\mathbb{Q}}), a≠0a\neq 0, l=1,…,n−1l=1,\ldots,n-1, is connected and dense in 𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω){\it Lin}(\omega).
Any complex structure JJ in this complement is Campana-simple. Indeed, the 2​l2l-dimensional complex subvarieties of (𝕋2​n,J)({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},J), l=1,…,n−1l=1,\ldots,n-1, all have non-zero rational fundamental classes; the corresponding Poincaré-dual non-zero rational cohomology classes are of type (l,l)(l,l) (see e.g. [Voi, Vol. 1, Sec. 11]), which is impossible since J∉𝒯aJ\notin{\mathcal{T}}_{a} for all a∈H2​l​(𝕋2​n,ℚ)a\in H^{2l}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n};{\mathbb{Q}}), a≠0a\neq 0. Therefore (𝕋2​n,J)({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},J) has no complex subvarieties of dimension 2​l2l, l=1,…,n−1l=1,\ldots,n-1, meaning that JJ is Campana-simple.
Thus, the set 𝐿𝑖𝑛s∩𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω){\it Lin}^{s}\cap{\it Lin}(\omega) of Campana-simple complex structures lying in 𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω){\it Lin}(\omega) contains a subset which is connected and dense in 𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω){\it Lin}(\omega). Hence, 𝐿𝑖𝑛s∩𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω){\it Lin}^{s}\cap{\it Lin}(\omega) is also connected and dense in 𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω){\it Lin}(\omega).
This finishes the proof.
Since 𝐿𝑖𝑛{\it Lin} is connected, there is a connected component of 𝐶𝐾⁡(𝕋2​n)\mathit{CK}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n}) containing it – denote this connected component by 𝒞l{\mathcal{C}}_{l}.
Let x=0∈𝕋2​n=ℝ2​n/ℤ2​nx=0\in{\mathbb{T}}^{2n}={\mathbb{R}}^{2n}/{\mathbb{Z}}^{2n}. Let Diff⁡(𝕋2​n,x)\mathrm{Diff}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},x) be a subgroup of Diff⁡(𝕋2​n)\mathrm{Diff}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n}) formed by the diffeomorphisms of 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n} fixing xx. Let Diff0​(𝕋2​n,x)\mathrm{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},x) be the identity component of Diff⁡(𝕋2​n,x)\mathrm{Diff}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},x). The group Diff0​(𝕋2​n,x)\mathrm{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},x) lies in Diff0​(𝕋2​n)\mathrm{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n}) and is, in fact, the identity component of the subgroup of
Diff0​(𝕋2​n)\mathrm{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n}) formed by the elements of Diff0​(𝕋2​n)\mathrm{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n}) fixing xx.
Let DiffH​(𝕋2​n,x):=DiffH​(𝕋2​n)∩Diff⁡(𝕋2​n,x)\mathrm{Diff}_{H}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},x):=\mathrm{Diff}_{H}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n})\cap\mathrm{Diff}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},x). Clearly, Diff0​(𝕋2​n,x)⊂DiffH​(𝕋2​n,x)\mathrm{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},x)\subset\mathrm{Diff}_{H}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},x).
Proposition 10.7
Let I∈𝐶𝐾⁡(𝕋2​n)I\in\mathit{CK}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n}).
Then the following claims hold:
A. There exist unique JI∈𝐿𝑖𝑛J_{I}\in{\it Lin} and ϕI∈DiffH​(𝕋2​n,x)\phi_{I}\in\mathrm{Diff}_{H}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},x) so that JI=ϕI∗​IJ_{I}=\phi_{I}^{*}I.
B. The complex structure JIJ_{I} and the diffeomorphism ϕI\phi_{I} depend
continuously on II.
C. If I∈𝒞lI\in{\mathcal{C}}_{l}, then ϕI∈Diff0​(𝕋2​n,x)\phi_{I}\in\mathrm{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},x).
D. If I∈𝒞lI\in{\mathcal{C}}_{l} and ψ∗​I∈𝐿𝑖𝑛\psi^{*}I\in{\it Lin} for some ψ∈Diff0​(𝕋2​n)\psi\in\mathrm{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n}), then ψ∗​I=I\psi^{*}I=I.
Proof of 10.1:
Let us prove part A of the proposition.
The proof of the existence of ϕI\phi_{I} and JIJ_{I} goes as in the proof of [EV1, Prop. 6.1].
Namely, recall the construction of the Albanese map: Let ΩI1​(𝕋2​n)\Omega_{I}^{1}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n}) be the space of holomorphic complex-valued holomorphic 1-forms on the complex
manifold (𝕋2​n,I)({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},I). It is a complex vector space of dimension nn that can be identified with HI1,0​(𝕋2​n,ℂ)H_{I}^{1,0}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n};{\mathbb{C}}). Pick a basis
ς1,…,ςn\varsigma_{1},\ldots,\varsigma_{n} of ΩI1​(𝕋2​n)\Omega_{I}^{1}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n}). The Albanese map AlbI:𝕋2​n→ℂn/ΓI{{\rm Alb}\,}_{I}:{\mathbb{T}}^{2n}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{n}/\Gamma_{I}
is defined by the map y↦(∫γς1,…,∫γςn)y\mapsto\left(\int_{\gamma}\varsigma_{1},\ldots,\int_{\gamma}\varsigma_{n}\right), where γ\gamma is an arbitrary smooth path from x=0x=0 to yy and ΓI\Gamma_{I} is a lattice in ℂn{\mathbb{C}}^{n} formed by the vectors (∫γς1,…,∫γςn)\left(\int_{\gamma}\varsigma_{1},\ldots,\int_{\gamma}\varsigma_{n}\right)
for all the loops γ\gamma. The Albanese map AlbI{{\rm Alb}\,}_{I} is a biholomorphism between (𝕋2​n,I)({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},I) and the complex torus ℂn/ΓI{\mathbb{C}}^{n}/\Gamma_{I} with
the complex structure induced by the standard complex structure on ℂn{\mathbb{C}}^{n} – see e.g. [Voi, Vol. 1, Def. 12.10 and Thm.
12.15].
Denote by πI:ℂn→ℂn/Γ\pi_{I}:{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{n}/\Gamma the natural projection
and let FI:ℝ2​n→ℂnF_{I}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{n} be an ℝ{\mathbb{R}}-linear isomorphism of
vector spaces mapping ℤ2​n{\mathbb{Z}}^{2n} to ΓI\Gamma_{I}. Then FIF_{I} covers a
diffeomorphism f¯I:T2​n=ℝ2​n/ℤ2​n→ℂn/ΓI{\bar{f}}_{I}:T^{2n}={\mathbb{R}}^{2n}/{\mathbb{Z}}^{2n}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{n}/\Gamma_{I},
i.e., f¯I∘πI=πI∘FI{\bar{f}}_{I}\circ\pi_{I}=\pi_{I}\circ F_{I}.
One easily sees that FIF_{I} can be chosen in such a way that f¯I{\bar{f}}_{I} induces the same map H∗​(ℝ2​n/ℤ2​n)→H∗​(ℂn/Γ)H^{*}({\mathbb{R}}^{2n}/{\mathbb{Z}}^{2n})\to H^{*}({\mathbb{C}}^{n}/\Gamma)
as AlbI{{\rm Alb}\,}_{I}.
Clearly, f¯I∗​I{\bar{f}}_{I}^{*}I is a linear complex structure on
T2​n=ℝ2​n/ℤ2​nT^{2n}={\mathbb{R}}^{2n}/{\mathbb{Z}}^{2n}. Therefore ϕI:=f¯I−1∘AlbI\phi_{I}:={\bar{f}}_{I}^{-1}\circ{{\rm Alb}\,}_{I} is a
diffeomorphism mapping II to a linear complex structure JIJ_{I} on
T2​nT^{2n}, acting as identity on H∗​(T2​n)H^{*}(T^{2n}) and sending x=0x=0 to itself.
Thus, ϕI∈DiffH​(𝕋2​n,x)\phi_{I}\in\mathrm{Diff}_{H}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},x). This proves the existence of JIJ_{I} and ϕI\phi_{I}.
Let us prove the uniqueness of JIJ_{I} and ϕI\phi_{I}. It suffices to show that if J0,J1∈𝐿𝑖𝑛J_{0},J_{1}\in{\it Lin} and ϕ∗​J0=J1\phi^{*}J_{0}=J_{1} for some ϕ∈DiffH​(𝕋2​n,x)\phi\in\mathrm{Diff}_{H}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},x), then J0=J1J_{0}=J_{1} and ϕ=I​d\phi=Id.
Denote the lifts of J0,J1J_{0},J_{1} to ℝ2​n{\mathbb{R}}^{2n} respectively by J¯0{\overline{J}}_{0}, J¯1{\overline{J}}_{1}.
The diffeomorphism ϕ:𝕋2​n→𝕋2​n\phi:{\mathbb{T}}^{2n}\to{\mathbb{T}}^{2n} lifts to a diffeomorphism ϕ¯:ℝ2​n→ℝ2​n{\bar{\phi}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2n} such that ϕ¯∗​J¯0=J¯1{\bar{\phi}}^{*}{\overline{J}}_{0}={\overline{J}}_{1} and for each v∈ℤ2​nv\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2n} there exists v′∈ℤ2​nv^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2n} so that ϕ¯​(x+v)=ϕ¯​(x)+v′{\bar{\phi}}(x+v)={\bar{\phi}}(x)+v^{\prime} for all x∈ℤ2​nx\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2n}.
The group G​L+​(2​n,ℝ)GL^{+}(2n,{\mathbb{R}}) acts transitively on 𝐿𝑖𝑛{\it Lin} (see e.g. [McDS], Prop. 2.5.2). Therefore there exists
F∈G​L+​(2​n,ℝ)F\in GL^{+}(2n,{\mathbb{R}})
so that F∗​J¯1=J¯0F^{*}{\overline{J}}_{1}={\overline{J}}_{0}. Then Γ:=F⁡(ℤ2​n)\Gamma:=F({\mathbb{Z}}^{2n}) is a lattice in ℝ2​n{\mathbb{R}}^{2n} and F​ϕ¯:ℝ2​n→ℝ2​nF{\bar{\phi}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2n} is a diffeomorphism preserving J^0{\hat{J}}_{0} so that for each v∈ℤ2​nv\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2n} there exists v′′∈Γv^{\prime\prime}\in\Gamma so that F​ϕ¯​(x+v)=F​ϕ¯​(x)+v′′F{\bar{\phi}}(x+v)=F{\bar{\phi}}(x)+v^{\prime\prime} for all x∈ℤ2​nx\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2n}.
Using an appropriate linear change of coordinates on ℝ2​n{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}, we can assume without loss of generality that J¯0{\overline{J}}_{0} is the standard linear complex structure on ℝ2​n{\mathbb{R}}^{2n} coming from the identification ℝ2​n≅ℂn{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}\cong{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
and thus F​ϕ¯:ℂn→ℂnF{\bar{\phi}}:{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{n} is a holomorphic map. The partial derivatives of this map with respect to the complex coordinates on ℂn{\mathbb{C}}^{n} are ℤ2​n{\mathbb{Z}}^{2n}-invariant and holomorphic, hence descend to holomorphic maps on the standard complex torus 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n}, hence are constant. Therefore F​ϕ¯:ℂn→ℂnF{\bar{\phi}}:{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{n} is a complex-linear affine map and consequently ϕ¯{\bar{\phi}} is of the form ϕ¯​(x)=A​x+c{\bar{\phi}}(x)=Ax+c for some A∈S​L​(2​n,ℤ)A\in SL(2n,{\mathbb{Z}}), c∈ℝ2​nc\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}.
Since ϕ\phi acts trivially on H∗​(𝕋2​n)H_{*}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n}), the matrix AA is just the identity and ϕ¯{\bar{\phi}} is just a parallel translation by cc. Hence, ϕ\phi is a parallel translation too, and since ϕ\phi preserves xx, we have ϕ=I​d\phi=Id. Consequently J0=J1J_{0}=J_{1}.
This proves the uniqueness of JIJ_{I} and ϕI\phi_{I} and finishes the proof of part A.
Part B follows from 13.1
and from the construction of JIJ_{I} and ϕI\phi_{I} in part A.
Let us prove part C. Assume I∈𝒞lI\in{\mathcal{C}}_{l}. Then it can be connected to some J∈𝐿𝑖𝑛J\in{\it Lin} by a continuous path {It}⊂𝒞l\{I_{t}\}\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{l}. Considering the path {ϕIt}⊂DiffH​(𝕋2​n,x)\{\phi_{I_{t}}\}\subset\mathrm{Diff}_{H}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},x), which is
continuous
by part B, and noting that ϕJ=I​d\phi_{J}=Id, we immediately obtain that ϕI∈Diff0​(𝕋2​n,x)\phi_{I}\in\mathrm{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},x). This proves part C.
Let us prove part D. If I∈𝒞lI\in{\mathcal{C}}_{l} and ψ∗​I∈𝐿𝑖𝑛\psi^{*}I\in{\it Lin} for some ψ∈Diff0​(M)\psi\in\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M), then precomposing ψ\psi with a parallel translation, which is a biholomorphism of (𝕋2​n,I)({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},I), we can assume without loss
of generality that ψ⁡(x)=x\psi(x)=x. Thus, ψ∈DiffH​(𝕋2​n,x)\psi\in\mathrm{Diff}_{H}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},x). By part A, this means that ψ∗​I=JI=I\psi^{*}I=J_{I}=I.
This proves part D and finishes the proof of the proposition.
Proposition 10.8
The restriction of the projection 𝒞l→𝒞l/Diff0​(𝕋2​n)=:𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞l​(𝕋2​n){\mathcal{C}}_{l}\to{\mathcal{C}}_{l}/\mathrm{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n})=:{\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{l}}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n}) to 𝐿𝑖𝑛{\it Lin} is a homeomorphism 𝐿𝑖𝑛→𝒞l/Diff0​(𝕋2​n)=𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞l​(𝕋2​n){\it Lin}\to{\mathcal{C}}_{l}/\mathrm{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n})={\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{l}}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n})
that identifies 𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω)⊂𝒞l{\it Lin}(\omega)\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{l} with 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞l​(𝕋2​n,ω)⊂𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞l​(𝕋2​n){\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{l}}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},\omega)\subset{\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{l}}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n}).
Proof of 10.1:
Parts A and C of 10.1
imply that every orbit of the Diff0​(𝕋2​n)\mathrm{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n})-action on 𝒞l{\mathcal{C}}_{l} intersects 𝐿𝑖𝑛{\it Lin}. By part D of 10.1, it intersects 𝐿𝑖𝑛{\it Lin} at exactly one point.
Thus, the map 𝐿𝑖𝑛→𝒞l/Diff0​(𝕋2​n){\it Lin}\to{\mathcal{C}}_{l}/\mathrm{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n}), associating to each I∈𝐿𝑖𝑛I\in{\it Lin} its Diff0​(𝕋2​n)\mathrm{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n})-orbit, is well-defined and bijective. Clearly, it is also continuous.
By part B of 10.1,
the map 𝒞l→𝐿𝑖𝑛{\mathcal{C}}_{l}\to{\it Lin}, I↦JII\mapsto J_{I}, is continuous. This means that the inverse of the map 𝐿𝑖𝑛→𝒞l/Diff0​(𝕋2​n){\it Lin}\to{\mathcal{C}}_{l}/\mathrm{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n}) is continuous too.
Thus, the projection 𝐿𝑖𝑛→𝒞l/Diff0​(𝕋2​n){\it Lin}\to{\mathcal{C}}_{l}/\mathrm{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n}) is a homeomorphism.
Clearly, the image of 𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω){\it Lin}(\omega) under this homeomorphism lies in 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞l​(𝕋2​n,ω){\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{l}}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},\omega). Let us show that it coincides with 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞l​(𝕋2​n,ω){\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{l}}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},\omega), or, equivalently, that for any complex structure I∈𝒞lI\in{\mathcal{C}}_{l} compatible with ω\omega we have JI∈𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω)J_{I}\in{\it Lin}(\omega).
Indeed,
since ω\omega is compatible with II, the cohomology class [ω][\omega] is Kähler with respect to II, hence also with respect to JIJ_{I}, because JIJ_{I} obtained from II by the action of Diff0​(𝕋2​n)\mathrm{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n}).
Now
10.1
implies
that JI∈𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω)J_{I}\in{\it Lin}(\omega). Consequently, the homeomorphism 𝐿𝑖𝑛→𝒞l/Diff0​(𝕋2​n){\it Lin}\to{\mathcal{C}}_{l}/\mathrm{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n})
identifies 𝐿𝑖𝑛⁡(ω){\it Lin}(\omega) with 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞l​(𝕋2​n,ω){\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{l}}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},\omega).
Proposition 10.9
Let ω\omega be a Kähler-type form on 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n}.
Then SympH​(𝕋2​n,ω)\mathrm{Symp}_{H}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},\omega) acts transitively on the set of connected components of 𝐶𝐾⁡(𝕋2​n)\mathit{CK}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n}) compatible with ω\omega.
Proof of 10.1:
By [EV1, Prop. 6.1], any Kähler-type form on 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n} can be mapped by a diffeomorphism of 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n} acting trivially on H∗​(𝕋2​n)H_{*}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n}) to a linear symplectic form. Thus, we can assume without loss of generality that our Kähler-type form ω\omega is linear.
Let I∈𝐶𝐾⁡(𝕋2​n)I\in\mathit{CK}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n}) be an arbitrary complex structure compatible with ω\omega. By [EV1, Prop. 6.1], there exists a diffeomorphism ϕ\phi of 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n} acting trivially on H∗​(𝕋2​n)H_{*}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n}) so that the complex structure ϕ∗​I\phi^{*}I is linear. The symplectic form ϕ∗​ω\phi^{*}\omega is then compatible with ϕ∗​I\phi^{*}I and has the same cohomology class as ω\omega.
Thus, the cohomology class
[ω][\omega] is Kähler with respect to the linear complex structure ϕ∗​I\phi^{*}I.
Therefore, by 10.1, the linear form
ω\omega itself is Kähler with respect to ϕ∗​I\phi^{*}I.
We have obtained that ϕ∗​ω\phi^{*}\omega and ω\omega are cohomologous and compatible with the same complex structure ϕ∗​I\phi^{*}I. Therefore the straight path in the space of 2-forms connecting these forms is formed by cohomologous symplectic forms. By Moser’s theorem [Mos], this means that there exists ψ∈Diff0​(𝕋2​n)\psi\in\mathrm{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n}) such that
ψ∗​ϕ∗​ω=ω\psi^{*}\phi^{*}\omega=\omega. Thus, ϕ​ψ∈SympH​(𝕋2​n,ω)\phi\psi\in\mathrm{Symp}_{H}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},\omega)
and moreover (ϕ​ψ)∗​I=ψ∗​ϕ∗​I(\phi\psi)^{*}I=\psi^{*}\phi^{*}I lies in the same connected component of 𝐶𝐾⁡(𝕋2​n)\mathit{CK}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n}) as ϕ∗​I\phi^{*}I – i.e., in 𝒞l{\mathcal{C}}_{l}.
We have proved that any complex structure I∈𝐶𝐾⁡(𝕋2​n)I\in\mathit{CK}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n}) compatible with ω\omega can be mapped by an element of SympH​(𝕋2​n,ω)\mathrm{Symp}_{H}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},\omega) into a complex structure lying in 𝒞l{\mathcal{C}}_{l}. This readily implies the proposition.
Proposition 10.10
Let ω\omega be a Kähler-type symplectic form on 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n}.
Then ω\omega is compatible with a Campana-simple complex structure if and only if
ω\omega is irrational.
In this case for any connected component 𝒞0{\mathcal{C}}_{0} of
𝐶𝐾⁡(𝕋2​n)\mathit{CK}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n}) compatible with ω\omega the set
𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(𝕋2​n,ω)∩𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑s​(𝕋2​n){\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},\omega)\cap{\tt Teich}^{s}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n})
is a dense connected subset of 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(𝕋2​n,ω){\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},\omega).
Proof of 10.1:
Assume that ω\omega is rational.
Then, by part 1 of
9, there are no Campana-simple complex structures compatible with ω\omega.
By [EV1, Prop. 6.1], any Kähler-type form on 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n} can be mapped by a diffeomorphism of 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n} to a linear symplectic form.
Thus, we can assume without loss of generality that our Kähler-type form ω\omega is linear.
In view of 10.1, we can also assume without loss of generality
that 𝒞0=𝒞l{\mathcal{C}}_{0}={\mathcal{C}}_{l}. Now the proposition follows from
10.1,
10.1.
10.2 Campana-simple complex structures on K3 surfaces
Let MM be a smooth manifold underlying a K3 surface.
The space 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑⁡(M){\tt Teich}(M) has the structure of a complex manifold (see e.g. [Cat]).
More
precisely, a combination of the fundamental theorems of
Kuranishi [Ku1] and Bogomolov-Tian-Todorov
[Bo2, Tia, Tod] shows that for any J0∈𝐶𝐾⁡(M)J_{0}\in\mathit{CK}(M) the Kuranishi
space of (M,J0)(M,J_{0}) (the base of a certain universal complex-analytic local
deformation family of a complex structure defined in [Ku1]) is
a smooth complex manifold as long as the canonical bundle of
(M,J0)(M,J_{0}) is trivial, which, of course, is true in the hyperkähler
case, and in particular, for K3 surfaces. Moreover (see e.g. [Cat]), there exists a
homeomorphism between a neighborhood
𝚄[J0]{\tt{U}}_{[J_{0}]}
of [J0][J_{0}] in 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑⁡(M){\tt Teich}(M)
and an open subset of the Kuranishi space of (M,J0)(M,J_{0}). This homeomorphism maps
each [J]∈𝚄[J0][J]\in{\tt{U}}_{[J_{0}]} to the point
xx of the Kuranishi space
corresponding to a complex structure on MM representing [J][J].
The pullback of
the universal complex-analytic local deformation family over the Kuranishi space
under the homeomorphism, together with a smooth trivialization of the universal fibration over a neighborhood of xx in the Kuranishi space, induces a complex-analytic deformation family M×𝚄[J0]→𝚄[J0]M\times{\tt{U}}_{[J_{0}]}\to{\tt{U}}_{[J_{0}]}.
The
smooth and the complex structures on 𝚄[J0]{\tt{U}}_{[J_{0}]} are the pullbacks of the
corresponding structures on the Kuranishi space.
Remark 10.11
Note that the complex manifold 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑⁡(M){\tt Teich}(M) is not Hausdorff (see [At1], [BurR, p.238], cf. [Sal]).
The description above also implies that for any J∈𝐶𝐾⁡(M)J\in\mathit{CK}(M)
any smooth path in 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑⁡(M){\tt Teich}(M) starting at [J][J] can be lifted to a smooth path in 𝐶𝐾⁡(M)\mathit{CK}(M) starting at JJ.
Let q:H2​(M,ℂ)×H2​(M,ℂ)→ℂq:H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{C}})\times H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathbb{C}} be the intersection form. Its restriction to H2​(M,ℝ)H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}}) is a real-valued symmetric form of signature (3,19)(3,19).
A subspace of H2​(M,ℝ)H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}}) is called positive if the restriction of qq to it is positive. We use ⊥\bot to denote the orthogonal complement with respect to qq in H2​(M,ℝ)H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}}).
Let I∈𝐶𝐾⁡(M)I\in\mathit{CK}(M). Then
dimℂHI2,0​(M,ℂ)=dimℂHI0,2​(M,ℂ)=1,dimℂHI1,1​(M,ℂ)=20\dim_{\mathbb{C}}H_{I}^{2,0}(M;{\mathbb{C}})=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}H_{I}^{0,2}(M;{\mathbb{C}})=1,\ \dim_{\mathbb{C}}H_{I}^{1,1}(M;{\mathbb{C}})=20
(see e.g. [Huyb]). The spaces HI2,0​(M,ℝ)⊕HI0,2​(M,ℝ)H_{I}^{2,0}(M;{\mathbb{R}})\oplus H_{I}^{0,2}(M;{\mathbb{R}}) and HI1,1​(M,ℝ)H^{1,1}_{I}(M;{\mathbb{R}}) are orthogonal with respect to qq.
If Ω\Omega is a holomorphic 22-form spanning HI2,0​(M,ℂ)H^{2,0}_{I}(M;{\mathbb{C}}), then (HI2,0​(M,ℂ)⊕HI0,2​(M,ℂ))∩H2​(M,ℝ)\left(H_{I}^{2,0}(M;{\mathbb{C}})\oplus H_{I}^{0,2}(M;{\mathbb{C}})\right)\cap H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}})
is the 22-dimensional plane spanned by Re⁡Ω\operatorname{Re}\Omega and Im⁡Ω\operatorname{Im}\Omega. Denote this plane by WIW_{I}. The basis {Re⁡Ω,Im⁡Ω}\{\operatorname{Re}\Omega,\operatorname{Im}\Omega\} defines an orientation of the plane WIW_{I}. We have WI⊥=HI1,1​(M,ℂ)∩H2​(M,ℝ)W_{I}^{\bot}=H_{I}^{1,1}(M;{\mathbb{C}})\cap H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}}).
The restriction of qq to WIW_{I} is positive and the restriction of qq to its orthogonal complement HI1,1​(M,ℝ)H_{I}^{1,1}(M;{\mathbb{R}}) in H2​(M,ℝ)H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}}) has signature (1,19)(1,19). Consequently, the set {a∈HI1,1​(M,ℝ)|q⁡(a,a)>0}\{a\in H_{I}^{1,1}(M;{\mathbb{R}})\ |\ q(a,a)>0\} has two connected components: the one that contains the Kähler cone of (M,I)(M,I) is called the positive cone of (M,I)(M,I) and will be denoted Pos⁡(M,I){\rm{Pos}\,}(M,I), and the other one
