Lemma 11.10
The set SS is dense in 𝑀𝑎𝑡2​n​(ℝ){\it Mat}_{2n}({\mathbb{R}}).
Proof of 11:
If ZZ has the form
(ABCD),\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix},
where A,B,C,D∈𝑀𝑎𝑡n​(ℝ)A,B,C,D\in{\it Mat}_{n}({\mathbb{R}}), then
λ​Zt​(0In−In0)​Z=(At​C−Ct​AAt​D−Ct​BBt​C−Dt​ABt​D−Dt​B).\lambda Z^{t}\begin{pmatrix}0&I_{n}\\
-I_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}Z=\begin{pmatrix}A^{t}C-C^{t}A&A^{t}D-C^{t}B\\
B^{t}C-D^{t}A&B^{t}D-D^{t}B\end{pmatrix}.
Since n>1n>1, the set of Z∈𝑀𝑎𝑡2​n​(ℝ)Z\in{\it Mat}_{2n}({\mathbb{R}}) such that A,C∈𝑀𝑎𝑡n​(ℚ)A,C\in{\it Mat}_{n}({\mathbb{Q}}) and Bt​D−Dt​B∉𝑀𝑎𝑡n​(ℚ)B^{t}D-D^{t}B\notin{\it Mat}_{n}({\mathbb{Q}})
is dense in 𝑀𝑎𝑡2​n​(ℝ){\it Mat}_{2n}({\mathbb{R}}) and also lies in SS. Thus, SS is dense in 𝑀𝑎𝑡2​n​(ℝ){\it Mat}_{2n}({\mathbb{R}}).
This proves the lemma.
Since S⊂𝑀𝑎𝑡2​n​(ℝ)S\subset{\it Mat}_{2n}({\mathbb{R}}) is a dense set,
it suffices to prove the corollary only in the case when Z⁡(e1,e2,…,e2​n)Z(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{2n}) lies in SS.
Indeed, if Vol⁡(λ​P​(e1,…,e2​n),ω0)<Vol⁡(𝕋2​n,ω){\rm{Vol}\,}\left(\lambda P\left(e_{1},\ldots,e_{2n}\right),\omega_{0}\right)<{\rm{Vol}\,}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},\omega) for some λ>0\lambda>0 and some Kähler-type form ω\omega on 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n},
then there exists a basis e1′,…,e2​n′e^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,e^{\prime}_{2n} of ℝ2​n{\mathbb{R}}^{2n} such that Z⁡(e1′,e2′,…,e2​n′)∈SZ(e^{\prime}_{1},e^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,e^{\prime}_{2n})\in S,
the parallelepiped P⁡(e1′,…,e2​n′)P(e^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,e^{\prime}_{2n})
contains the parallelepiped P⁡(e1,…,e2​n)P(e_{1},\ldots,e_{2n}) and has only slightly bigger volume than it, so that
Vol⁡(λ​P​(e1′,…,e2​n′),ω0)<Vol⁡(𝕋2​n,ω){\rm{Vol}\,}(\lambda P(e^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,e^{\prime}_{2n}),\omega_{0})<{\rm{Vol}\,}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},\omega). If λ​P​(e1′,…,e2​n′)\lambda P(e^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,e^{\prime}_{2n}) admits a Kähler-type, or respectively an ε\varepsilon-tame, embedding into (𝕋2​n,ω)({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},\omega), then the composition of this embedding with the inclusion
λ​P​(e1,…,e2​n)→λ​P​(e1′,…,e2​n′)\lambda P(e_{1},\ldots,e_{2n})\to\lambda P(e^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,e^{\prime}_{2n}) is a Kähler-type, or respectively an ε\varepsilon-tame, embedding of
λ​P​(e1,…,e2​n)\lambda P(e_{1},\ldots,e_{2n}) into (𝕋2​n,ω)({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},\omega).
Now, in view of 11, it suffices to show that
for any P⁡(e1,…,e2​n)P(e_{1},\ldots,e_{2n}), such that Z⁡(e1,e2,…,e2​n)∈SZ(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{2n})\in S,
there exists an irrational
Kähler-type symplectic form ω\omega on 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n} satisfying the following condition:
for any λ>0\lambda>0, such that Vol⁡(λ​P​(e1,…,e2​n))<Vol⁡(𝕋2​n,ω){\rm{Vol}\,}\left(\lambda P\left(e_{1},\ldots,e_{2n}\right)\right)<{\rm{Vol}\,}({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},\omega),
there exists a Kähler-type embedding P⁡(e1,…,e2​n)→(𝕋2​n,ω)P(e_{1},\ldots,e_{2n})\to({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},\omega).
Pick P⁡(e1,…,e2​n)P(e_{1},\ldots,e_{2n}), such that Z⁡(e1,e2,…,e2​n)∈SZ(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{2n})\in S and
let us construct such an ω\omega.
Without loss of generality, assume that Vol⁡(λ​P​(e1,…,e2​n),ω0)=1{\rm{Vol}\,}\left(\lambda P\left(e_{1},\ldots,e_{2n}\right),\omega_{0}\right)=1.
Consider the integral lattice Λ:=Spanℤ​{e1,…,e2​n}⊂ℝ2​n\Lambda:={{\rm Span}\,}_{\mathbb{Z}}\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{2n}\}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}. The fundamental domain of Λ\Lambda
is exactly P⁡(e1,…,e2​n)P(e_{1},\ldots,e_{2n}).
The space ℝ2​n/Λ{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}/\Lambda
is a torus and the form ω0\omega_{0} on ℝ2​n{\mathbb{R}}^{2n} induces
a Kähler-type symplectic form on ℝ2​n/Λ{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}/\Lambda of total volume 11 that will be also denoted by ω0\omega_{0}. The matrix Z⁡(e1,e2,…,e2​n)Z(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{2n}) induces a linear map ℝ2​n→ℝ2​n{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2n} mapping ℤ2​n{\mathbb{Z}}^{2n} to Λ\Lambda. This map induces
a diffeomorphism F:𝕋2​n=ℝ2​n/ℤ2​n→ℝ2​n/ΛF:{\mathbb{T}}^{2n}={\mathbb{R}}^{2n}/{\mathbb{Z}}^{2n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}/\Lambda. Since Z⁡(e1,e2,…,e2​n)∈SZ(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{2n})\in S,
the form ω:=F∗​ω0\omega:=F^{*}\omega_{0} is an irrational Kähler-type form on 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n} of total volume 11.
For any 0<λ<10<\lambda<1 the inclusion λ​P​(e1,…,e2​n)→(ℝ2​n/Λ,ω0)\lambda P(e_{1},\ldots,e_{2n})\to({\mathbb{R}}^{2n}/\Lambda,\omega_{0}) is a Kähler-type embedding.
This embedding is holomorphic with respect to the complex structure on ℝ2​n/Λ{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}/\Lambda induced by the standard complex structure J0J_{0} on ℝ2​n{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}. The composition of this embedding with F−1F^{-1}
is then a Kähler-type embedding λ​P​(e1,…,e2​n)→(𝕋2​n,ω)\lambda P(e_{1},\ldots,e_{2n})\to({\mathbb{T}}^{2n},\omega) holomorphic with respect to the linear complex
structure F∗​J0F^{*}J_{0} on 𝕋2​n{\mathbb{T}}^{2n}.
Thus, for any 0<λ<10<\lambda<1 there exists a Kähler-type embedding λ​P​(e1,…,e2​n)→(ℝ2​n/Λ,ω0)\lambda P(e_{1},\ldots,e_{2n})\to({\mathbb{R}}^{2n}/\Lambda,\omega_{0}),
provided Z⁡(e1,e2,…,e2​n)∈SZ(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{2n})\in S.
This finishes the proof of the corollary in the case where 𝕎{\mathbb{W}} is the disjoint union of kk identical copies of
a parallelepiped.
Let us prove 3.4. We recall it here.
Corollary 11.11 (= 3.4)
Assume:
•
MM is a smooth manifold underlying a K3 surface.
•
𝕎:=⨆i=1kWi{\mathbb{W}}:=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}W_{i} is a disjoint union of kk identical copies of a parallelepiped
P(e1,…,e4):={∑j=14sjej,0≤sj≤1,j=1,…,4},P(e_{1},\ldots,e_{4}):=\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{4}s_{j}e_{j},0\leq s_{j}\leq 1,j=1,\ldots,4\right\},
where e1,…,e4e_{1},\ldots,e_{4} is a basis of the vector space ℝ4{\mathbb{R}}^{4}.
•
ε>0\varepsilon>0.
Then there exists a Diff+​(M)\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(M)-invariant open dense set of Kähler-type symplectic forms on MM, depending on 𝕎{\mathbb{W}} and ε\varepsilon and containing, in particular, all irrational Kähler-type symplectic forms on MM,
so that for each ω′\omega^{\prime} in this set
νT,ε​(M,ω′,𝕎)=1\nu_{T,\varepsilon}(M,\omega^{\prime},{\mathbb{W}})=1, meaning that ε\varepsilon-tame embeddings of λ​𝕎\lambda{\mathbb{W}} into (M,ω′)(M,\omega^{\prime}) are unobstructed.
Proof of 11 (=3.4):
Consider the integral lattice Λ:=Spanℤ​{2​e1,2​e2,2​e3,2​e4}⊂ℝ4\Lambda:={{\rm Span}\,}_{\mathbb{Z}}\{2e_{1},2e_{2},2e_{3},2e_{4}\}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{4}. The parallelepiped P⁡(e1,…,e4)P(e_{1},\ldots,e_{4}) is the fundamental domain of the lattice Λ/2\Lambda/2.
Recall the following construction by Kummer of a K3 surface – see e.g. [Bea2, p.55].
Consider the torus 𝕋4:=ℝ4/Λ{\mathbb{T}}^{4}:={\mathbb{R}}^{4}/\Lambda and let π:ℝ4→𝕋4\pi:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{T}}^{4} be the projection.
Equip the torus 𝕋4{\mathbb{T}}^{4} with the complex structure J0J_{0} and the symplectic structure ω0\omega_{0} induced by the standard complex and symplectic structures on ℝ4{\mathbb{R}}^{4}.
The reflection with respect to the origin of ℝ4{\mathbb{R}}^{4} defines an involution of ℝ4{\mathbb{R}}^{4}, which in turn defines a holomorphic and symplectic involution ι:(𝕋4,J0,ω0)→(𝕋4,J0,ω0)\iota:({\mathbb{T}}^{4},J_{0},\omega_{0})\to({\mathbb{T}}^{4},J_{0},\omega_{0}).
The involution ι\iota has 1616 fixed points – these are exactly the points of 𝕋4{\mathbb{T}}^{4} that are the images under π\pi of
the points in ℝ4{\mathbb{R}}^{4} all of whose coordinates with respect to the basis {2​e1,2​e2,…,2​e2​n}\{2e_{1},2e_{2},\ldots,2e_{2n}\} are either 00 or 1/21/2. Let (𝕋~4,J~0)(\tilde{{\mathbb{T}}}^{4},{\widetilde{J}}_{0}) be the complex blow-up of (𝕋4,J0)({\mathbb{T}}^{4},J_{0})
at these 16 points.
The involution ι\iota lifts to a holomorphic involution ι~:𝕋~4→𝕋~4{\widetilde{\iota}}:\tilde{{\mathbb{T}}}^{4}\to\tilde{{\mathbb{T}}}^{4}. Then M:=𝕋~4/ι~M:=\tilde{{\mathbb{T}}}^{4}/{\widetilde{\iota}}, equipped with the
complex structure induced by J~0{\widetilde{J}}_{0}, is
a complex manifold which is, in fact, a K3 surface (called a Kummer surface). Denote by II the complex structure on MM induced by J~0{\widetilde{J}}_{0}.
The complex manifold (M,I)(M,I) can also be obtained as follows: The space 𝕋4/ι{\mathbb{T}}^{4}/\iota is a complex orbifold with 1616 isolated singular orbifold points x1,…,x16x_{1},\ldots,x_{16}. Denote by J¯0{\overline{J}}_{0} the complex structure on 𝕋4/ι{\mathbb{T}}^{4}/\iota induced by J0J_{0}. Then (M,I)(M,I) is the smooth complex manifold obtained by the complex blow-up of (𝕋4/ι,J¯0)({\mathbb{T}}^{4}/\iota,{\overline{J}}_{0}) at x1,…,x16x_{1},\ldots,x_{16}.
Let p​r:𝕋4→𝕋4/ιpr:{\mathbb{T}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{T}}^{4}/\iota and Π:M→𝕋4/ι\Pi:M\to{\mathbb{T}}^{4}/\iota be the natural projections. The form ω0\omega_{0} on 𝕋4{\mathbb{T}}^{4} is ι\iota-invariant and induces an orbifold Kähler form ω¯0{\overline{\omega}}_{0} on the complex orbifold 𝕋4/ι{\mathbb{T}}^{4}/\iota which is a smooth Kähler form outside the singularities.
The points x1,…,x16∈𝕋4/ιx_{1},\ldots,x_{16}\in{\mathbb{T}}^{4}/\iota lie on the boundary of (p​r∘π)​(P⁡(e1,…,e4))(pr\circ\pi)\left(P\left(e_{1},\ldots,e_{4}\right)\right). The interior I​n​t​P​(e1,…,e4)Int\,P(e_{1},\ldots,e_{4}) of P⁡(e1,…,e4)P(e_{1},\ldots,e_{4}) projects diffeomorphically onto its image under p​r∘πpr\circ\pi and is an open dense subset of 𝕋4/ι{\mathbb{T}}^{4}/\iota. Consequently, p​r∘π:I​n​t​P​(e1,…,e4)→𝕋4/ιpr\circ\pi:Int\,P(e_{1},\ldots,e_{4})\to{\mathbb{T}}^{4}/\iota is an embedding which is
holomorphic with respect to J¯0{\overline{J}}_{0} and symplectic with respect to ω¯0{\overline{\omega}}_{0}.
The map
F:=Π−1∘p​r∘π:I​n​t​P​(e1,…,e4)→MF:=\Pi^{-1}\circ pr\circ\pi:Int\,P(e_{1},\ldots,e_{4})\to M is then an II-holomorphic embedding.
Let 0<λ<10<\lambda<1. Move λ​P​(e1,…,e4)\lambda P(e_{1},\ldots,e_{4}) by a parallel translation into the interior of
P⁡(e1,…,e4)P(e_{1},\ldots,e_{4})
and then map it by FF into MM. Denote the resulting map by f:λ​P​(e1,…,e4)→Mf:\lambda P(e_{1},\ldots,e_{4})\to M. It is an II-holomorphic embedding.
Following the proof of [EV2, Thm. 3.6] (or, alternatively, using 5.5), one can find rational cohomology classes bi∈H2​(Π−1​(xi),ℚ)b_{i}\in H^{2}(\Pi^{-1}(x_{i});{\mathbb{Q}}), i=1,…,16i=1,\ldots,16, so that
for any ϵ>0\epsilon>0 and any sufficiently small 0<ϵ1,…,ϵ16<ϵ0<\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{16}<\epsilon, there exist
a Kähler form ωϵ\omega_{\epsilon} on (M,I)(M,I) and sufficiently small neighborhoods Uxi,ϵ,λU_{x_{i},\epsilon,\lambda} of Π−1​(xi)\Pi^{-1}(x_{i}), i=1,…,16i=1,\ldots,16, in MM
with the following properties:
•
Uxi,ϵ,λ∩f⁡(λ​P​(e1,…,e4))=∅U_{x_{i},\epsilon,\lambda}\cap f\left(\lambda P\left(e_{1},\ldots,e_{4}\right)\right)=\emptyset, i=1,…,16i=1,\ldots,16.
•
The form ωϵ\omega_{\epsilon} coincides with Π∗​ω¯0\Pi^{*}{\overline{\omega}}_{0} outside ⋃i=116Uxi,ϵ,λ\bigcup_{i=1}^{16}U_{x_{i},\epsilon,\lambda}.
•
[ωϵ]=Π∗​[ω¯0]+∑i=116ϵi​bi[\omega_{\epsilon}]=\Pi^{*}[{\overline{\omega}}_{0}]+\sum_{i=1}^{16}\epsilon_{i}b_{i}.
•
Vol⁡(𝕋4/ι,ω¯0)>(1−ϵ)​Vol​(M,ωϵ){\rm{Vol}\,}({\mathbb{T}}^{4}/\iota,{\overline{\omega}}_{0})>(1-\epsilon){\rm{Vol}\,}(M,\omega_{\epsilon}).
If the vector (ϵ1,…,ϵ16)∈ℝ16(\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{16})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{16} is not a real multiple of a vector in ℚ16{\mathbb{Q}}^{16}, the cohomology class
[ωϵ]=Π∗​[ω¯0]+∑i=116ϵi​bi∈H2​(M,ℝ)[\omega_{\epsilon}]=\Pi^{*}[{\overline{\omega}}_{0}]+\sum_{i=1}^{16}\epsilon_{i}b_{i}\in H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}})
is a not a real multiple of a rational cohomology class, meaning that ωϵ\omega_{\epsilon} is irrational.
Our construction implies that f:λ​P​(e1,…,e4)→(M,ωϵ)f:\lambda P(e_{1},\ldots,e_{4})\to(M,\omega_{\epsilon}) is a Kähler-type embedding which is holomorphic with respect to II and
Vol⁡(λ​P​(e1,…,e4),ω0)=λ4​Vol​(P⁡(e1,…,e4),ω0)={\rm{Vol}\,}\left(\lambda P(e_{1},\ldots,e_{4}),\omega_{0}\right)=\lambda^{4}{\rm{Vol}\,}\left(P(e_{1},\ldots,e_{4}),\omega_{0}\right)=
=λ4​Vol​(𝕋4/ι,ω¯0)>λ4​(1−ϵ)​Vol​(M,ωϵ).=\lambda^{4}{\rm{Vol}\,}({\mathbb{T}}^{4}/\iota,{\overline{\omega}}_{0})>\lambda^{4}(1-\epsilon){\rm{Vol}\,}(M,\omega_{\epsilon}).
For any δ>0\delta>0 we can choose
λ<1\lambda<1 sufficiently close to 11 and ϵ>0\epsilon>0 sufficiently small
so that λ4​(1−ϵ)>1−δ\lambda^{4}(1-\epsilon)>1-\delta.
Then
f:λ​P​(e1,…,e4)→(M,ωϵ)f:\lambda P(e_{1},\ldots,e_{4})\to(M,\omega_{\epsilon})
is a Kähler-type embedding
and
Vol⁡(λ​P​(e1,…,e4),ω0)/Vol⁡(M,ωϵ)≥1−δ.{\rm{Vol}\,}(\lambda P(e_{1},\ldots,e_{4}),\omega_{0})/{\rm{Vol}\,}(M,\omega_{\epsilon})\geq 1-\delta.
By 11, this implies the corollary.
12 Hyperkähler case – proofs
As we mentioned in Section 3.5, the results of Section 3.4
generalize to the IHS-hyperkähler case. In this section we
explain how the proofs of the results in the K3 case should be adjusted for general IHS-hyperkähler manifolds.
Let MM be a closed connected and simply-connected manifold carrying IHS-hyperkähler structures.
Recall from Section 3.5 that in the IHS-hyperkähler case the words “Kähler-type” should be replaced everywhere by the words “IHS-hyperkähler-type”. Accordingly, in this section we will use the same notation as in Section 10.2 for IHS-hyperkähler-type objects as for Kähler-type objects. In particular, 𝐶𝐾⁡(M)\mathit{CK}(M) will denote the space of IHS-hyperkähler-type complex structures, 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑⁡(M)=𝐶𝐾⁡(M)/Diff0​(M){\tt Teich}(M)=\mathit{CK}(M)/\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M) the Teichmüller space of IHS-hyperkähler-type complex structures etc.; all the symplectic forms and complex structures will be assumed to be of IHS-hyperkähler-type.
For general IHS-hyperkähler manifolds:
•
The Teichmüller space 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑⁡(M)=𝐶𝐾⁡(M)/Diff0​(M){\tt Teich}(M)=\mathit{CK}(M)/\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M) has the structure of a (non-Hausdorff) complex manifold for the same reasons as in Section 10.2 (see [Cat]).
•
Instead of the intersection form one should consider the Bogomolov-Beauville-Fujiki (BBF) symmetric bilinear form qq on H2​(M,ℝ)H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}})
(see [Bea1], [Fu]) – for K3 surfaces the intersection form and the BBF form coincide, up to a multiplicative factor. The BBF form is a primitive integral quadratic form of signature
(3,b2−3)(3,b_{2}-3), where b2=dimℝH2​(M,ℝ)>0b_{2}=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}})>0.
The space G​r+⁣+​(H2​(M,ℝ))Gr_{++}(H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}})) is defined then using the BBF form qq.
We will use ⟂\perp to denote orthogonal complements with respect to qq.
•
Part A of the Torelli theorem (10.2) remains true for IHS-hyperkähler manifolds – see [Ver6].
•
In the definition of S⁡(M,I)S(M,I), for I∈𝐶𝐾⁡(M)I\in\mathit{CK}(M), (−2)(-2)-classes should be replaced by the so-called MBM cohomology classes in H2​(M,ℤ)H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{Z}}) defined in [AmV1]. With S⁡(M,I)S(M,I) defined in this way, 10.2 remains true for general IHS-hyperkähler manifolds.
•
The analogues of 10.2 and 10.2 remain true for general IHS-hyperkähler manifolds.
Let ω\omega be an IHS-hyperkähler-type symplectic form on MM.
The set Dω{\it D}_{\omega} is then defined literally as in Section 10.2 with (−2)(-2)-classes being replaced by the MBM classes. 10.2 and 10.2 remain true in this case – in their proofs one should replace
1919 by b2−3b_{2}-3, 1818 by b2−4b_{2}-4, and (−2)(-2)-classes by MBM classes [AmV1].
Next, let us state the analogue of 10.2 in the IHS-hyperkähler case.
Proposition 12.1
The symplectic form ω\omega is compatible with a Campana-simple IHS-hyperkähler-type complex structure on MM if and only if
ω\omega is irrational. In this case, for any connected component 𝒞0{\mathcal{C}}_{0} of
𝐶𝐾⁡(M)\mathit{CK}(M) compatible with ω\omega the set
𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M,ω)∩𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑s​(M){\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M,\omega)\cap{\tt Teich}^{s}(M) of Campana-simple structures lying in 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M,ω){\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M,\omega)
is a dense connected subset of 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M,ω){\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M,\omega).
The proof of 12
that we are going to give will not be a direct generalization of the proof of 10.2 and requires
a number of preliminaries.
Consider an IHS-hyperkähler-type structure 𝔥={I1,I2,I3,ω1,ω2,ω3}{\mathfrak{h}}=\{I_{1},I_{2},I_{3},\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3}\} on MM.
For any a,b,c∈ℝa,b,c\in{\mathbb{R}}, a2+b2+c2=1a^{2}+b^{2}+c^{2}=1, the tensor a​I1+b​I2+c​I3aI_{1}+bI_{2}+cI_{3}
is a complex structure of hyperkähler type on MM. We will call
any such a​I1+b​I2+c​I3aI_{1}+bI_{2}+cI_{3} a complex structure induced by 𝔥{\mathfrak{h}}. Clearly, all complex structures induced by 𝔥{\mathfrak{h}} lie in the same connected
component of 𝐶𝐾⁡(M)\mathit{CK}(M) – we will say that this connected component is favored by 𝔥{\mathfrak{h}}.
Following [Ver2],
we say that a closed subset Z⊂MZ\subset M is
trianalytic (with respect to 𝔥{\mathfrak{h}}), if ZZ
is a complex
subvariety of (M,I)(M,I) for each complex structure II induced by
𝔥{\mathfrak{h}}.
Theorem 12.2 [Ver2, Thm. 4.1]
Let a∈H∗​(M,ℚ)a\in H^{*}(M;{\mathbb{Q}}), a≠0a\neq 0.
Then aa is Poincaré-dual to the fundamental class [Z][Z] of a trianalytic Z⊂MZ\subset M if and only if aa is invariant with respect to the action of any induced complex structure on H∗​(M,ℚ)H^{*}(M;{\mathbb{Q}}).
Let 𝒞0{\mathcal{C}}_{0} be a connected component of 𝐶𝐾⁡(M)\mathit{CK}(M).
Let ZZ be a closed subvariety of MM for a certain (IHS-hyperkähler-type) complex structure on MM lying in 𝒞0{\mathcal{C}}_{0}.
Following [Kur, SolV], we say that ZZ is absolutely trianalytic
(with respect to 𝒞0{\mathcal{C}}_{0})
if the cohomology class Poincaré-dual to the fundamental class of ZZ is
of type (p,p)(p,p) for all complex structures I∈𝒞0I\in{\mathcal{C}}_{0}.
By 12, such a
manifold is trianalytic with respect to any IHS-hyperkähler structure favoring 𝒞0{\mathcal{C}}_{0}.
Given a∈H∗​(M,ℚ)a\in H^{*}(M;{\mathbb{Q}}), a≠0a\neq 0, denote by
𝚃𝒞0,a⊂𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M){\tt T}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0},a}\subset{\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M) the set of all [I]∈𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M)[I]\in{\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M) such that a∈HI1,1​(M,ℝ)a\in H_{I}^{1,1}(M;{\mathbb{R}}).
It follows e.g. from the Torelli theorem for hyperkähler manifolds [Ver6] that 𝚃𝒞0,a{\tt T}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0},a} is an analytic submanifold of
𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M){\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M) and 𝖯𝖾𝗋⁡(𝚃𝒞0,a)=G​r+⁣+​(a⊥)\operatorname{\sf Per}({\tt T}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0},a})=Gr_{++}\left(a^{\bot}\right).
For a real vector space WW equipped with a non-degenerate indefinite
quadratic form we denote by S​O+​(W)SO^{+}(W) the identity component of S​O​(W)SO(W). Similarly, S​O+​(p,q)SO^{+}(p,q) stands for the identity component of the Lie group S​O​(p,q)SO(p,q).
For the proof of 12 we will need the following algebraic lemma.
Lemma 12.3
Let WW be a real vector space equipped with a non-degenerate
quadratic form qq of signature (2,l)(2,l).
Consider the set ℱ⁡(W){\mathcal{F}}(W) of F∈S​O+​(W)F\in SO^{+}(W) fixing pointwise a linear subspace U⊂WU\subset W, dimℝU=dimℝW−2\dim_{\mathbb{R}}U=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}W-2 whose orthogonal complement
is a positive 2-dimensional plane in WW.
Then the set ℱ⁡(W){\mathcal{F}}(W)
generates S​O+​(W)SO^{+}(W).
Proof of 12:
Let NN be the
subgroup of S​O+​(W)SO^{+}(W) generated by ℱ⁡(W){\mathcal{F}}(W).
Clearly, NN is normal.
Assume l≠2l\neq 2.
Then the group
S​O+​(W)SO^{+}(W) is a connected simple Lie group (in the sense of Lie theory). Therefore it follows from [Rag] that any normal subgroup of S​O+​(W)SO^{+}(W) is either
the whole S​O+​(W)SO^{+}(W) or is contained in the center of S​O+​(W)SO^{+}(W) (which is discrete). Hence, N=S​O+​(W)N=SO^{+}(W), meaning that
ℱ⁡(W){\mathcal{F}}(W)
generates S​O+​(W)SO^{+}(W) for l≠2l\neq 2.
Let us consider the case l=2l=2 – i.e., the case when qq has signature (2,2)(2,2).
Identify (W,q)(W,q) with the space 𝑀𝑎𝑡2​(ℝ){\it Mat}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}) of real 2×22\times 2-matrices equipped with the bilinear form ⟨A,B⟩=−t​r​(A​J​B​J)\langle A,B\rangle=-tr(AJBJ), where
J=(0−110)J=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&-1\\
1&0\\
\end{array}\right).
Consider the following action of S​L​(2,ℝ)×S​L​(2,ℝ)SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})\times SL(2,{\mathbb{R}}) on W≅𝑀𝑎𝑡2​(ℝ)W\cong{\it Mat}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}):
each h1×h2∈S​L​(2,ℝ)×S​L​(2,ℝ)h_{1}\times h_{2}\in SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})\times SL(2,{\mathbb{R}}) acts on A∈𝑀𝑎𝑡2​(ℝ)A\in{\it Mat}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}) by
A↦h1​A​h2−1A\mapsto h_{1}Ah_{2}^{-1}.
This action preserves the bilinear form and induces a homomorphism P:S​L​(2,ℝ)×S​L​(2,ℝ)→S​O+​(W)P:SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})\times SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})\to SO^{+}(W)
whose kernel is {±I​d}\{\pm Id\}. The subgroup P−1​(N)⊂S​L​(2,ℝ)×S​L​(2,ℝ)P^{-1}(N)\subset SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})\times SL(2,{\mathbb{R}}) is normal.
The Lie group S​L​(2,ℝ)×S​L​(2,ℝ)SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})\times SL(2,{\mathbb{R}}) is a connected semi-simple Lie group. Therefore it follows from [Rag] that any normal subgroup of S​L​(2,ℝ)×S​L​(2,ℝ)SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})\times SL(2,{\mathbb{R}}) which is not the whole S​L​(2,ℝ)×S​L​(2,ℝ)SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})\times SL(2,{\mathbb{R}}) is contained either in S​L​(2,ℝ)×{±I​d}SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})\times\{\pm Id\} or in
{±I​d}×S​L​(2,ℝ)\{\pm Id\}\times SL(2,{\mathbb{R}}).
Let us show that P−1​(ℱ⁡(W))⊈S​L​(2,ℝ)×{±I​d}P^{-1}\left({\mathcal{F}}(W)\right)\nsubseteq SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})\times\{\pm Id\} and consequently P−1​(N)⊈S​L​(2,ℝ)×{±I​d}P^{-1}(N)\nsubseteq SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})\times\{\pm Id\} (the claim P−1​(N)⊈{±I​d}×S​L​(2,ℝ)P^{-1}(N)\nsubseteq\{\pm Id\}\times SL(2,{\mathbb{R}}) is proved similarly). Indeed, if an element h×I​d∈S​L​(2,ℝ)×S​L​(2,ℝ)h\times Id\in SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})\times SL(2,{\mathbb{R}}) is sent by PP into ℱ⁡(W){\mathcal{F}}(W), then its action on 𝑀𝑎𝑡2​(ℝ){\it Mat}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}) fixes a two-dimensional subspace of 𝑀𝑎𝑡2​(ℝ){\it Mat}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}). This easily implies that h∈S​L​(2,ℝ)h\in SL(2,{\mathbb{R}}) has a two-dimensional eigenspace for the eigenvalue 11, meaning that h=I​dh=Id. Hence, P−1​(ℱ⁡(W))⊈S​L​(2,ℝ)×{±I​d}P^{-1}\left({\mathcal{F}}(W)\right)\nsubseteq SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})\times\{\pm Id\}
and consequently P−1​(N)=S​L​(2,ℝ)×S​L​(2,ℝ)P^{-1}(N)=SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})\times SL(2,{\mathbb{R}}) and N=S​O+​(W)N=SO^{+}(W), meaning that
ℱ⁡(W){\mathcal{F}}(W)
generates S​O+​(W)SO^{+}(W) also for l=2l=2.
This finishes the proof of the lemma.
Now we are ready to prove 12.
Proof of 12:
If the cohomology class [ω][\omega] is rational,
then by part 1 of
9, there are no Campana-simple complex structures compatible with ω\omega.
Assume that ω\omega is irrational and 𝒞0{\mathcal{C}}_{0} is a connected component of
𝐶𝐾⁡(M)\mathit{CK}(M) compatible with ω\omega.
The analogue of 10.2 in the
IHS-hyperkähler case implies that 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M,ω){\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M,\omega) is a dense connected subset of the analytic subvariety
𝚃𝒞0,[ω]{\tt T}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0},[\omega]} of 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M){\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M).
Consequently, for each a∈H∗​(M,ℚ)a\in H^{*}(M;{\mathbb{Q}}), a≠0a\neq 0, there are only two possible options:
(1) 𝚃𝒞0,a∩𝚃𝒞0,[ω]=𝚃𝒞0,[ω]{\tt T}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0},a}\cap{\tt T}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0},[\omega]}={\tt T}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0},[\omega]}, and consequently 𝚃𝒞0,a∩𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M,ω)=𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M,ω){\tt T}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0},a}\cap{\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M,\omega)={\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M,\omega),
(2) 𝚃𝒞0,a∩𝚃𝒞0,[ω]{\tt T}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0},a}\cap{\tt T}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0},[\omega]} is a proper (possibly empty) complex subvariety of 𝚃𝒞0,[ω]{\tt T}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0},[\omega]} and consequently 𝚃𝒞0,a∩𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M,ω){\tt T}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0},a}\cap{\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M,\omega) is a proper complex subvariety of 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M,ω){\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M,\omega).
Lemma 12.4
Assume Z⊂MZ\subset M is a complex subvariety of (M,I)(M,I) (some complex structure I∈𝒞0I\in{\mathcal{C}}_{0}) which is not absolutely trianalytic
with respect to 𝒞0{\mathcal{C}}_{0}. Let a∈He​v​e​n​(M,ℚ)a\in H^{even}(M;{\mathbb{Q}}) be the cohomology class Poincaré-dual to [Z][Z].
Then 𝚃𝒞0,a∩𝚃𝒞0,[ω]⫅̸𝚃𝒞0,[ω]{\tt T}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0},a}\cap{\tt T}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0},[\omega]}\nsubseteqq{\tt T}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0},[\omega]} (meaning that option (2) holds for aa).
Proof of 12:
We start with a number of preparations.
Given I∈𝒞0I\in{\mathcal{C}}_{0} and t∈ℝt\in{\mathbb{R}}, consider the Hodge rotation map H∗​(M,ℂ)→H∗​(M,ℂ)H^{*}(M;{\mathbb{C}})\to H^{*}(M;{\mathbb{C}}) that acts on each HIp,q​(M,ℂ)H_{I}^{p,q}(M;{\mathbb{C}})
as multiplication by e−1​(p−q)​te^{\sqrt{-1}\>(p-q)t}. Such a map preserves H∗​(M,ℝ)H^{*}(M;{\mathbb{R}}) and its fixed point set is exactly the union of HIp,p​(M,ℂ)H_{I}^{p,p}(M;{\mathbb{C}}) for all pp. We will view the action of this map on H∗​(M,ℝ)H^{*}(M;{\mathbb{R}}) as an element of
G​L​(H∗​(M,ℝ))GL\left(H^{*}(M;{\mathbb{R}})\right) and denote it by ρI​(t):H∗​(M,ℝ)→H∗​(M,ℝ)\rho_{I}(t):H^{*}(M;{\mathbb{R}})\to H^{*}(M;{\mathbb{R}}). The restriction of each ρI​(t)\rho_{I}(t) to H2​(M,ℝ)H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}})
preserves the BBF form qq and lies in S​O+​(H2​(M,ℝ))SO^{+}\left(H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}})\right). The maps ρI​(t)|H2​(M,ℝ)\rho_{I}(t)|_{H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}})}, t∈ℝt\in{\mathbb{R}}, are exactly the maps that act trivially on the subspace HI1,1​(M,ℝ)H_{I}^{1,1}(M;{\mathbb{R}})
and rotate its orthogonal complement, which is the positive 2-dimensional plane (HI2,0​(M,ℂ)⊕HI0,2​(M,ℂ))∩H2​(M,ℝ)(H_{I}^{2,0}(M;{\mathbb{C}})\oplus H_{I}^{0,2}(M;{\mathbb{C}}))\cap H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}}).
Denote by G⊂G​L​(He​v​e​n​(M,ℝ))G\subset GL\left(H^{even}(M;{\mathbb{R}})\right) the
subgroup generated by
ρI​(t)|He​v​e​n​(M,ℝ)\rho_{I}(t)|_{H^{even}(M;{\mathbb{R}})} for all I∈𝒞0I\in{\mathcal{C}}_{0} and t∈ℝt\in{\mathbb{R}}. The
restriction to H2​(M,ℝ)H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}}) defines a homomorphism G→S​O+​(H2​(M,ℝ))G\to SO^{+}\left(H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}})\right). It follows from
[Ver3, Thm. 2.2] and
[Ver1, Corollary 8.2]
that this homomorphism
is, in fact, an isomorphism.
(On Ho​d​d​(M)H^{odd}(M) the group generated by ρI​(t)\rho_{I}(t), for all I∈𝒞0I\in{\mathcal{C}}_{0} and t∈ℝt\in{\mathbb{R}}, acts as
the identity component of Spin⁡(H2​(M,ℝ))\operatorname{Spin}(H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}})),
with the center of the spinor group acting as −Id-\operatorname{Id}.)
Denote by G[ω]⊂GG_{[\omega]}\subset G
the subgroup generated by all Hodge rotation maps ρI​(t)\rho_{I}(t), t∈ℝt\in{\mathbb{R}}, I∈𝒞0I\in{\mathcal{C}}_{0}, [I]∈𝚃𝒞0,[ω][I]\in{\tt T}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0},[\omega]}.
Each such ρI​(t)\rho_{I}(t) fixes [ω][\omega] because [ω]∈HI1,1​(M,ℝ)[\omega]\in H_{I}^{1,1}(M;{\mathbb{R}}). Hence, [ω][\omega] is fixed by G[ω]G_{[\omega]}. Thus, G[ω]G_{[\omega]}
lies in the stabilizer of [ω][\omega] in G≅S​O+​(H2​(M,ℝ))G\cong SO^{+}\left(H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}})\right). The latter stabilizer is isomorphic to S​O+​([ω]⟂)SO^{+}([\omega]^{\perp}). Note that
the restriction of the BBF form qq to [ω]⟂⊂H2​(M,ℝ)[\omega]^{\perp}\subset H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}}) has signature (2,b2−3)(2,b_{2}-3). Now 12
yields that the maps ρI​(t)|H2​(M,ℝ)\rho_{I}(t)|_{H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}})}, t∈ℝt\in{\mathbb{R}}, [I]∈𝚃𝒞0,[ω][I]\in{\tt T}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0},[\omega]}, generate the stabilizer. In other words,
G[ω]G_{[\omega]} coincides with the stabilizer of [ω][\omega] in GG.
For the class a∈He​v​e​n​(M,ℚ)a\in H^{even}(M;{\mathbb{Q}}) given in the hypothesis of the lemma, denote by GaG_{a} the stabilizer of
aa in GG.
Now we are ready to prove the lemma.
Assume, by contradiction, that 𝚃𝒞0,[ω]⊂𝚃𝒞0,a{\tt T}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0},[\omega]}\subset{\tt T}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0},a}, meaning that option (1) above, and not (2), holds for aa.
Then aa is of type (p,p)(p,p) for all
complex structures I∈𝒞0I\in{\mathcal{C}}_{0} such that [I]∈𝚃𝒞0,[ω][I]\in{\tt T}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0},[\omega]}.
In particular, aa is fixed by ρI​(t)\rho_{I}(t) for all such II and all t∈ℝt\in{\mathbb{R}}.
Thus, G[ω]G_{[\omega]} fixes aa or, in other words,
S​O+​(2,b2−3)≅G[ω]⊂Ga⊂G≅S​O+​(3,b2−3).SO^{+}(2,b_{2}-3)\cong G_{[\omega]}\subset G_{a}\subset G\cong SO^{+}(3,b_{2}-3).
By [EV1, Lem. 9.9],
any intermediate subgroup
G[ω]⊂H⊂GG_{[\omega]}\subset H\subset G is
equal either to G[ω]G_{[\omega]} or to GG.
This implies that
GaG_{a} equals
either G[ω]G_{[\omega]} or GG.
Since [ω][\omega] is not rational, [EV1, Lem. 9.10] yields that Ga≠G[ω]G_{a}\neq G_{[\omega]}.
Since ZZ is not
absolutely trianalytic, there exists I∈𝒞0I\in{\mathcal{C}}_{0} such that a∉HI1,1​(M,ℝ)a\notin H_{I}^{1,1}(M;{\mathbb{R}}) and consequently
aa is not fixed by ρI​(t)∈G\rho_{I}(t)\in G for t≠0t\neq 0. Therefore ρI​(t)∉Ga\rho_{I}(t)\notin G_{a} and Ga≠GG_{a}\neq G.
This yields a contradiction and finishes the proof of the lemma.
Now let us finish the proof
of 12.
Let 𝒜⊂H∗​(M,ℚ)∖{0}{\mathcal{A}}\subset H^{*}(M;{\mathbb{Q}})\setminus\{0\} be the set of all a∈H∗​(M,ℚ)a\in H^{*}(M;{\mathbb{Q}}), a≠0a\neq 0, for
which 𝚃𝒞0,a∩𝚃𝒞0,[ω]{\tt T}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0},a}\cap{\tt T}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0},[\omega]} is a proper (possibly empty) complex subvariety of 𝚃𝒞0,[ω]{\tt T}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0},[\omega]} and consequently 𝚃𝒞0,a∩𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M,ω){\tt T}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0},a}\cap{\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M,\omega) is a proper complex subvariety of 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M,ω){\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M,\omega).
By 12, 𝒜{\mathcal{A}} includes, in particular, all a∈H2​(M,ℚ)a\in H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{Q}}) that are Poincaré-dual to the fundamental classes of
not absolutely trianalytic subvarieties (with respect to 𝒞0{\mathcal{C}}_{0}).
The set
S:=𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M,ω)∖⋃a∈𝒜(𝚃𝒞0,a∩𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M,ω))S:={\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M,\omega)\setminus\bigcup_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\left({\tt T}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0},a}\cap{\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M,\omega)\right)
is connected and dense in 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M,ω){\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M,\omega). For any I∈𝒞0I\in{\mathcal{C}}_{0} such that [I]∈S[I]\in S, the complex manifold (M,I)(M,I) can contain only absolutely trianalytic subvarieties. By [EV1, Lem. 7.9], the union of all such subvarieties of (M,I)(M,I) is of measure zero, meaning that II is Campana-simple and [I]∈𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M,ω)∩𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑s​(M)[I]\in{\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M,\omega)\cap{\tt Teich}^{s}(M). Hence,
S⊂𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M,ω)∩𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑s​(M)⊂𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M,ω)S\subset{\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M,\omega)\cap{\tt Teich}^{s}(M)\subset{\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M,\omega).
Since SS is connected and dense in 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M,ω){\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M,\omega), so is 𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑𝒞0​(M,ω)∩𝚃𝚎𝚒𝚌𝚑s​(M){\tt Teich}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}(M,\omega)\cap{\tt Teich}^{s}(M).
This finishes the proof of the proposition.
We are now ready to discuss the analogue of 3.4
in the IHS-hyperkähler case, as described in Section 3.5.
The proof of the claims of 3.4
in the IHS-hyperkähler case
(apart from the claim on the transitivity of the SympH​(M,ω)\mathrm{Symp}_{H}(M,\omega)-action)
is the same as in the K3 case – one just needs
to use the generalizations of the results of Section 10.2 to the IHS-hyperkähler case discussed above
(instead of the corresponding results for the K3 surfaces) and use 9
in the simply-connected case involving Campana-simple complex structures.
As for the transitivity of the action of SympH​(M,ω)\mathrm{Symp}_{H}(M,\omega), we cannot say anything in the IHS-hyperkähler case, as we have no analogue
of part B of 10.2 for IHS-hyperkähler manifolds.
Finally, let us discuss the results on tame embeddings into (M,ω)(M,\omega) for an IHS-hyperkähler-type form ω\omega.
Recall from Section 3.5 that there are two possibilities to define tame embeddings into such an (M,ω)(M,\omega). One possibility is to use Kähler-type complex structures tamed by ω\omega. Another possibility is to use instead
IHS-hyperkähler-type complex structures tamed by ω\omega.
The claim νT,ε​(M,ω1,𝕎)=νT,ε​(M,ω2,𝕎)\nu_{T,\varepsilon}(M,\omega_{1},{\mathbb{W}})=\nu_{T,\varepsilon}(M,\omega_{2},{\mathbb{W}}) of 3.4 holds for both definitions of νT,ε\nu_{T,\varepsilon}, as long as
ω1\omega_{1} and ω2\omega_{2} lie in the same connected component of the space of IHS-hyperkähler-type symplectic forms on MM.
Indeed, it is easy to see that 11 holds for both definitions of νT,ε\nu_{T,\varepsilon}.
The relevant claim about the density of the Diff+​(M)\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(M)-orbits of the form ω1\omega_{1}, ω2\omega_{2} in the space of forms of the same volume in the same connected component of the space of IHS-hyperkähler-type symplectic forms on MM follows from [EV1, Thm. 9.2].
13 Appendix
In this section we discuss various well-known facts concerning
the dependence of the Hodge decomposition on the complex structure,
the deformations of complex structures, and the Moser method, that we used in the paper and
for most of which we could not find a direct reference in the literature.
13.1 Dependence of the Hodge decomposition on the complex structure
In this section we prove two auxiliary facts about Kähler manifolds. These facts must have been known to the experts
but we have not been able to find a reference to them.
Assume MM, 2​n=dimℝM2n=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}M, is a closed manifold admitting Kähler structures – i.e., 𝐶𝐾⁡(M)≠∅\mathit{CK}(M)\neq\emptyset.
Recall that we use the C∞C^{\infty}-topology on 𝐶𝐾⁡(M)\mathit{CK}(M).
For each l=0,1,…,2​nl=0,1,\ldots,2n denote by Al​(M)A^{l}(M) the space of smooth complex-valued differential ll-forms on MM.
For each I∈𝐶𝐾⁡(M)I\in\mathit{CK}(M) let
Al(M)=⊕p+q=lAIp,q(M)A^{l}(M)=\oplus_{p+q=l}A_{I}^{p,q}(M)
be the (p,q)(p,q)-decomposition of Al​(M)A^{l}(M).
Let
ΩI1,0​(M)\Omega_{I}^{1,0}(M)
be the space of II-holomorphic 1-forms on MM, with respect to II.
By Hodge theory, dimℂH1​(M,ℂ)=2​dimℂHI1,0​(M)=2​dimℂH0​(ΩI1​(M))\dim_{\mathbb{C}}H^{1}(M;{\mathbb{C}})=2\dim_{\mathbb{C}}H_{I}^{1,0}(M)=2\dim_{\mathbb{C}}H^{0}(\Omega_{I}^{1}(M)).
Let m:=dimℂH1​(M,ℂ)/2m:=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}H^{1}(M;{\mathbb{C}})/2.
Proposition 13.1
Let J∈𝐶𝐾⁡(M)J\in\mathit{CK}(M).
Then there exists a neighborhood 𝒲{\mathcal{W}} of JJ in 𝐶𝐾⁡(M)\mathit{CK}(M) with the following property:
For each I∈𝒲I\in{\mathcal{W}} one can choose a basis θ1​(I),…,θm​(I)\theta_{1}(I),\ldots,\theta_{m}(I) of H0​(ΩI1​(M))H^{0}(\Omega_{I}^{1}(M))
continuously with respect to II.
Proof of 13.1:
Pick a JJ-invariant Riemannian metric gg on MM. Then there exists a neighborhood 𝒲{\mathcal{W}} of JJ in 𝐶𝐾⁡(M)\mathit{CK}(M) such that
for any I∈𝒲I\in{\mathcal{W}} sufficiently close to JJ
we have an II-invariant Riemannian metric (g+I∗​g)/2(g+I^{*}g)/2 that depends continuously on II and coincides with gg for I=JI=J.
Together with I∈𝒲I\in{\mathcal{W}} such an II-invariant Riemannian metric defines a Hermitian metric hIh_{I} on (M,I)(M,I) that depends continuously on II.
For each I∈𝒲I\in{\mathcal{W}} and each l=0,1,…,2​nl=0,1,\ldots,2n the Hermitian metric hIh_{I} allows to define the Laplace-Beltrami operator on Al​(M)A^{l}(M) that will be
denoted ΔIl:Al​(M)→Al​(M)\Delta_{I}^{l}:A^{l}(M)\to A^{l}(M). This is an elliptic operator. Denote the kernel of ΔIl\Delta_{I}^{l} (i.e., the space of harmonic ll-forms with respect to ΔIl\Delta_{I}^{l}) by ℋIl​(M){\mathcal{H}}_{I}^{l}(M). Then dimℂℋIl​(M)=dimℂHl​(M,ℂ)\dim_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathcal{H}}_{I}^{l}(M)=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}H^{l}(M;{\mathbb{C}}).
Also, ℋI1​(M)∩AI1,0​(M)=ΩI1,0​(M){\mathcal{H}}_{I}^{1}(M)\cap A_{I}^{1,0}(M)=\Omega_{I}^{1,0}(M).
These results follow from the classical
Hodge theory (see e.g. [Wel, Sec. IV.4, IV.5]).
Thus we have obtained that for each ll the operators ΔIl\Delta_{I}^{l} form a family of elliptic operators depending
continuously on I∈𝒲I\in{\mathcal{W}}
so that the kernels of these operators all have the same dimension. Then these kernels – i.e., the spaces ℋIl​(M){\mathcal{H}}^{l}_{I}(M), I∈𝒲I\in{\mathcal{W}} – form a continuous vector bundle over 𝒲{\mathcal{W}}. This
follows from a standard argument essentially contained in [At2, Lem. 2.1]. Namely, one passes to a Hilbert completion of
Al​(M)A^{l}(M) and extends the continuous family of elliptic operators ΔIl\Delta_{I}^{l}, I∈𝒲I\in{\mathcal{W}}, to a continuous family of Fredholm operators that, by elliptic regularity, have the same kernels, all of the same dimension. An easy functional analysis argument implies then that the kernels of these Fredholm operators form a continuous vector bundle (in fact, we only need the operators to be bounded with a closed image and these conditions are satisfied by any Fredholm operator).
Since the spaces ℋI1​(M){\mathcal{H}}_{I}^{1}(M), I∈𝒲I\in{\mathcal{W}}, form a continuous vector bundle over 𝒲{\mathcal{W}},
so do their II-invariant parts – i.e., the spaces ΩI1,0​(M)\Omega_{I}^{1,0}(M) – which are all of the same dimension mm.
This readily implies the proposition.
13.2 Deformation families of complex structures
Let (M,I)(M,I) be a complex manifold.
Assume 𝒳{\mathcal{X}}, ℬ{\mathcal{B}} are connected (not necessarily Hausdorff)
smooth manifolds. Let s0∈ℬs_{0}\in{\mathcal{B}} be a marked point.
Let 𝒳→ℬ{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{B}} be a smooth map.
If 𝒳→ℬ{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{B}} is a proper fiber bundle whose fibers are equipped with complex structures depending
smoothly on the fiber and the fiber over s0s_{0} is
(M,I)(M,I), we say that 𝒳→(ℬ,s0){\mathcal{X}}\to({\mathcal{B}},s_{0}) is a smooth deformation family, or a
smooth deformation of (M,I)(M,I).
If 𝒳{\mathcal{X}}, ℬ{\mathcal{B}} are complex manifolds and the map 𝒳→ℬ{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{B}}
is a proper holomorphic submersion, then it is a fiber bundle (by Ehresmann’s lemma, the fibration admits smooth local trivializations). The fibers of 𝒳→ℬ{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{B}} are then closed
complex submanifolds of 𝒳{\mathcal{X}} that are diffeomorphic to MM. Denote
by MsM_{s} the fiber over s∈ℬs\in{\mathcal{B}} and denote by IsI_{s} the complex
structure on MsM_{s} induced by the complex structure on 𝒳{\mathcal{X}}. Assume
that Is0=II_{s_{0}}=I. In this case we say that 𝒳→(ℬ,s0){\mathcal{X}}\to({\mathcal{B}},s_{0}) is
a complex-analytic deformation family, or a complex-analytic deformation of (M,I)(M,I).
Given a complex structure II on MM,
a smoothly trivial smooth (respectively complex-analytic) deformation family
M×U→UM\times U\to U of (M,I)(M,I) over an open set U⊂ℂmU\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{m} (respectively U⊂ℝmU\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{m}), s0=0∈Us_{0}=0\in U,
is called a
local smooth (respectively complex-analytic) deformation of II. It can be viewed as a smooth
(respectively complex analytic) family {Is}s∈U\{I_{s}\}_{s\in U}, I0=II_{0}=I.
Assume {Is}s∈U\{I_{s}\}_{s\in U}, I0=II_{0}=I, is a smooth local deformation of a Kähler-type complex structure II on MM.
The Kodaira-Spencer stability theorem [KoS2] says that for any symplectic form ω\omega on MM compatible with II there exists a neighborhood U′U^{\prime} of 00 in UU and a smooth family {ωs}s∈U′\{\omega_{s}\}_{s\in U^{\prime}}, ω0=ω\omega_{0}=\omega, of symplectic forms ωs\omega_{s} on MM
compatible with IsI_{s} for each s∈U′s\in U^{\prime}; in particular, each complex structure IsI_{s}, s∈U′s\in U^{\prime}, is of Kähler type.
It follows from the Kodaira-Spencer stability theorem (see [EV1, Thm. 5.6]) that there exists a neighborhood U′′⊂U′U^{\prime\prime}\subset U^{\prime} of 00 so that for each s∈U′′s\in U^{\prime\prime} the cohomology class [ω]Is1,1[\omega]_{I_{s}}^{1,1} is Kähler with respect
to IsI_{s}.
Proposition 13.2
The space 𝐶𝐾⁡(M)\mathit{CK}(M) is locally C∞C^{\infty}-path-connected.
Proof of 13.2:
Kuranishi’s theorem [Ku1], [Ku2], [Ku3]
implies that the Diff0​(M)\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M)-action on the space of complex structures on MM admits local slices that are finite-dimensional analytic spaces. This implies that the space of complex structures on MM is locally path-connected.
Therefore,
since Diff0​(M)\mathrm{Diff}_{0}(M) acts trivially on the cohomology and preserves 𝐶𝐾⁡(M)\mathit{CK}(M), the Kodaira-Spencer stability theorem yields that the space 𝐶𝐾⁡(M)\mathit{CK}(M) is locally C∞C^{\infty}-path-connected.
This proves the proposition.
13.3 Relative version of Moser’s method
Here we recall the following (well-known) relative version of Moser’s method [Mos].
Proposition 13.3
Let NN be a closed manifold.
I. Assume that X⊂NX\subset N is a closed domain with a piecewise-smooth boundary, such that H1​(X,ℝ)=0H^{1}(X;{\mathbb{R}})=0.
Let {Ωt}\{\Omega_{t}\}, t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1], be a smooth family of cohomologous symplectic forms on NN, so that
all the forms Ωt\Omega_{t}, t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1], coincide on a neighborhood of XX.
Then there exists an isotopy {ϕt:N→N}0≤t≤1\{\phi_{t}:N\to N\}_{0\leq t\leq 1}, ϕ0=I​d\phi_{0}=Id, such that for each t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1] the diffeomorphism ϕt\phi_{t} is identity on a neighborhood of XX
and ϕt∗​Ω0=Ωt\phi_{t}^{*}\Omega_{0}=\Omega_{t}.
II. Assume that X⊂NX\subset N is a proper submanifold. Let {Ωt}\{\Omega_{t}\}, t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1], be a smooth family of cohomologous symplectic forms on NN, so that
all the forms Ωt|X\Omega_{t}|_{X}, t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1], are non-degenerate.
Then there exists an isotopy {ϕt:N→N}0≤t≤1\{\phi_{t}:N\to N\}_{0\leq t\leq 1}, ϕ0=I​d\phi_{0}=Id, such that ϕt​(X)=X\phi_{t}(X)=X
and ϕt∗​Ω0=Ωt\phi_{t}^{*}\Omega_{0}=\Omega_{t}
for each t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1].
III. Assume that N=ℂ​Pn×ℂ​P1N={\mathbb{C}}P^{n}\times{\mathbb{C}}P^{1}, X1:=ℂ​Pn−1×ℂ​P1⊂NX_{1}:={\mathbb{C}}P^{n-1}\times{\mathbb{C}}P^{1}\subset N, X2:=ℂ​Pn×pt⊂NX_{2}:={\mathbb{C}}P^{n}\times\textrm{pt}\subset N. Let {Ωt}\{\Omega_{t}\}, t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1], be a smooth family of cohomologous symplectic forms on NN, so that
all the forms Ωt|Xi\Omega_{t}|_{X_{i}}, i=1,2i=1,2, t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1], are non-degenerate.
Then for every open neighborhood 𝒰{\mathcal{U}} of X1∩X2X_{1}\cap X_{2} there exists an isotopy {ϕt:N→N}0≤t≤1\{\phi_{t}:N\to N\}_{0\leq t\leq 1}, ϕ0=I​d\phi_{0}=Id, such that
•
ϕt​(X1∪X2)=X1∪X2\phi_{t}(X_{1}\cup X_{2})=X_{1}\cup X_{2},
•
ϕt∗​Ω0=Ωt\phi_{t}^{*}\Omega_{0}=\Omega_{t} outside 𝒰{\mathcal{U}}
for each t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1].
Proof of 13.3:
Let us prove part I of the proposition.
The proof
follows the lines of the proof of the standard, absolute, version of the same result, as long as one can find a smooth family {λt}\{\lambda_{t}\}, t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1], of 1-forms on NN so that for all t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1]
d​λt=dd​t​Ωt,d\lambda_{t}=\frac{d}{dt}\Omega_{t},
and all the forms λt\lambda_{t}, 0≤t≤10\leq t\leq 1, vanish on a neighborhood of XX.
In order to find such a family, pick first a smooth family {μt}\{\mu_{t}\}, t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1], of 1-forms on NN satisfying
d​μt=dd​t​Ωtd\mu_{t}=\frac{d}{dt}\Omega_{t}
for all t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1] (this can be done e.g. using Hodge theory – see e.g. [McDS, Thm. 3.2.4]). Note that all the forms d​μtd\mu_{t}, 0≤t≤10\leq t\leq 1, vanish on a neighborhood of XX.
Now, since H1​(X,ℝ)=0H^{1}(X;{\mathbb{R}})=0, it is easy to show that there exists a smooth family {Ft}\{F_{t}\}, t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1], of functions defined on a
neighborhood UU of XX
so that d​Ft=μtdF_{t}=\mu_{t} on UU for all t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1]. Extend these functions from possibly a smaller neighborhood U′⊂UU^{\prime}\subset U of the set XX
to a smooth family of functions on NN. By a slight abuse of notation, denote the latter family of functions on NN also by {Ft}\{F_{t}\}, t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1]. Then {λt:=μt−dFt}\{\lambda_{t}:=\mu_{t}-dF_{t}\}, t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1], is the wanted family of 1-forms on NN.
This finishes the proof of part I.
Part II is proved in [McDP, Cor. 4.1.B].
Part III is proved in [McDP, Prop. 4.1.C].
This finishes the proof
of the proposition.
13.4 Alexander’s trick
Let W⊂ℝ2​nW\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2n} be a compact domain with piecewise-smooth boundary that is starshaped with respect to the origin.
Proposition 13.4
Assume f:W→ℝ2​nf:W\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2n} is an embedding. Let ι:W→ℝ2​n\iota:W\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2n} be the inclusion map.
The following claims hold:
I. Let U⊂ℝ2​nU\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2n} be an open neighborhood WW in ℝ2​n{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}. Assume that the embedding ff is symplectic.
If ff is sufficiently C1C^{1}-close to ι\iota,
then there exists a smooth family of symplectic embeddings {ft:W→U}0≤t≤1\{f_{t}:W\to U\}_{0\leq t\leq 1} so that f0=ιf_{0}=\iota, f1=ff_{1}=f.
Moreover, all ftf_{t}, t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1], can be made arbitrarily C∞C^{\infty}-close to ι\iota, provided
ff is sufficiently C∞C^{\infty}-close to ι\iota.
II.
Assume that the embedding ff is holomorphic, f⁡(0)=0f(0)=0, and f⁡(W)⊂Wf(W)\subset W.
Then there exists a smooth family of holomorphic embeddings {ft:W→W}0≤t≤1\{f_{t}:W\to W\}_{0\leq t\leq 1} so that f0=ιf_{0}=\iota, f1=ff_{1}=f
and ft​(0)=0f_{t}(0)=0 for all t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1].
Proof of 13.4:
Let us prove part I.
If ff is sufficiently C1C^{1}-close to ι\iota,
then, composing ff, if necessary, with a family of small affine symplectomorphisms staring at I​dId, we may assume without
loss of generality, that f⁡(0)=0f(0)=0 and d​f​(0)=I​ddf(0)=Id.
Consider the following family of maps {gt}0≤t≤1\{g_{t}\}_{0\leq t\leq 1} defined on WW:
gt​(x):=g⁡(t​x)/t,if​t∈(0,1],g_{t}(x):=g(tx)/t,\ \textrm{if}\ t\in(0,1],
g0=ι.g_{0}=\iota.
If ff is sufficiently C1C^{1}-close to ι\iota,
then all the maps gtg_{t} are symplectic embeddings taking values in UU. The family {gt}0≤t≤1\{g_{t}\}_{0\leq t\leq 1} may not be smooth at t=0t=0 but for an appropriate smooth function χ:[0,1]→[0,1]\chi:[0,1]\to[0,1], χ⁡(0)=0\chi(0)=0, χ⁡(1)=1\chi(1)=1,
the family {gχ⁡(t)}0≤t≤1\{g_{\chi(t)}\}_{0\leq t\leq 1} is a smooth family of symplectic embeddings W→UW\to U connecting ff and ι\iota.
The construction clearly shows that
all ftf_{t}, t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1], can be made arbitrarily C∞C^{\infty}-close to ι\iota, provided
ff is sufficiently C∞C^{\infty}-close to ι\iota.
This finishes the proof of part I.
Let us prove part II.
For C>0C>0 consider the following family of maps {gt}0≤t≤1\{g_{t}\}_{0\leq t\leq 1} defined on WW:
gt​(z):=f⁡(t​z)/C​t,if​t∈(0,1],g_{t}(z):=f(tz)/Ct,\ \textrm{if}\ t\in(0,1],
g0:=d​f​(0)/C.g_{0}:=df(0)/C.
For a sufficiently large C>0C>0 we have Im​gt⊂W{\rm{Im}\,}g_{t}\subset W for all t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1].
The family {gt}0≤t≤1\{g_{t}\}_{0\leq t\leq 1} may not be smooth at t=0t=0 but for an appropriate smooth function χ:[0,1]→[0,1]\chi:[0,1]\to[0,1], χ⁡(0)=0\chi(0)=0, χ⁡(1)=1\chi(1)=1,
the family {gχ⁡(t)}0≤t≤1\{g_{\chi(t)}\}_{0\leq t\leq 1} is a smooth family of holomorphic embeddings (W,0)→(W,0)(W,0)\to(W,0) connecting g0g_{0} and g1=f/Cg_{1}=f/C.
If C>0C>0 is sufficiently large, the linear holomorphic embedding g0g_{0} can be easily connected by a smooth family of holomorphic embeddings (W,0)→(W,0)(W,0)\to(W,0) to I​d/CId/C and the latter can be connected to I​dId.
Corollary 13.5
Let M2​nM^{2n} be a manifold and ω\omega a Kähler-type symplectic form on MM.
Let f:W→(M,ω)f:W\to(M,\omega) be a Kähler-type/tame/ε\varepsilon-tame embedding (in the latter case we assume that MM is closed).
Then any symplectic embedding f′:W→(M,ω)f^{\prime}:W\to(M,\omega) sufficiently C1C^{1}-close to ff is of Kähler-type/tame/ε\varepsilon-tame,
and there exists ϕ∈Symp0​(M)\phi\in\mathrm{Symp}_{0}(M) such that ϕ∘f=f′\phi\circ f=f^{\prime}. The symplectomorphism ϕ\phi can be made arbitrarily C∞C^{\infty}-close to I​dId provided f′f^{\prime} is sufficiently C∞C^{\infty}-close to ff.
Proof of 13.4:
Assume ff extends to an II-holomorphic symplectic embedding f:U→(M,ω)f:U\to(M,\omega), where UU is a neighborhood of WW in ℝ2​n{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}
and II is a complex structure on MM with the relevant properties as needed for a Kähler-type/tame/ε\varepsilon-tame embedding.
Let f′:W→(M,ω)f^{\prime}:W\to(M,\omega) be a symplectic embedding (extending to a symplectic embedding of an open neighborhood of WW). If f′f^{\prime} is sufficiently C1C^{1}-close to ff, then f′​(W)⊂f​(U)f^{\prime}(W)\subset f(U) and
f−1∘f′:W→Uf^{-1}\circ f^{\prime}:W\to U is a symplectic embedding that can be made arbitrarily C1C^{1}-close to the identity. By part I of 13.4, it can be connected to the inclusion ι:W→U\iota:W\to U by a smooth family of symplectic
embeddings W→UW\to U arbitrarily C∞C^{\infty}-close to the identity, provided f′f^{\prime} is sufficiently C∞C^{\infty}-close to ff. Composing these embeddings with ff, we get a smooth family of symplectic embeddings ft:W→(M,ω)f_{t}:W\to(M,\omega), 0≤t≤10\leq t\leq 1,
connecting ff and f′f^{\prime}. These embeddings can be made arbitrarily C∞C^{\infty}-close to ff, provided f′f^{\prime} is sufficiently C∞C^{\infty}-close to ff.
By a standard result concerning extension of symplectic isotopies (proved as [McDS, Thm. 3.3.1]),
there exists ϕ∈Symp0​(M,ω)\phi\in\mathrm{Symp}_{0}(M,\omega) such that ϕ∘f=f′\phi\circ f=f^{\prime}.
The symplectomorphism ϕ\phi can be chosen to be arbitrarily C∞C^{\infty}-close to the identity, if the symplectic
embeddings ftf_{t}, 0≤t≤10\leq t\leq 1, are sufficiently C∞C^{\infty}-close to ff, which is true if f′f^{\prime} is sufficiently C∞C^{\infty}-close to ff.
The symplectic embedding f′f^{\prime} is of Kähler-type/tame/ε\varepsilon-tame because it is holomorphic with respect to the
complex structure ϕ∗​I\phi_{*}I on MM satisfying the relevant properties.
This finishes the proof of the corollary.

Instructions for reporting errors
We are continuing to improve HTML versions of papers, and your feedback helps enhance accessibility and mobile
support. To report errors in the HTML that will help us improve conversion and rendering, choose any of the
methods listed below:
Click the "Report Issue" (
) button, located in the page header.
Tip: You can select the relevant text first, to include it in your report.
Our team has already identified the following issues. We appreciate your time reviewing and reporting rendering errors we
may not have found yet. Your efforts will help us improve the HTML versions for all readers, because disability
should not be a barrier to accessing research. Thank you for your continued support in championing open access for
all.
Have a free development cycle? Help support accessibility at arXiv! Our collaborators at LaTeXML maintain a list of packages that need conversion, and welcome developer contributions.
We gratefully acknowledge support from
our major funders,
member institutions, ,
and all contributors.
